on behalf of the gue/ngl group the bluefin tuna is perhaps the most famous endangered fish species we know of, and that's a dubious honor. in 50 years, the number of bluefin tuna has declined by almost 90%. this has brought the species to the brink of extinction, and the reason for this has been the severe overfishing that has taken place for decades. overfishing, which was largely due to the subsidized overcapacity of our european fishing fleet. in 2006, measures were taken to reduce overfishing and protect tuna. but while it does seem to be working somewhat, there is no reason for euphoria. because if the tuna population goes down to almost zero and then that population doubles again, you've got twice as much as almost zero. the bluefin tuna is still listed on the red list of endangered species due to human activity, and many organizations warn that it is too early to fish for tuna again. and that is precisely what the rapporteur and the european commission want: more fish! we are not even talking about the by-catch of other species, all of which, like the tuna, are facing a horrible death. the party for the animals does not agree with this, because those who decide to fish more endangered species are committing a predation on our planet and thus on future generations. i also believe that agricultural subsidies and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
we are talking today about the tuna recovery plan and its transposition into european rules. it looks good. i congratulate the rapporteur on the very clear way in which he has dealt with this. i think we will be able to reach agreement with the commission in a number of trialogue meetings. that is a hopeful development, but on the other hand i am very concerned about the other fish species in the mediterranean. almost all of these species are severely overfished in the mediterranean. for example, the hedgehog is estimated to be caught ten times as much as is responsible. the younger the hedgehog is, the less it reproduces. this phenomenon occurs in several species of fish. i therefore ask commissioner vella: the commission is also proposing to set up a european fisheries management organisation, which will be responsible for the management of the fisheries resources of the mediterranean. after all, this sea is being ruined in that way and so are the fish in this sea.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. thank you for your contribution, commissioner. first of all, i would like to emphasize - and we must not lose sight of this - the importance of this subject to the european public. people want clean air and people are really sick of being conned by the auto industry. secondly: this is the commission that has better legislation as its top priority. if this decision is an example of anything, it's not better legislation. the present decision is not based on any impact assessment and no member state dares to take political responsibility for this decision. i think it is significant that the council is completely absent again. the member states blame brussels for this decision, but it is the member states that we must blame for the fact that clean air is not yet a reality for our citizens. third, i'm getting back on that clean air. it is a fundamental human right to have clean air. but i would ask the commissioner how the drivers of our european cities can generate clean air for their citizens if this way dirty cars can continue to drive around in the cities. how is that possible, commissioner? a city like london had already crossed the nitrogen oxide limit for the rest of the year on january 7. on january 7th, across the border for the whole of 2016! how can a city like london with these kinds of decisions ever give its citizens clean air? and finally, in the netherlands, a word of the year for 2015 was chosen: "cheating software". that was the word coined to describe the practices of the auto industry. the way in which the member states in particular think they can operate politically will give us a much worse word of the year next year. and that word will be: "political fraud".
on behalf of the verts/ale group.– madam president, i would like to thank the commissioner for explaining the proposal once more. i am a bit disappointed by that, commissioner, because i expected to hear what potential change you can see in the proposal and what i get from your speech is that no change is possible any more. what was agreed by the member states, who indeed are not present here, is that all vehicles can emit up to 168 mg/km until 2019 – you mentioned only the date for new models, but if we discuss all vehicles then it is 2019. you know that very well. you are also aware of the 1.5 conformity factor, which means changing our standard from 80 to 120 and that only starting in 2021, although already in 2007 we as co-legislators decided that it should go to 80 in 2014. that is what we did in co-legislation. what they are doing now in a technical meeting is to say that this value can move to 120 until 2021. even if you are on the side of the car industry, you cannot accept that a technical committee is adjusting what we decided as co-legislators. it is important to realise that already we can improve much more. today in the flemish news, mr belet, it was mentioned that opel, for one car and with a simple software correction, went down from 750 to 200 in just one day, and you are agreeing now that they have time until 2021 to water down a proposal that was decided in 2007. that is what you are going to agree upon. commissioner, the social democrats asked a very clear question: will there be a new proposal on the conformity factors – yes or no? we demand a clear answer tonight.

on behalf of the s&d group, there is sand in the wheels of the european economy. investment is lagging behind and growth is fragile. banks and especially smes have a hard time finding each other. capital markets are underdeveloped. the capital markets union can therefore be an opportunity to stimulate financial innovation, to remove barriers between member states and to use capital efficiently. the commission has looked closely at the united states. it has often been said that capital markets provide 80 percent of the credit there and only 20 percent here. but to speak to david bowie: "this is not america". the 80/20 ratio suggests that capital markets need to be better developed, but also that bank financing needs to be better developed. it's important to fix benches. it is good to look again at the united states. there has been a separation between savings and business banking. according to oecd research, the classification of banks is better regulated there. the report by mr burkhard balz - my compliments to the rapporteur - is based on the premise that the financial sector must serve the real economy. regulation is a means to that end. it's not an end in itself, it's not an illness in itself, it's a means. it is this balanced approach which enables the s&d group to endorse this report. the objective of regulation must always be to make the financial sector serviceable. it is also good to periodically check the serviceability of the financial sector. the capital markets union can only work in the end if the european context is recognised. that means boosting innovation and fixing banks. that's how we get the sand out of the machine and get on the road to new growth and jobs.

i also extend a warm welcome to mr rutte. the dutch presidency is unfortunately the presidency of crises. we know prime minister rutte as someone who is wary of big things and wants to tackle the problems in a practical way. i'm still asking for a vision. the crises do not fall from the sky, but have a similar cause. the european union has been a toy for big business in recent years, not a europe of and for the citizens. this was made very clear when the citizens in the netherlands and france said no to the european constitution in 2005, but that constitution was pushed through the treaty of lisbon. but there 's more . the british are being pushed into an ever more federal europe, although they made it very clear from the moment of accession that they did not want that. and now there's a threat of brexit. when internal border controls were abolished, no one said that there would be an asylum policy at european level. i deplore the attitude of those eastern european member states which do not want to take in asylum seekers. but i do understand that people feel attacked there. add to this the dictates to greece, portugal and ireland, while the imbalances in the eurozone simply persist. this was not mentioned when the euro was introduced. the brussels bubble knew full well that the euro would lead to a huge transfer of powers in the financial and economic sphere. mr rutte, you have a great opportunity now to restore the citizens' confidence in europe. and not through a nice app, as minister koenders proposed this week, but by finally taking the dutch voter seriously when a referendum on the treaty with ukraine is held in the netherlands on 6 april. tell the dutch voters that you will ensure that the treaty with ukraine does not enter into force if there is sufficient turnout and a majority says no. a little vision can't hurt.
welcome to the prime minister rutte. we hope for a successful dutch presidency. i want to start with a quote: "we are committed to strengthening economic growth. we must create jobs in europe and tackle backwardness". you're probably thinking, well, that's a bit of our presidency. that was balkenende, july 1, 2004. in fact , every presidency since the last one has put jobs and growth back at the center . we must deliver that, and then all will be well with the confidence in europe. perhaps we should consider that this growing euroscepticism and rejection of europe is due to something else. it's because of a lack of credibility. i am not saying that the european parliament should be the only one to have a say in the matter, but i am saying that the european parliament should be the only one to have a say in the matter. in that sense, it is good to hear that you think the european parliament is so important, because i have not heard you say that very often in the hague. let's move on to the credibility issue. europe has a credibility problem in greece. the commission is also proposing to reduce the number of jobs created by the new technologies by a factor of two. in poland we have a credibility problem. by talking about values now and talking about a union of values, but in the meantime continuing with nord stream, including the netherlands. in france, by leaving test balloons about mini-schengen where france doesn't even belong. what credibility is that? in portugal, by talking about closing budgets in portugal, but meanwhile luring the 20 largest companies to amsterdam to get their revenue there without any economic activity. in our neighbouring countries, by talking about hosting in the region, but cutting back on development cooperation and transferring zero euros to the un food programme, which is what was promised. or in the netherlands by constantly talking about giving up sovereignty, which we don't want when it comes to the eu, but meanwhile wanting to give away tons of sovereignty when it comes to ttip. that's the credibility! it is time for the netherlands to show leadership to become truly credible. that means redirecting, over that 50, transferring the money that we promised, stopping nord stream 2, really going to a social europe where we're tackling our own trade surplus and not just looking at the deficits in the south, but also our surplus in the north, and finally in the ukraine debate, really campaigning. not only because the association agreement is a trade agreement, but also because it is important for ukraine that it is not a political toy between russia and europe. that is a credible europe. that is the kind of leadership we need from the netherlands during this presidency. on behalf of the s&d group.– madam president, the situation in the southeast of turkey is very worrying. since the fighting between the pkk and the state security forces restarted this summer, the situation on the ground is deteriorating still further. imagine living in the silopi, cizre or sur districts. most people left their houses in a hurry when an open-ended, round-the-clock curfew was declared in early december. at that time, more than 200 000 people had to flee. heavy fighting is going on day and night between the security forces and the pkk youth militias who have built barricades and dug trenches in the middle of these neighbourhoods. the anti-terrorism operations heavily affect the civilian population: not being able to leave your house for more than 40 days; so-called esedullah forces who intimidate the local population; and an increasing number of civilian deaths, including of children and elderly people. how is it possible that a 16-year-old boy can bleed to death on the streets without receiving any medical aid? the attacks by the pkk last week killing police officers and their family members can in no way be justified, but why are there crackdowns on peaceful protesters? in this regard, i am also gravely concerned about the intimidation and prosecution of more than 1 000 academics who signed a petition calling for peace. how can reasonable people calling for the violence to stop be labelled traitors and promoters of terrorism? the right of turkey to fight against terrorism is not questioned here today. the general feeling, however, is that the operations are a collective punishment rather than aiming at restoring public order. it is time that the eu made a strong and determined call for a ceasefire and for the peace talks to be restarted. the curfews must be lifted and those people who can be part of a peace campaign must be empowered and supported.

association agreements help the eu to make all kinds of agreements with countries, conditions for trade, rules on human rights and the rule of law. although ukraine is an important partner for the eu, it is certainly not the only country with which we have such agreements, from israel to chile, from egypt to south africa. in the netherlands, particular attention is being paid to the agreement with ukraine because a referendum is due to be held on 6 april on whether the netherlands' signature, with the support of the government and the house of representatives, should remain under these agreements. some people fear that the agreement will quietly lead to ukraine's accession. but the opposite is true. it is precisely when a country is not eligible for accession that an association agreement is a very useful and good way of establishing agreements. it is good that the eu is helping ukraine to tackle corruption and reform its economy and society. a stable and prosperous ukraine is good for the people of the country itself, but also good for europeans. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' to cover all the activities of the public authorities.
these treaties, which the european union has signed with georgia, ukraine and moldova, are the most ambitious of their kind and these association agreements should ensure greater stability, democracy and prosperity throughout the european continent, which is also in our interest. many difficult reforms have already been carried out in these countries over the past year. but if you look, you'll see that there are still three persistent problems. one of them is corruption. the fight against corruption must be the basis for all progress. moldova and ukraine in particular are still facing this problem. there is still a real shift to be made in the legal sector and unfortunately, the winner takes all mentality remains prevalent in these three countries. another thing they have in common is the threat and aggression of their great neighbour russia. it is good that the eu is concluding these ambitious treaties with these three countries, which are not about membership but about intensive cooperation. i hope that on 6 april the majority of the dutch people will vote in favour of the association agreement with ukraine.

author.– mr president, the hydrogen bomb test that north korea performed in early january has been condemned by the council and the un security council. we, too, strongly condemn the test, which is a direct threat to stability and peace on the korean peninsula. further provocations should be avoided at all costs by abandoning nuclear and ballistic missile programmes, and an end should be sought to the provocations through the six-party talks, which should be resumed. another key point of concern to us is that the human rights situation and the humanitarian situation are very, very dire. people face systematic repression, and the commission of inquiry has established that crimes against humanity were perpetrated. we need more concrete international action to end the impunity and to bring the perpetrators to justice. we should also consider more targeted sanctions. it is essential for the international community to convince the chinese government to use its influence, both economically and politically, to push north korea to de-escalate. it should also make an effort to accept refugees from north korea.

i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr marias, and my fellow shadow rapporteurs for their pleasant cooperation on this report. in this report, parliament expresses serious concern about the effects of quantitative easing, which puts 60 billion euro of financial growth hormones into the economy every month. mr draghi, you have said it many times and you have repeated it today: the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. economic reforms and sound fiscal policies are also needed. you mentioned it as the third point in your speech today. do you agree that the situation in these areas is not going in the right direction? in many countries, the labour market is still closed. it seems that in several places the mental switch from a job for life to a life of jobs still needs to be made. the adjustment of the retirement age is not yet in order everywhere and there are mainly large countries which have the highest debt and the highest deficits, combined with the lowest growth and also the lowest inflation. these countries are the ones who are trying the hardest to get out of the budget agreements. not a good development! in view of all this, i wonder whether it is wise in these circumstances to continue and expand quantitative enlargement. this concern is reinforced by the strong negative side effects of this type of monetary stimulus on the stability of pension funds and financial markets in europe. if we don't take the extremely low oil prices into account, inflation is not that dramatically low. i therefore wonder whether it is necessary to add another shovel of quantitative enlargement. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the budget for the financial year 2000 and whether he is prepared to accept the amendments which have been tabled by the committee on budgets.
"blue card" question. what is it? mr rosati, you have made a most astonishing speech. you hold mr draghi's hand over his head and you say: the ecb has done its job well. that is very surprising to me, because the ecb has not done its job well at all. the ecb is to ensure price stability and inflation at a level of around 2%. inflation has not been at that level since 2013. how can you say that mr draghi has done his job well? it's not right!
as far as i am concerned, this is the last annual report by president mario draghi. mario needs to move. he has not achieved the ecb's objective of bringing inflation down to 2%. not since 2013. in fact, we've been at near-zero inflation for months. the deflationary spell is knocking on the door and the price of oil continues to fall, with both a declining demand and an increasing supply. so we're going to be in deflation, too. but what do we see? the ecb continues to buy up debt securities, hoping to boost inflation. you can't do that, even if you double your program. because what happens? debt continues to rise, necessary reforms are not being carried out. stop this program! you've lost your credibility. i'm asking you to draw your conclusions and step aside. mario needs to move.

on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, the european union is the world leader when it comes to the export of services. we expect the sector to become even more important. our service providers are competitive, as the eu itself is already very open and foreign companies are treated equally on our market. there is therefore a lot to be gained by seeing more countries reciprocally opening up their markets to eu service providers and having a level playing field across a larger group of countries. in fact, we would ultimately like tisa to become a multilateral agreement. similarly, services are being developed in new areas, such as the digital economy, which will require the rules to be updated. everyone with internet access could in principle offer a service in a third country. the trade in services agreement should contribute to clear rules to ensure that principles such as fair competition, but also fundamental rights, are respected. in today’s report the parliament calls on the commission to aim for an ambitious agreement, especially in sectors such as e-commerce, telecommunication, financial services, procurement and transport. i think – and this is important to underline amidst all the concerns that also have to be addressed today – that we have every reason to be self-confident. this parliament does confirm existing legislation and standards are not lowered and free trade should follow rules. therefore, public services and audiovisual services are also confirmed to be excluded in our report, and tisa cannot force national governments to break open blue-card answer.– what we have put in this resolution that deals with the trade-in-services negotiations that are ongoing, is that we would like to see all the other partners to this agreement ultimately respect labour rights as enshrined under the ilo. but i agree with my colleague from the uk, jude kirton-darling, when she says that the practice of respecting standards is more important than seeking to underline demands on paper which may not be realistic. so, indeed, strengthening the rules-based system in trade, including trade in services, is there to raise the bar when it comes to standards, including labour rights.
tisa, the trade and services agreement, is the ugly little brother of ttip and ceta. and those treaties didn't win any beauty awards either. tisa is another example of how the european union is at the mercy of multinationals. this time it's the really good friends of services. there is nothing wrong with stimulating the economy, but only if it is at the expense of labour rights, democratic control and the economy of other countries. that's what tisa is going to do. tisa forces countries to open their markets, it puts public services on the back burner, and it makes it impossible to back down as insight advances. i would urge members to vote for amendment no 20 so that we call on the commission to withdraw from the negotiations. if you really want to protect public services, tisa is the wrong way to go. i would also like to call for the abolition of the so-called ratchet clause, amendment no 29, and for the protection of small and mediumsized enterprises, amendment no 49. and i would ask you to also submit the agreement to the national parliaments, as described in amendment no 53. and especially for mrs. kirton-darling: amendment no 36 to the basic ilo treaties as a binding condition.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, dumping products on the eu market under the market price is not allowed and should be met with appropriate measures. the question is how the eu can best address the inevitable expiry of the protocol on china’s accession to the wto that was signed 15 years ago. the question before us is not whether we believe china has a market economy where prices are set by supply and demand: it does not. the question before us is how we should address illegal trade practices by a country that is also an important trading partner. my group believes we must address this complex and technical matter with a cool head. some people have already taken very emotional positions; others wish to use this moment to enforce their wish-list of protectionist measures. but they seem to forget that there are both importers and exporters in europe, and that the economic angle is not the only one to consider. i believe we need a thorough impact assessment and a legal analysis, and that we must strengthen the rules-based system of trade. particularly in relation to china, it is key that we too, on the eu side, practise what we preach and that our solutions should be wto- proof. it does mean that we need effective trade defence instruments to address subsidies, dumping and other illegal practices by china or any other country for that matter. i believe that we must address this issue proactively, and i do not believe in the proposals by those who think we should best leave it up to wto arbitration to decide.
the granting of market economy status to china has, as has already been said, major consequences. after all, there are countless jobs at stake. there should be no automatic allocation and the commission is well advised to study the approach of the united states, canada and australia in this respect and to use elements of this for its own approach. the gradual granting of market economy status to certain industries that meet the conditions could be an option. in addition, china could make concessions in a package deal. i am thinking of self-restrictions on exports and a better playing field for european companies in exchange for market economy status. the eu must also fully honour china's wto accession pledge. commissioner, please do not let our companies and workers down! i wish you every success in this complex matter.

madam president, after the previous european biodiversity strategy completely failed, i became rapporteur in 2011 on biodiversity, and we agreed here that this time it would be different. in this plenary hall we all emphasised the enormous importance of biodiversity for mankind. from the far left all the way to the far right, this time would be different, and yes, the reform of the fisheries policy was successful and made it much more sustainable. but what about the common agricultural policy and cohesion policy? there we failed. we did not make it more sustainable. so here we are in 2016, half way, and one conclusion stands: the eu fails seriously in achieving its biodiversity strategy. simply muddling through is no solution any more. so, commissioner – and the absent council – do not let 500 million europeans down, and please take serious steps on biodiversity.
the world faces a huge challenge to produce food while reducing the pressure on our ecosystem. and the only way to do that is to work smartly with nature, which is something that farmers and gardeners have a natural ability to do. it is precisely through new technologies and applications that we are producing more and more food, while at the same time reducing our environmental impact and improving the health of our animals. i therefore believe that the greatest threat to our biodiversity is not agriculture and horticulture. the greatest danger is incomprehension and lack of knowledge. there's no one solution to conserving biodiversity, but by doing modern farming, we reduce the pressure on our natural resources and we need less land per kilogram of food. a rigid interpretation of european rules, such as in the netherlands around natura 2000, causes nature conservation to be locked in. only by working together, using common sense and engaging all stakeholders will you create the best solutions to meet biodiversity goals.
member of the commission.– madam president, on behalf of the commission, i would like to thank parliament for the very open and very strong commitment and support for the protection of our natural capital. i believe that, once adopted, this timely solution – together with the council conclusions delivered last december – can very much help steer the eu towards achieving its biodiversity targets. the commission’s mid-term review, the council conclusions and parliament’s report all send a consistent message about the critical importance of the effective integration of biodiversity objectives with, and into, all the other sectors. the european parliament has a very important and crucial role to play in reaching eu biodiversity targets, both in raising awareness of the value of biodiversity by actually integrating biodiversity objectives into other eu policies and by actually transforming the calls for this resolution into concrete decisions on eu policies and funding. i could not agree more with the comments that we need a broader and full support of all stakeholders. this is very important. any legislation without enough support is probably the hardest to implement. to step up their implementation. ms boylan asked me whether i can declare my position. i have to say that any position on this will not be the prerogative of only me, commissioner vella, but will be a collegial decision of the commission based on the evidence gathered in the fitness check exercise. my objective is not to undermine existing legislation but to make sure we have all the necessary tools in place to achieve the 2020 target. i took note of what ms auken said. we have worked together in the past and i truly know how genuine she is. i can assure her that all members of the college, including first vice-president timmermans, are as much in favour of nature and biodiversity as are all of us. to the many comments from the honourable members who pointed out that we have to involve farmers if biodiversity targets are to be achieved: yes, i agree. we have been talking about implementation, but let us not forget that the state of the environment report, the state of the nature report, and the mid-term review itself, identify and point to a number of causes, not simply implementation. one of the causes which all the reports have identified is not agriculture but some of the bad practices in agriculture. in the implementation of the current cap, the emphasis in the next years should be on ensuring that the potential of biodiversity support measures is used fully at the national and local levels. given their role in managing landscapes, farmland and nature, farmers should be supported and rewarded for protecting and restoring biodiversity, and for the services they deliver to the public. achieving the eu biodiversity targets is, after all, as important for society at large as it is for the farming sector itself. i think, as ms köstinger said, farmers have to be act on the causes of insufficient progress, we should keep sight of what has been achieved. we have put in place important policy frameworks to build on. we have accumulated a true wealth of positive experiences to learn from and an increasing pool of knowledge to underpin decision-making. we have a growing network of stakeholders and citizens who are very committed to protecting and enhancing biodiversity. we need to capitalise on this to put the eu back on track to reaching our biodiversity targets. my last thought is that the directives that we have been discussing are not an end in themselves. they are a means to an end, the end being achieving our 2020 targets. whether we review our directives or not, i believe that we have to work harder. we have to work closely together to achieve this end.
in writing. as much as a quarter of all european animal species are threatened with extinction and many ecosystems are at risk. despite the european nature policy, the recovery of nature is totally inadequate. the protection of nature must be strengthened. however, natural recovery can only be successful if the causes of the loss are removed. one of the greatest threats to biodiversity is intensive livestock farming. the dutch pig sector alone is already causing $34 million in damage from wildlife loss. the huge nitrogen emissions pollute surface water, air and soil, reducing biodiversity in the vicinity of livestock factories. but the destructiveness of intensive livestock farming extends to the rain forests of latin america, which must give way to giant fields of maize and soybeans. and all to feed the billions of animals in europe's bio-industry. a shift to sustainable agriculture and livestock farming is necessary to halt the loss of valuable nature and the extinction of thousands of animal and plant species. therefore, brussels' attempts to undermine existing nature conservation laws should be stopped immediately. the european birds and habitats directives are important and should not be relaxed, but rather strengthened and better enforced to stop the dramatic loss of biodiversity in europe.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection. it's nice that the platform against black labor is coming. such exploitation is not acceptable in the european union. the commissioner said that many small selfemployed people complained about unfair competition from companies employing black workers. even the functioning of the internal market as a whole is being undermined by black-marketing. i am glad to see that many of the proposals from the committee on the internal market and consumer protection have been adopted. it is a pity that parliament is not automatically represented on the platform. i therefore hope that the commission will report to parliament annually on the platform, even though it is only formally obliged to do so regularly. i would have liked to see the platform also promote operational cooperation, but there is no explicit provision for this. i also think it is a bit silly that member states should appoint a representative but not be obliged to participate in all the activities of the platform. nevertheless, on behalf of the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, i would like to congratulate mr pirinski on the outcome of the trialogue.
in these times of a choice between no work, undeclared work or flex work, many people are between a rock and a hard place and it is all the more important that abuses such as undeclared work and pseudo-independence are brought to light and that workers can simply exercise their existing formal rights. i would therefore like to thank mr pirinski and his team for the great effort they have made to create this platform to combat undeclared work. it is a first step in the right direction in the fight against the abuse of workers, against the abuse of social security and the undermining of a level playing field between companies. it is also hoped that this platform will increase understanding and improve cooperation between member states. those are all positive points. it is also positive that the participation of social partners as members of this platform will help to identify problems in specific sectors in specific countries. it is therefore important that all member states commit themselves to this approach. in belgium, for example, this approach has already resulted in secretary of state tommelein receiving very large sums of additional social contributions. the platform is therefore part of the puzzle and i ask the commissioner: where's the labour market integration package? because in addition to this platform and the enforcement directive, we also need proposals to close the gaps in the legislation.
there is a package here to tackle tax avoidance and aggressive tax planning and we strongly support that objective, because we agree that everyone should pay their fair share of taxes, citizens, smes, but certainly also multinationals, who should not be left out of the loop. so it looks like the right things are being done here, but that doesn't get you there. because you don't just have to do the right things, you have to do the right things right. i therefore regard it as a missed opportunity that no proper impact assessment has been carried out. i regret that the international context in which we must operate to remain competitive has not been taken into account. and that there are still some vagueness, some broad definitions, for example of the anti-abuse provision, which can still lead to ambiguity and differences of interpretation, which will lead to a court case. and we'll create jobs, but only for lawyers. and that's not what we're talking about. so we are going to look at these proposals very carefully and where necessary we will try to improve them. author.– madam president, if you were to ask someone to make a list of top priorities of what is important in his or her life, there is a big chance that one’s health tops the list, and that is what this debate is about. it is about health, and that is why it is so important. the issue we are discussing here is very clear and straightforward. we have a legal text, called the regulation, and in this legal text there is a legally-binding mandate for the european commission to adopt scientific criteria for endocrine disruptors by december 2013. it is not written in chinese. we have many official languages in the eu, but chinese is not one of them. so it is very clear and straightforward: by december 2013, the european commission had to adopt scientific criteria for endocrine disruptors. i must say i can be extremely flexible if there are technical reasons for not being on time, but i am very inflexible when there are political reasons behind not meeting a deadline, and of course that is the case here. the court ruling is extremely clear: the commission should have adopted the scientific criteria in december 2013. to quote from the court ruling: ‘there is no provision of the regulation which requires an impact assessment’. so i am not against an impact assessment; i think it is a very valuable tool in this case too. but before starting an impact assessment, the commission should have adopted the scientific criteria. i understood that already in july 2013 they were ready, there somewhere on a big dust pile somewhere in the berlaymont building. i would say: get rid of the dust and table them as soon as possible – that is, within a few weeks.
you started well by saying that you would follow the law and the judgment of the court of justice. but then you became very unclear again about what is going on with the impact assessment. i'll make it very clear once again: the court of justice has said that only scientific criteria should apply. they were finished in 2013, so they can be published now. why do you need until the summer of 2016? is that because you want to wait for the impact assessment? if so, you are directly contradicting the judgment of the court of justice, which said that the impact assessment should not be used to determine the scientific criteria. if you do, you will be going against the judgment of the court of justice and that is a disgrace to the commission.
member of the commission.– madam president, first of all i would like to express my deepest sympathies to the victims of the tragic incident that occurred recently during a clinical trial in france and to their families. i fully trust that the french authorities have taken all the necessary measures to address this situation and are doing their utmost to clarify exactly what happened. i am personally in close contact with the french minister of health to monitor the situation and any new developments related to the investigations. it is important that we all wait for the outcome of this investigation before drawing any conclusions. clinical trials are essential for the development of new medicines and to improve, compare and refine treatments using already—authorised medicines. clinical trials are often the last hope for patients suffering from rare or serious conditions. in areas such as paediatric oncology, for example, there are clearly established links between participation in clinical trials and the life expectancy of patients. it should not be forgotten that a certain risk is associated with any clinical trial. clinical research is a sector in which europe is a world leader. offering europeans patients the best, most innovative treatments under the highest standards of patient protection remains the main objective of regulation in this area. we can all agree that transparency is an essential component of clinical research. it should be possible to know which clinical trials are ongoing, where, by whom, what their purpose is and what are the recruitment criteria. some of this information is already available via the existing eu clinical trials register managed by the european medicines agency, with which i am in constant contact. furthermore, once a trial has been completed, its outcome – whether positive or negative – should be made publicly available. additionally, the european medicines agency (ema) has pro-actively engaged in a policy to release clinical study reports submitted to the agency as part of the application for marketing authorisation of human medicines before this summer. this will make europe a true frontrunner as regards the release of data concerning clinical trials. this transparency initiative creates a bridge from now until when the new clinical trial regulations become applicable. this will trigger real improvement as regards transparency. all information on clinical trials authorised by member states will be stored in a new european union database managed by ema, with some exceptions which were defined during the legislative process. the whole content of the european union database will be publicly available. furthermore, in cases of overriding public interest, all information concerning specific trials will immediately be made publicly available. the european union database is being developed by the european medicines agency. this it tool is crucial for the correct functioning of the new regulation. it will have to handle the flow of information concerning the thousands of clinical trials ongoing every day in europe. it certainly constitutes a complex and challenging it project. while understanding all the difficult technical challenges, i regret to say that this project is running behind schedule. all concerned parties – ema, member states and the commission – will have to step up efforts to make the eu database operational as soon as possible, in order to enable the full implementation of the new regulation. to date, there are no elements indicating that the application of the new regulation would have prevented this incident in france. i am sure that ema has, and will deploy, the necessary technical resources. the commission will monitor this closely. i also call on all member states, because cooperation between ema and the member states is sometimes very difficult. i call on them to scale up their constructive collaboration on this project. madam president, honourable members, let me conclude by underlining once again the importance of increased transparency of clinical trials in europe. this will be achieved under the new regulation on clinical trials. i appeal again to all interested parties to work constructively together in order for the regulation to become applicable as early as possible.
before a medicinal product is placed on the market and even before clinical trials take place, it is routinely tested on animals. and for each drug, thousands of animals are used, including rats, rabbits and dogs. the use of ape people is already banned in europe. animal testing is unethical, it causes a lot of pain and stress to the animals, but above all it is completely unreliable. the predictive value of animal models is extremely low, and there is no guarantee that drugs are safe because they have been tested on animals. the use of these unreliable tests puts human lives at risk, as was also shown in france last month. according to the french minister, this drug had been extensively tested on animals, even on chimpanzees. so i have two questions for the commissioner: is it true that these drugs have been tested on chimpanzees even though it's banned in europe? are you going to encourage the development of reliable models based on the human body? we can then stop the senseless animal experiments and at the same time prevent further casualties.

we are talking today about a possible brexit, but of course it is really about the dismantling of the eu, and that dismantling has already begun. i can imagine that for many of you, that's very difficult to accept. you've believed that for years, decades. that's why i'm helping you with reality rehab. my tip is: when it's quiet at home, stand in front of the mirror and say to yourself, "schengen is dead", slowly but clearly: "schengen is dead". do that ten times a day, that's my recipe. it's the brain adjusting to the new reality because things have changed. the shock will not be so great if schengen is abolished in a few weeks or months.

i am glad that parliament has now finally received the impact report on the agreement with philip morris. according to the report, pmi's cigarette intercepts have decreased by 85% since 2004. however, as the researchers themselves have pointed out, this is not saying much. since 2004, an increasing number of unbranded cigarettes, cheap whites, have been smuggled into the eu. the researchers therefore had to conclude - and i think this is very important - that the collaboration with pmi has not produced any demonstrable benefit for public health in europe. the small amount of fines that pmi had to pay, amounting to over eur 68 million, remains remarkable. the researchers note that the interceptions are of ever smaller quantities of cigarettes. they are thus kept under the radar because the pmi agreement only covers interceptions of at least 50 000 cigarettes. so that's a very good thing for the tobacco industry. now the big question: should we renew the agreement with pmi? in my group's view, no. the researchers already point out that it is at least curious when you conclude a cooperation agreement with the same manufacturers who are legally challenging the european tobacco directive. in addition, within a few years new legal rules will enter into force which will oblige pmi to make similar arrangements to those agreed in the agreement. the main argument, however, is that the rules of the world health organization require that any appearance of conflict of interest or entanglement with the tobacco industry be avoided. let us not become dependent on money that sticks to blood. i therefore hope that today we can agree with the commission that the cooperation with pmi will not be renewed.

on behalf of the alde group the danger of antibiotic resistance is still too much underestimated. the use of antibiotics worldwide is increasing dramatically, by as much as 67 percent in just one generation. however, no new classes of antibiotics have been added since 1987. action must therefore be taken both veterinary and human. that this is possible is proven by the netherlands! in just four years, the use of antibiotics in animal husbandry has been reduced by more than half. it is good that the dutch presidency has made this a priority. for the first time in history, a meeting of the ministers of health and agriculture has been organized, and the so-called 'one health approach' is essential. the new animal health law now being negotiated in europe is a step in the right direction. the liberal group's efforts have preserved some crucial elements, such as the legal obligation to use antibiotics responsibly. antibiotic reduction is not an easy task. it can only be done through smart collaboration, good management and innovation in, for example, livestock systems. the cost aspect is important. it cannot be that one farmer has to invest and the other does not. we want to ensure a level playing field while at the same time eradicating the problem at its root. good legislation is one thing, but what matters is implementation. the netherlands is leading the world in reducing antibiotic use. it is high time that the rest of europe followed suit.
member of the commission.– mr president, honourable members, once again i would like to express my gratitude to the rapporteurs, madam paulsen and mr selimović, the shadow rapporteurs, and their team for their excellent and tireless work. i have listened to the views of this house with close attention. the various points illustrate the very strong interest of the honourable members in this legislative initiative. some of you mentioned the economic situation, and the commission is aware about today’s economic situation which, in some cases, will damage animal health issues. some of you raised questions about stray dogs: the european parliament recently adopted a resolution calling upon the commission to adopt rules for the compulsory identification and registration of dogs and cats and some other animals commonly used as pets. the new animal health law is a legal framework and contains basic rules, including those for the identification and registration of animals. it provides more specific rules only for those animal species where the identification and registration rules exist at present. however, the regulation also provides for the possibility to lay down further detailed requirements for the identification and registration of animals of other species such as dogs, cats, and so on, for which specific requirements do not exist at present. the necessity of such future rules will be assessed by the commission in the course of the years following the adoption of animal health law, and then delegated and implementing acts will be prepared. the commission is committed to animal welfare and considers it a very important issue, as you just raised in the discussions. however, the scope of this regulation focuses on the transmissible animal diseases and does not directly regulate animal welfare. a number of compromise solutions were found during the trialogue negotiations to clarify the relation between animal health and animal welfare and to address the concerns of the parliament on animal welfare. it was not always easy to agree to all of them; nonetheless, the commission understands the importance of animal welfare for the european parliament and has therefore made a statement confirming its commitment to animal welfare. the union has a well-developed acquis regarding animal welfare, covering different species (broilers, laying hens, pigs, calves) or activities (farming, transport, slaughter, research, etc.). this animal welfare legislation will necessarily continue to apply. some mentioned antimicrobial resistance. antimicrobial resistance is very important to me, therefore issues of antimicrobial resistance were always included in the commission’s proposal on animal health. this, together with the future legislation on veterinary medicines and medicated feed, will provide solid grounds to address this issue. finally, the commission has also made the statement, ‘the commission undertakes to publish a regular report as regards the use of antimicrobial medicinal products in animals in the eu on the basis of data made available by the member states.’ let me finish by expressing my sincere hope for the support of parliament for an early second reading agreement on this file, which would pave the way for its multiple benefits to come to fruition as early as possible.
in writing. this bill deals with animal diseases and animal health. that's why i want to emphasize: this is not an animal welfare bill! a new animal welfare strategy is still very much needed and i am still waiting for a proposal for a framework law on animal welfare from the commission! fortunately, the proposal does stipulate that pain, stress and suffering in animals should be avoided when implementing animal health legislation. the legislation also introduces mandatory registration of professional breeders and sellers of dogs. this is the way to tackle the illegal trade in puppies, which is still leading to countless painful examples of animal suffering. there are still puppy mills where puppies are illegally and unvaccinated traded on the conveyor belt. this has got to stop! good identification and registration can help. however, a major sticking point in the law remains the distinction between domestic and wild animals. stray and runaway animals are included in the definition of wild animals; the presence of the owner or carer of an animal is therefore a determining factor in the degree of protection against disease (such as vaccination) and disease control measures. it was never the intention to kill healthy stray animals "preventively" under the guise of disease control measures.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr fleckenstein, for his report. he's put his neck out and got down to business. the ppe, alde and ecr groups have therefore now achieved an excellent result with the rapporteur. i think of the following points: firstly, the reduction of red tape and social burdens for ports. initially, the proposal was full of social provisions, such as the obligation for port companies to provide training and education for port workers. but many port companies don't even deal with that. that's right now. secondly, ports now have a choice about how they want to be organised. mandatory market access is no longer an issue. europe is now aligning itself with the situation in the regions of the member states, not the other way around. port companies decide how they want to be organised, and rightly so. the situation in rotterdam is very different from that in vigo, spain, for example. subsidiarity and sovereignty have really been vindicated. thirdly, there is an exception for private ports. we have paid particular attention to the situation in the private ports of england and they can now arrange for services such as towing or mooring of ships. they also don't have to go through any complicated tendering procedures, so that's also very nice. some are calling for the proposal to be rejected. that would be very stupid. i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, because then the directorate-general for competition will look around and commissioner vestager will make sure that the provisions which apply to competition also apply to ports. well, we're not really far from home until that box of tricks is pulled open. it would therefore be very unwise to reject this and i call on everyone, including my own group, to support this proposal.
in writing. ‒ the 2014 annual report on the protection of the eu's financial interests, voted on by the european parliament on tuesday 8 march 2016, is misleading. the report is based on the commission's wish, supported by the european parliament, to be able to determine for itself whether member states are corrupt and, if so, to what extent. that is a task which has been well done by independent journalists to date. but independent investigations have consistently shown that a number of member states are deeply corrupt. and so the european commission, itself previously dismissed for corruption, wants to come up with new rules. that's like asking the mob to rewrite the criminal code. the pvv voted against.
in writing. – human dignity, respect and a safe place to stay! let us not forget this when we talk about women refugees and asylum seekers in the eu and their vulnerable situation. let us talk about people and not only about numbers. we are talking about women who are too afraid to close their eyes in the mixed sleeping areas of reception centres. we are talking about victims of sexual violence. we are talking about people whom we are failing to protect, whose multiple victimisation we are not able to prevent. women suffer disproportionately on their way to the eu and while staying in reception centres. we fail to protect them sufficiently and need to take appropriate measures to change this degrading situation. we need to prioritise these measures: first, make available safe and legal routes to the eu, by cooperating with third countries such as turkey and by unravelling criminal networks. second, we need to improve our internal cooperation; stop talking about numbers – take action! third, we need to ensure that gender budgeting is applied while funding facilities for refugees: practical measures to make separate sleeping arrangements and bathrooms, and to organise gender sensitivity training for coast guards, police, interviewers and interpreters.

in your introduction to this debate, madam high representative, you referred to discussions you had yesterday with your turkish partners. there are three recent events that concern me, which is why i ask: how do you define turkey's role in the regional war currently raging in syria? on two occasions, hundreds of heavily armed jihadists, whether from jabhat al-nusra or the islamic state, have attacked kurdish units from turkish territory. once at the city of azaz, the second time at the city of tel abyad. so turkish territory is being used as a base for jihadists in that war. finally, a third event: on 28 february the greek authorities detained a ship from turkey, loaded with heavy weapons, on its way to lebanon. mrs mogherini, i have the impression that turkey is more the problem than the solution to the crisis situation surrounding this whole syrian regional war.

last weekend in my own country, the headline news was that hundreds of romanian workers were working for one euro in dutch shipyards, hired through a dutch office with a mailbox in romania. unfortunately, that was no accident. the romanian workers are not to blame, but the people who deliberately seek out loopholes in the law are. some people say that you are moving too fast with the revision of the secondment directive. i am inclined to say that you are finally realising that a union of 28 different labour markets can only exist by guaranteeing fair labour mobility and the protection of workers. your proposal to amend the posting of workers directive is a step in the right direction. i am in favour of extending chain responsibility and the obligation for temporary employment agencies to pay workers the same as they are in the host country. but that's not the end of the story. there are still large differences in social security contributions between member states, as is the case with romanian workers who worked in the shipyards in the netherlands. in the netherlands, they call it "premium shopping". the commission has not yet taken a decision on this. i therefore look forward to your proposals for social security coordination later this year, because only by introducing equal pay for equal work in the same workplace can we maintain the support and solidarity in the union, also in the future.
first of all, i am glad that this proposal is now on the table and that we can start working on it. we all know that there are quite different views on the posting directive between the member states, and also here in parliament. i would therefore like to compliment mrs thyssen on her steadfastness on this matter. this is an important proposal to improve the position of two million posted workers in europe and to put an end to unfair competition in the labour market. the free movement of services and persons is a very important cornerstone of the internal market, but it must be done in a fair way: not just free mobility, but fair mobility, on a level playing field. the proposal meets two of my major priorities in this area. first of all, the duration of the secondment. secondment is by definition temporary and should be regulated as such. we can and will debate in this parliament the exact length of secondment, but the proposal to limit the duration is very good. the same applies to the proposals on the remuneration of posted workers. it is clear that the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on social affairs and employment. this proposal by commissioner thyssen is a balanced proposal, a good basis for starting our work here. i hope that we here in the european parliament, from left to right and from east to west, north and south, will proceed in the same balanced way.
i have three questions. what good is a pan-european labour market if people need two jobs to pay the bills? what does a zero-hour contract do for an educated young man in spain who is desperate to find work to start a family? and what does a skills approach do for a worker in bulgaria who earns 95 euros a month in a garment factory? these are the questions which i believe the social pillar should address. central should be about: how do we ensure labour markets that can support our ambitions for innovation, while being inclusive and providing support when needed? after all, you're not 21 and in top shape every day of your life. the second. how do we adequately combat poverty and inequality, but also make work worthwhile and thereby boost purchasing power? the third. how can we actually strengthen the social partners by turning their agreements into legislation rather than just lip service? in short, commissioner, not words but deeds and preferably good deeds and a little quickly.
i am very pleased to see that the commission has taken this initiative. i should like to say something about the idiotic sums promised to the wrong turkey or the immense sums that have been transferred to greece in vain. but i want to talk about the flexibility instrument. parliament wants to have an extra amount of money on top of the budget for emergencies and that is a very bad idea. after all, how do you know if today's emergency is the worst thing that's gonna happen this year? because the eu is causing quite a lot. and before you know it, you've spent your emergency money prematurely and wrong. and i predict what's going to happen: i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i am saying that it should be involved in the work of the council. governments are notoriously unable to deal with money, not at national level and certainly not at european level, and therefore less, less, less money to the eu and no flexibility instrument.

commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the oecd predicts that in just one generation, the use of antibiotics worldwide will increase by 67%. action must therefore be taken both veterinary and human. it is irresponsible to compensate for poor animal housing with the preventive use of antibiotics. in addition, further research into new antibiotics, veterinary medicines and vaccines is crucial. the use of genetic modification should remain possible, as is the case with human medicines. for example, this technology produces insulin for diabetic patients. in some cases, a gmo drug or vaccine is the only solution. the amendments seeking to ban genetic modification therefore put both human and animal health at risk and have no basis in reality.
as the ecr shadow rapporteur on the committee on agriculture, i attach great importance to this dossier on veterinary medicines. the point is that the legislative framework stimulates the development of new veterinary medicines. this includes a reasonable period of data protection, as also mentioned by the rapporteur. reducing administrative burdens is also important. it is a good thing that companies will soon be able to convert a decentralized marketing authorization into a central authorization. responsible use of antibiotics is a shared responsibility. i am glad that the wording on community has been incorporated into the text. i would point out that reductions in antibiotic use already achieved in some member states should be recognised in future reduction targets. for a concerted effort and a level playing field are necessary to achieve an effective and lasting result.

i hear a colleague say we should finish what we started. and indeed, the banking union was one of the greatest achievements of the last parliamentary term, with the aim of stopping the citizens from taking the irresponsible risks of banks. if i were to give most of my colleagues here a blank page, they would no doubt sign a banking union with three pillars: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the financial markets are not affected. but in times of crisis, much has happened and trust between member states has been greatly affected. we must not pretend that the guarantee of deposits is not already covered by the national systems we have. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. so we don't just have to finish what we started. we have to do what we agreed to. and just as we have a no creditor worst off criterion, we should also have a no member state worst off criterion. i'm for a third pillar, but not in a hurry! so do it carefully, or we'll be building on quicksand. [ the speaker will answer a 'blue card' question in accordance with rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure.]
blue-card answer. – exactly. therefore, look at the rules. the rules already are that deposits of up to eur 200 000 are guaranteed through the national schemes. if banks are irresponsible and give their clients products that are high-risk without informing them, that is a consumer issue, but not a deposit guarantee issue. what i am afraid of is if member states – and i do not mention any – try to find a way around the ‘bail in’ principle, which we actually have in order to reduce risk and to reduce moral hazard, then that is basically saying that we do not want bail in, so we want to give the bill back to the taxpayer and back towards the state. i am afraid that some member states do not want to send the bill to their own member state, but to another one. this is why i said we should work towards a third pillar, but with no member state worse off.
