on behalf of the alde group the pig price has fallen sharply due to overproduction and a closed russian market. the russian market should be opened to products which are not part of the political embargo but are boycotted because of the alleged african swine fever. we need to be the weed countermeasure, breaking into alternative markets. japan is a good example of this. but the most important thing is that we focus on structural solutions. the position of the pig farmer in the chain should be improved, for example through producer organisations. european pig farmers must invest heavily to meet strict animal welfare and environmental requirements. these investments must be able to earn back the pig farmers' market share. a level playing field in the eu is of unprecedented importance. in some countries, money from brussels is being used to increase pork production even further, while there is a surplus. moreover, european agreements are far from being properly implemented everywhere. it is great to see that the sector itself is also taking initiatives to improve its market position, such as product innovations. they should have more room.
the crisis in the pig sector has gone on for far too long. on behalf of the sectoral organisation in my country, the netherlands, a few questions. is the commission prepared to consider alternative measures to private storage? this is an exceptional crisis situation. the sector considers that temporary early slaughter is a more effective measure than the storage of meat. the price will then improve without risk of subsequent market distortion. secondly, the reopening of the russian market is of great importance. the wto has given a negative assessment of the russian measures and a technical solution with russia should be quickly negotiated. finally, i would like to stress, with my colleague mr huitema, the need for a level playing field within the eu and beyond.
the pig industry has been bouncing from one crisis to another for decades. this crisis is not new. we've had crises from swine fever, from swine flu, crises from market collapses, crises from hormone scandals, you name it. and only because europe wants to be the butcher of the whole world! millions of pigs are kept in miserable conditions to be turned into cheap hams and carbonades as quickly as possible. many pigs are castrated without anesthesia. tails and teeth are cut off when the animals are less than seven days old, and this despite a 20-year ban. the problems are not caused by the collapse of the market in russia. there's simply more supply than demand. instead of reducing production, we store the meat in freezers and advertise eating pork. europe must no longer be allowed to be dragged along by the livestock industry, which is unwillingly growing this animal-friendly, polluting and disease-causing industry. this is the time to shrink and reform the sector. this pig industry is as sick and unstable as the pigs that live in it. that's why i don't want any more money coming in here. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
as always, pig farming is the last item on the agenda. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's agricultural policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. often there is no money for the household either. since the russian boycott - and hopefully it will be resolved soon - supply and demand have been completely out of balance. while european pig production continues to increase, prices are falling. at the same time, the cost of living in the netherlands has increased enormously in recent years due to the stricter application of environmental and animal welfare standards, mrs hazekamp. this means that farmers in the netherlands have an extra cost of 20 cents per kilogram of carcass weight compared with 7 cents in germany and denmark. the pig market therefore lacks a single european level playing field. the 10 million euros which the dutch government has allocated to pig farmers from the 500 million extra crisis fund is a small consolation. the money does not go into the farmer's wallet to pay for the costs. we also need to help those who want to quit. so , what do you say ? 1) good guidance for growers who want to stop; 2) help for those who want to start over; (iii) aid to pig farmers wishing to expand their holdings. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i thought crisis money was for all sectors. finally, improving the bargaining position is an important part of the solution. last year, i had the pleasure of receiving a good plan from copa-cogeca for the farmers' actions in brussels. it shows how farmers, feed suppliers, retailers and government get a fair price. shouldn't this be an example for europe?
mr president, i am lucky that i checked when the public debate would be in plenary, because i just found out that it is now! but my own process was as difficult as the whole process of the authorisation by the commission, because let us be clear: what kind of process is it when a commissioner, after tabling the authorisation, sends letters to the biggest industrial giant in this area asking for more transparency? so there have been huge difficulties and yes, we have efsa, we have a strong role in our decision-making process in europe, and we count on efsa, and efsa is relatively clear in its statement. so my group believes that we should look very closely at what efsa is saying. but efsa is not saying anything about the amount of time – the number of years – that we should go for. therefore we have just tabled an amendment, as the liberal group, in which we call on the commission to renew the approval of glyphosate with a maximum of five years, and to take into account any new evidence up to that time which proves that it is more dangerous than we previously thought, in which case the authorisation should be withdrawn. that is what we just tabled 20 minutes ago, and i hope that you can follow that line during the plenary vote.
i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this. nine committees! these are a lot of parliamentarians who are supposed to represent the people of the european union and who have wasted a lot of time and money in the last few months. because all you are proposing is to create more opportunities to come to the european union. holistically, in the sense of all-encompassing, you do quite literally take it by inviting the whole world to come this way. no suggestions to end the inflation of asylum, no mention of isis terrorists traveling with asylum seekers to spread death and destruction. no representation of the people who are fed up with this influx of refugees and who want their country to remain their country. the rubble of open borders cannot be solved by more european union. for that, you must close the borders and de-islamise!
the market situation of all our farmers and gardeners deserves attention. but then we must talk about structural measures, otherwise we shall soon be back here again. i am particularly interested in whether the 500 million euro which the eu gave to the agricultural sector seven months ago has really made a difference. are we going to continue to suggest that politics can do something to help the market recover or are we going to make real choices to make the sector stronger and healthier? europe must set a common course and strive to improve its competitiveness. first, take a serious look at the cost side. we must curb the drive to rule at national and european level. ensure effective rules and leave room for innovation and entrepreneurship. with new techniques and insights, we can produce more efficiently and more cheaply. secondly, add value to our agricultural products by differentiating ourselves in terms of taste, quality or sustainability. thirdly, look for better ways of working together, between farmers and gardeners, and across the whole chain. in short, come up with a concrete plan and don't write blank checks. finally, it is good that the commission is opening up new markets and promoting trade. the commitment and ambition of commissioner hogan are to be commended, but in doing so keep a close eye on the interests of each agricultural sector and hold our trading partners to the agreements made. because what about the development of new trade agreements, which i welcome? and how does the commission ensure that agreements are more strictly observed and that all subsectors, such as the poultry sector, are closely monitored?
the crisis in our agricultural sectors continues. the distribution of value in the chain is very uneven and that is a structural problem. in a time of crisis, however, this can make the difference between survival and bankruptcy for a farmer. the task force on agricultural markets is an important step in the long term. article 222 on crisis cartels has also been introduced, but only for dairy products. is the commission prepared to make wider use of article 222 and to do so in a more timely manner so that farmers in other sectors can also make arrangements? commissioner, it is not possible for one link in the chain (the primary producer) to be the only one to bear the consequences of the crisis. give farmers all the means to strengthen their position, so that the other links in the chain share proportionately in the consequences of the crisis.
i also speak on behalf of my colleague tom vandenkendelaere from belgium. europe was born after the second world war. it was about peace, but it was also about food, food production, produced by family farmers and family businesses. we almost forgot. but that is the strength of europe. look at the netherlands and belgium: sixty percent of the pig farmers don't make it. and 50 percent of dairy farmers are in serious financial trouble. i am not even talking about related sectors such as wage-earners and the like. many people think that market innovation is the answer, but it's not. there are other things. it is also a matter of a level playing field when it comes to state aid. it is also a question of how the crisis money should be put on the farmers' kitchen table at the moment. last july i asked for a plan of action. some people laughed at me back then, but we're a year ahead now. commissioner, you have proposed a plan and others have said things. let us work towards a fair, social, balanced market economy within europe. if we do this together quickly, we will preserve family farms and food production in europe. but if we don't start doing that with each other, then power, capital and multinationals will take over this entire sector. and what does that mean for a livable countryside? do we think enough about that here in this house, in this chamber?
i promised a few weeks ago to keep you informed of the results of the referendum held in the netherlands last week. the dutch people voted on an association agreement with ukraine and voted overwhelmingly against it. two thirds of the dutch people say no, but here 90% say yes. i am going to round off, let this be a nice prelude to the referendum in the united kingdom. [ the president shall decline the floor.]
in writing. ‒ this report follows the european commission's policy of a minimum vat rate of 15% until 2017. this is a missed opportunity, because member states should be able to apply a much lower vat rate for sustainable, healthy, environmentally and animal-friendly goods and services. on the one hand, the commission preaches sustainability, but when it comes down to it, member states are not free to tax the production of sustainable products and services and to encourage the purchase of these products. the commission's vat action plan should provide much more flexibility for member states to set vat rates for sustainable, healthy, environmentally and animal-friendly goods and services. but that idea is not in the report, and that is why i did not agree to it.
in writing. ‒ the sp group abstained from voting on the report. the sp has serious objections to the eu's agreement with turkey of 18 march 2016, as no guarantees have been provided that syrian refugees will not be returned to syria. reports from amnesty international and others show that this is happening, which is contrary to the refugee convention.
in writing. ‒ the sp group abstained from voting on the report. the sp has serious objections to the eu's agreement with turkey of 18 march 2016, as no guarantees have been provided that syrian refugees will not be returned to syria. reports from amnesty international and others show that this is happening, which is contrary to the refugee convention.
in writing. ‒ proportionality and subsidiarity are essential founding principles of the european legislative process. a subsidiarity test is necessary, because not everything needs to be harmonised immediately. if it can be better regulated at national or regional level, then european legislation is certainly not necessary. it is a shame that some amendments have been adopted, which include the controversial investor state dispute settlement (isds) mechanism in the text. it increases the influence of companies. multinationals use isds clauses in trade agreements to claim money from countries that they see as setting restrictive rules to protect the environment, animal welfare or public health, for example. this undermines democracy and the rule of law and isds will certainly not pass the proportionality and subsidiarity test.
in writing. ‒ the regulatory fitness and performance programme (refit) should aim to reduce the bureaucratic burden of european regulation on citizens and businesses. but it is mainly used in the fields of environmental protection, social legislation, animal welfare, public health, consumer protection and food safety. regulations that should protect citizens, animals and the environment must now give way to short-sighted commercial interests and the pursuit of profit; the commission has already taken the necessary steps to ensure that the rules are applied. however, this refit is mainly used to reduce consumer protection. for example, there are calls to delete the so-called 'nutrition profiles'. however, these nutrition profiles ensure that consumers are not misled by health claims on unhealthy foods with too much fat, salt or sugar. junk food is and remains unhealthy and should not be promoted as 'healthy' or 'vitamin-rich'.
in writing. ‒ this report on small-scale fisheries is a step in the right direction, but it lacks a critical note. european fish stocks are severely overfished and populations are threatened with extinction. there is a great need for a shift towards less fishing and more sustainability. the report calls for more exceptions, more quotas and more flexibility for small fisheries, but that is contrary to the common fisheries policy. if we do not start now to drastically reduce the impact of fishing on the marine environment, many fish species will go extinct. that is why i voted against this report.
in writing. ‒ sustainable management of global fish stocks requires a view beyond europe's borders. the eu is a big fish on the world stage; the eu's market for marine products is one of the largest in the world and the eu has concluded several fisheries agreements with third countries. although europe is a major contributor to global overfishing, it is important that this external dimension of the european fisheries policy is integrated into the common fisheries policy. the report makes a good contribution to a more sustainable management of the oceans. the commission has also been active in the fight against fraud, corruption and money laundering, and has also been involved in the development of the european union's fisheries policy. that is why i voted for this report.
i would like to express my deepest sympathy to the victims and survivors of the recent attacks in brussels. it is incredibly sad that these attacks could have been prevented. the national borders should have been closed long ago, because these heroes of islam traveled freely between the netherlands and belgium. the fight against islam should have started long ago, because muslim ghettos are cheering and protecting these muslim heroes. let me be clear, mr president, this is how i see it: you too are morally complicit in these attacks, as are many other meps, commissioners and national government leaders who all call for open borders and for the welcome of migrants from islamic countries. and we're losing more lives, thanks in part to you. how hypocritical was the commemoration of the victims of these attacks in this parliament? we are mourning the victims, while a section of this parliament itself is maintaining the conditions for attacks. moreover, israel should not have been left out of the list of countries hit by attacks! any form of counterterrorism begins with recognizing the cause of terrorism. you refuse to acknowledge this cause and you refuse to name it. the cause of terrorism worldwide is islam. islam has been an ideology of terror for 1400 years. the so-called "religion of peace" has killed at least 240 million people! islam is incompatible with our western values, islam is incompatible with our western culture. i say to you and to the european commission: admit your disgusting failure and step up. let someone else deal with this problem! because the counterterrorism approach is clear: close the national borders, stop mass immigration and stop islam! (the speaker will reply to a question on a blue card in accordance with rule 162 (8) of the rules of procedure.)
"blue card" is the answer. here again i hear the typical dismissal, denial, ignoring and trivialization of the problem as it really is. it is not only in belgium itself, in molenbeek, and in all those other parts of brussels where the streets were danced and cheered when the attacks on zaventem and maalbeek took place. and then we still get these kinds of stupid questions. i'm throwing that away!
mr president, i think we have to note that the so-called war on terror has failed. for 15 years now, since 9/11, we have been passing one measure after the other, and every time the terrorists are one step ahead. it is high time we rethink our strategy and do not, after every attack, in a pavlov reflex, talk about new megalomaniac it systems – because what we need is not more dots, but to connect the dots. i have been listening to the debate on pnr for many years now. there is not a debate that is as fact-free as the debate on pnr. there is a lot of genuine ignorance, but also a lot of lies. i would really like to know what has been keeping the member states from setting up national pnr systems. as a matter of fact, they have received eur 50 million from the commission for setting up those systems. we do not know what happened to the money. france, which has been pushing for pnr, received eur 18 million. what has happened to the money? they have got the legislation in place. so, instead of talking about new it systems all the time, what we need is, firstly, mandatory information-sharing – and i do not understand why the ppe and the ecr are categorically opposed to this. secondly, we need human intelligence and, thirdly, prevention. that is what we should be investing in, because that will bring greater security to citizens and not just talk, talk, talk about it systems. (the speaker agreed to take a blue-card question under rule 162(8))
blue-card answer.– mr woolfe, first of all i did not say that this parliament has failed to implement; quite the opposite. i said that we have been passing one legislative measure after another and actually, like data retention, it is not being implemented by the member states, and we will have the same problem with the pnr directive. but you cannot deny – and certainly not after the brussels and paris attacks – that so much information on the terrorists was available and it has not been shared. it has been sitting in a drawer and it was not used. whether we are talking about 9/11, boston, toulouse, london, madrid, brussels, paris, the killing of theo van gogh, mumbai: in every single case, the terrorists were known to the authorities beforehand, but they failed to share the information. they need to connect the dots; that is what they need to start doing.
blue-card question.– to be correct, i have never said that we categorically reject the use of pnr data, but first there has to be added value. if there is only collection of data at national level but no exchange, then what is the added value of a european directive? i do not understand – it really escapes me – why the epp, the ecr and the member state governments continue to refuse mandatory sharing of information. if we had that, i would be on your side.
as rapporteur for the fourth revision of the anti-money laundering directive, i negotiated the public register for the ultimate stakeholders of that directive. thank you to parliament for making this possible. that register is necessary to make it impossible for letterbox companies to do their job. to get these kinds of things like the panama papers out in the open and not just rely on leaks, on investigative journalists. now i know that the european commission is going to come forward on 1 june with new proposals to amend this directive again to tighten up the financing of terrorism. i think that is the time to look at the register again, because one of the shortcomings of the register is that it does not include trusts. we could not negotiate that with the council at the time, partly because david cameron did not want to. but there were other member states. so june 1st seems to me the appropriate time to make new proposals for trusts.
in the run-up to this debate i have consulted a number of cuba experts. like me, they all welcome cooperation between the eu and cuba, as long as it is not limited to the regime but includes civil society as well! i want to emphasize that! it is precisely on this last point that i have heard criticisms, also in my own country, of the eu from people who know cuba inside and out. whoever fits the shoe, madam high representative, put it on. as a member of the protestant community in the netherlands, which has for many years maintained close ties with the cuban church and christians, i also urge the high representative to appeal directly and continuously to the cuban authorities for concrete respect for freedom of religion and belief on their own territory. this freedom excludes, first, a system of government informers and informers in church meetings and congregations. that's very important, because there's just an atmosphere of fear, and that says a lot about the regime. so please let us not leave it to a facade which is cosmetic rather than reality. because then we'd be heading in the wrong direction.
madam president, of course we should negotiate and cooperate with the neighbouring countries, but that is not the same as outsourcing problems. negotiations take place between two equal powers, but the eu is weak as we are unwilling to solve our own problems, and the turks realise that. we should have learned from earlier experiences, like in 2010, when we were negotiating a deal with mr gaddafi for keeping the refugees there. that was also not a resounding success. i heard mr weber speak earlier, saying that there is finally, as he said, ‘recht und ordnung’. well that is not the way i perceive it, because we hear stories about people being pushed back to syria – refugees – and we have zero answers from the council or the member states. it is a disgrace that this continent – the richest, most prosperous and freest continent in the world – is tolerating this, and that we are not able to give our own response to the situation. finally, we talk a lot these days about our own values, about how immigrants need to embrace our values. let us uphold our own values and make sure that refugees live in acceptable conditions and not in this misery anymore.
you lock people up, they can't take care of themselves. when you lock people up, you have to take care of them. you have to feed them enough, you have to make sure that the sick are helped, you have to make sure that the children are protected. but what did 28 prime ministers do? they made a deal with turkey and forgot about the execution. and meanwhile, we're locking up refugees on greek islands with insufficient food, children between adults in overcrowded camps where there's not enough food and no doctors and no lawyers! if the prime ministers have put on the big pants to make a deal, they will have to put on the big pants to arrange the implementation and not look to the european commission to implement it. then they must also have an answer to the democratic control? is it the greek parliament that is to oversee the detention centres in greece or can we do something about it? what happens to a 14-year-old girl without food in a detention center with grown men? mr. tusk, what happens to a 14-year-old girl? blue-card answer.– i have a one-point plan, which is large-scale resettlement from third countries, giving people legal access to the european union. but as you heard, my critique was not about what erdoğan does or what turkey does, but what we are not doing, what we are leaving behind: that is, taking care of vulnerable people in detention centres.
visa-free entry for turks, accession to the european union, a few billion euros and a migrant exchange procedure. that's the great deal with turkey! and this commission is also accepting genocide against the kurdish population, oil and arms trade with the islamic state, the silencing of the opposition, the occupation of cyprus and - most sad of all - for the citizens of europe more violence by migrants, more oppression by stealthy islamization, more poverty by the skyrocketing costs of shelter. this commission has no moral compass. and i said: we are the voice of the people and we say on behalf of the people: enough is enough! this bad deal has to go! stop mass immigration, close national borders and send migrants back!
the trojan horse is from ankara. the deal with erdogan is barely working. only a few hundred refugees are now in europe and only a few hundred are now back in turkey. meanwhile, the flow of refugees from italy is increasing by the hour! it is also absurd that the turks have been promised visa-free travel to the european union and a concrete prospect of accession! turkey is now creeping into the eu like the trojan horse of the past. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. five days after the dutch referendum, ukrainians were also promised visa-free travel. how do you make it up! the presidency and the commission must do their homework better. there needs to be a comprehensive plan with selection at the gate to help real refugees.
turkey and the eu need each other to address the refugee crisis in a humane way. for years, we thought that the crisis in syria did not affect us, and we did not care much about the fate of the syrians who fled en masse to neighboring countries. this changed only when more than a million asylum seekers arrived in the eu last year. the way we have dealt with the influx of this vulnerable group of people to date is not a beauty contest. only a few european countries are now taking in the refugees. there is no question of shared solidarity. the agreements with turkey are far from perfect and still need to be worked out. but the alternative is for countries to continue to raise fences and tighten barbed wire, while people continue to drown at sea. that is not my europe! it is therefore our duty to make this agreement work and to defend our values. cooperation with turkey must comply with european and international law. the report by amnesty on the pushback of refugees from turkey to syria is alarming. it is now up to the european commission to ensure that we have clear guarantees that this practice will stop and until then syrians cannot be returned to turkey. but it is also important that the european leaders get on with it and stick to their commitments: the commission will continue to support the efforts of the turkish authorities to ensure that the eu's external borders are fully respected.
in writing. ‒ the sp voted against the amendment welcoming the gsp+ application of kyrgyzstan. the gsp+ trade preferences may only be granted to countries that are signatories to 27 un conventions in the fields of human rights, labour rights and environmental protection. kyrgyzstan has not successfully implemented these conventions and should therefore not receive trade preferences through gsp+ schemes. the sp voted in favour of the report as a whole, as it is very critical of developments in the field of human rights, democracy and the fight against corruption in the partner countries concerned and stresses that trade and geopolitical interests of the union are subordinated to human rights, environment and development in the (european) policy in the region.

on behalf of the alde group.– madam president, i too would like to extend my compliments to the rapporteurs, to former commissioner reding, to commissioner jourová and the luxembourg presidency. i will never forget the long sessions we had with a lot of coffee and not a whole lot of food. this is the result of it. well, we got food but not very healthy food: lots of sandwiches and cookies. when we set out on this adventure, we were just getting acquainted with the first generation of ipads. i suppose there is not anybody in this room who does not have an ipad. well, when we started – the first resolution – it had only just entered the market, so just to show how quickly things are evolving: a lot more quickly than we are legislating. i too believe that the regulation and the directive do not constitute an obstacle, they actually remove obstacles. something that we have been discussing a lot lately, and we will discuss it later today, is fighting terrorism and sharing information. i am convinced that having common standards on data protection will facilitate sharing of information. it will create the trust that agencies need to share information. i also think it is in the interest of the internal market, and we need the internal market to remove obstacles and have a single rule book throughout the european union. we need that urgently if you listen to companies like spotify who are threatening to leave the european union. the final remark: i am very glad that europe is once again setting global standards. i think we can be really proud of ourselves, but of course everything hinges on the application and the enforcement. just today there was a statement by the article 29 working party on the privacy shield. i would strongly urge the commission to take their recommendation extremely seriously and show that we are serious about data protection, not just on paper but also in practice.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of personal data. others have spoken about it, but today the european data protection authorities have once again reminded the european commission that privacy shield, the agreement between the european union and the united states, the replacement for safe harbor, is not complete. that it does not protect the data of european citizens and that those citizens in america still have no room to claim their rights. i think that the fact that we are all agreeing on a standard for the protection of our personal data is also the reason why you must renegotiate with the americans. it is still a matter of time before our data protection authorities are enforced or the next court case is brought. and no one wants to see our cell phones, our other equipment, go black. so, commissioner, it is up to you to make improvements.
on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, first of all i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, not least because this is the start of a new chapter in his life where he will not meet with daily harassment by his favourite shadow rapporteur from the alde group. but i will do something unusual for a politician: i am going to confess that in my group we have been struggling with this topic. emotions have been running high, we have had and we still have very difficult debates. but i am actually quite proud of the liberal group that we have that debate, that we have discussion and doubt and that we do not take a position blindfolded, that we are not dogmatic or ideological or represent certain interests or opportunism. and we have to confess that external pressure has been huge; the debate has often been completely fact-free, the lobby has been aggressive and sometimes immoral, and there have been many ad hominem attacks, but we leave all that behind us. now we may all assess the outcome differently, including in my group – i will personally vote against, that will not come as a surprise – but it remains to be seen whether we get the security that everybody is talking about, or whether this is just fake security. my group was unanimous on a number of principles. we wanted a truly european and effective pnr. we wanted a regulation that would enter into force immediately. we wanted mandatory sharing of information, i.e. the result of analysis. we wanted targeted data collection instead of indiscriminate data collection. we wanted the single system, not 28 systems. but all these proposals have been categorically refused by the ppe, the ecr and the member states. the question is, will this directive see the same fate as the data retention directive? on behalf of the s&d group.– madam president, today the eu and turkey are facing many of the same challenges. think about the refugee crisis, the threat of terrorism, and a long-lasting conflict in syria that creates instability. one thing that has been made clear through these challenges is that turkey and the eu need each other to find solutions to combat these issues. however, and i want to stress this, we cannot turn a blind eye to the internal developments in the country. turkey must be stable and more democratic if we want it to be a strategic partner we can build on. linking the accession process to migration cooperation is neither wise nor fair. accelerating talks should depend on the pace of the reforms, not on a political tit-for-tat. a lot has already been said this morning on the eu-turkey deal, so i will just briefly say: the turkish population has been hosting three million refugees. many european countries could learn from the hospitality shown in this regard by the turks. but turkey is reaching its limits, and it is time that the eu really reached out to help by providing large-scale resettlement of refugees to eu countries. that is genuine burden-sharing; that is genuine cooperation. many chances to further connect and work together have been missed in recent years. let our current talks on migration lead to a more intensified and honest dialogue. this means that genuine concerns should not be taken as insults. the situation in the south-east of the country is of great concern to this parliament. the eu urges that the peace process be relaunched as there is no military solution to the kurdish question. the current status quo is unacceptable. while we deeply regret the return to violence by the pkk and strongly condemn the attacks against security forces and civilians, we stress, however, that anti-terrorism measures taken by the government must be proportionate and in line with international law. the 400 000 citizens who had to flee their houses because of the violence and the 24-hour curfews in many kurdish-populated cities have put a very heavy toll on the livelihood of ordinary people. we believe that turkey has to return to a path where journalists are not imprisoned for doing their jobs and censorship does not become an instinct for journalists. turkey has to return to a path where academics are not losing their jobs and put in jail for expressing their views. finally, turkey has to return to a path where judges do not hesitate to decide and to give a verdict out of fear of being displaced or fired. we need a more stable and democratic turkey. this is not a threat but an encouragement. it is important that turkey returns to the path of democratic reforms and grasps this opportunity of closer engagement with the eu. finally, let me also thank my colleagues and co-rapporteurs for the good cooperation. i hope that we can send a strong signal from this parliament with this resolution. blue-card answer.– it is clear that it would be too harsh to say that it is because of the eu that the process is not accelerating. first of all, the responsibility lies clearly with the turkish government. it is a merit-based process, but we must judge turkey the same way we also judge other candidate countries. i have to say there have certainly been political differences at play here, with some political parties and politicians in europe not always treating turkey fairly based on the reforms in the country.

the directive on the protection of trade secrets is not only unnecessary but also dangerous for the position of whistleblowers, journalists and trade unions. i now read to you the statement my group received from antoine deltour, the whistleblower behind luxleaks, in english. in 2012, i gave documents to a journalist whose publication triggered the luxleaks scandal. i was awarded the european citizens' award by the european parliament last year for this. but both me and the journalist are now being prosecuted in luxembourg for trade secrets violation. the exemptions provided in this directive to supposedly protect whistle-blowers would not protect me or the journalist, because we did not reveal anything illegal, just immoral. this directive will enable companies to sue anyone who accesses, uses or publishes any information that they consider a trade secret, which, according to the definition provided in the text, can be almost any internal information. it applies to all citizens and not, as it should, to economic competitors only. before you vote on this directive, i urge you to reflect on this question: do you really want a society where it's impossible for the public to access information crucial to the public good? so much for antoine deltour. on april 26th, i will be present at the trial against deltour. i hope i don't have to go in there empty-handed. the committee on budgetary control has already agreed that i will write a report on the goal of whistleblowers in protecting the financial interests of the eu. well, that's something. but it is unacceptable that we have not yet reached any agreement on legislation that actually protects whistleblowers, but we do adopt legislation that directly undermines their position. with the adoption of this directive, no more luxleaks, no more panama papers! but more lawsuits against whistleblowers! i therefore call on my colleagues tomorrow to support our amendment to reject the bill in its entirety.

on behalf of the alde group.– mr president, today’s debate on how to shape the rules-based trade system and how one of our main trade partners, china, can be held to its commitment to fair trade practice is key for europe. we need to ensure eu policies are in place after the protocol of china’s wto accession, which was agreed to 15 years ago, expires. our group feels strongly about not leaving this to the wto courts to decide. not only do we need solid eu trade defence instruments in general, but specifically the eu needs to be able to apply anti-dumping measures in cases of dumping of goods by china beyond the end of this year. our group wants a thorough assessment of the legal consequences of the expiration of the protocol, as well as a long-term legal, economic and political impact assessment. we look forward to seeing the results of the outcome of the public consultation and the commission’s impact assessment. we need eu measures to be wto compliant. it is the only credible way to underline the importance that we adhere to global trade based on rules that apply equally to all. this debate is not, contrary to what the name may suggest, a debate on whether or not china’s economy operates according to free trade and fair competition laws and principles. we share the concerns about state interference, the creation of overcapacity, the lack of transparency and the existence of reliable indicators that can predictably verify whether prices are the result of supply and demand or whether they are kept artificially low. what we do know is that wages and environmental standards are lower in china than they are in the eu. in fact, importers in europe benefit from this aspect of globalisation, and it can also lead to lower prices for consumers. so it is key to look at all sides of this debate, both the short-term implications and the long-term, and particularly also to liaise with our trading partners and key allies in making the right decisions in this complex but impactful file.
shortly after our last debate in february, the european chamber of commerce in china published a report on overcapacity in the people's republic of china. the report concludes that the role of the chinese government in the economy is part of the problem, and that is rather mildly expressed. this is further proof that china is not a market economy. it is all the more reason to make rapid and well-considered decisions on china's market economy status. european industry must be protected and remain protected against unfair competition. i expect that, and our citizens expect that of the council, the commission and this parliament. shortly before this debate i received news from a good friend, a dutch worker. he's home now, he used to work in the steel industry. it is the faces and the threat of unemployment that should certainly guide us in this debate on the market economy status for china. fair trade and fair competition? unfortunately, that's not the case here.
the commission is therefore proposing that the council should adopt the proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. the country is a patient that needs a financial infusion every six months. it has already cost 215 billion euros, and what has happened to this money? a small 10 billion went into the greek budget. almost 140 billion has been used to pay off french and german bank loans, to save these banks. the european citizens must bleed for the mistakes of the european elite. in the case of the netherlands, it is already about 25 billion euros. money we'll never get back. money coughed up by hardworking dutchmen. think of what we could all have done with those billions, for health care, for our seniors, for pensions, for strengthening our borders, for our security. the president of the council of ministers, mr rutte, promised during the elections: "no more penny to greece". what a perverted liar. the netherlands, like so many european countries, such as greece, was given a policy of demolition and cuts before the elections. all to artificially keep the bankrupt eu and the bankrupt euro alive. this money faucet has to close. no more billions to greece, no more grabbing at the pensions of european citizens. the euro must be abolished. greece must return to the drachma. enough is enough.
when wolfgang schäuble arrived at the euro group yesterday, he said that greece had made wrong decisions and had to pay the price. it reminds me of a tantalus sting: the moment the target approaches, the target is moved. greece has undergone a massive budgetary adjustment. it was necessary, but it was unparalleled. and yet the target has been moved. last year a surplus of three and a half per cent was agreed. so, in addition to the wrong decisions of greece, we can also talk about the unrealistic demands of the eu countries. christine lagarde has called the target of 3.5 percent unrealistic and the imf has acknowledged that the issue of debt relief should have been on the agenda as early as 2010. it is therefore time for the eu countries to look critically at their own actions and demands. not because i believe in schäuble's guilt and penance, but because i think you can learn from mistakes. as from 24 may it must be clear that greece will receive such debt relief that greece can stand on its own feet again.
for the ecr group earlier this year, in february, jordan's king abdullah warned that his country had reached a boiling point because of the influx of hundreds of thousands of syrian refugees. the facts speak for themselves. syrian refugees now make up 20 percent of the total jordanian population. as a result, the hashemite kingdom is forced to spend as much as 25 percent of its national budget on them. the budgetary stretch in amman is therefore completely gone. eu assistance in this critical situation in the form of creating necessary jobs think of boosting the tourism sector and improving market access for jordanian products to the european market is more than welcome. i wholeheartedly support the requests made by the jordanian authorities in this resolution. the situation in lebanon is even more dramatic. there, syrian refugees already make up 25 percent of the total population. moreover, 90 percent of these syrian refugees are non-sunni, with all the implications for the delicate religious balance between sunnis, shiites and christians in the country. new lebanese visa measures for syrians have now led to large-scale illegal syrian immigration into the country. the sad result is that currently 70 percent of syrian refugees in lebanon are in abject poverty. just last month, a research report revealed that 70 percent of syrian refugee children in lebanon are ruthlessly exploited in the labor market. an insider told me that there is slavery and human trafficking on a large scale, not to mention the abhorrent sexual slavery that syrian women are suffering physically and mentally in lebanon. i would ask you, high representative, to urge the lebanese authorities to address the abuse of children and women by syrian refugees in their own country and to ensure that their human dignity is protected to the fullest.
madam president, i would like to thank the high representative for her heartfelt remarks today on a very tough situation in which too many people find themselves. the risk, danger and additional human suffering of a destabilised lebanon – and that is what i will focus on – must not be underestimated, because the vast majority of the approximately 1.2 refugees in lebanon are young and female. with a population of 4 million people in total, no other country is sheltering so many – until recently, with open borders. the lebanese people are five times as poor as the average european, and 25% of the population are refugees. let those who claim that we are the first to suffer the consequences of the ongoing terrible war in syria, think again and look to lebanon. it should humble and shame all of us. let the situation in lebanon we are discussing today be a reality check for those who still preach shelter in the region as a mantra that will be a self-fulfilling prophecy, or who see the outsourcing of our problems to turkey as the only credible solution. do we plan to do as comprehensive a deal with lebanon, or only when people reach our shores? this would be too cynical, but we do have to look at what is spent per capita, also in lebanon, and make sure that we look at the bigger picture, including stability, infrastructure, countering terrorism and countering radicalisation.
for the ecr group by financing illegal construction projects in area c of the west bank, the eu is violating the 1995 oslo accords, to which it is a signatory. this unilateral action by the eu, albeit under the guise of humanitarian aid, is in breach of israel's military and administrative powers in area c. the eu's arbitrary action in area c only deepens the impasse in the israeli-palestinian conflict. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament. i therefore urge the high representative to call for an immediate halt to all european aid to illegal construction projects in area c. and it is strange to me that on the very night that israel remembers its fallen in the wars and its victims of terrorism - it is about the bigger picture - we are here again to israelbash. not a word about the hamas terror tunnels. there is also talk of the two-state solution, but then you have to tell me when mahmoud abbas recognized israel as a jewish state, let alone hamas. i do not hear any clarity on this from the eu side. and then talk about a two-state solution! it 's more than hypocritical . it's irresponsible. it is european self-disqualification in israel and also in the region. it is high time for political reflection in the midst of all the crises that are plaguing brussels today.
"blue card" question. what is it? unfortunately, this solidarity is lacking. there is a large group of member states which do not want to cooperate on a joint concept. that is a regrettable but realistic fact and we must find an answer to it. now you may say that solidarity must be established, but we must have a means of enforcing it. the means chosen now are far too heavy and out of proportion. how do you propose to address this lack of solidarity and yet ensure that care is provided jointly?
refugees are future-oriented. they think about a safe place now, but they also think about their children and what kind of existence those children will have in the future. member states seem to be living in the present: i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to introduce a new system of controls on the movement of persons, which is now being introduced in the member states. the european commission is generally forward-looking. mr avramopoulos, you are here for the future of europe and its citizens, and we want to work together for stability in the region. so the urgency must be very great for you to make such a short-sighted proposal to change the dublin system. my question to you is: what can we do to help you? for the future of europe and for the future of those refugee children. "blue card" is the answer. thank you for your attention to the issue of missing children. if children could have come to europe legally, they wouldn't be missing now, that's one. secondly, i think that a lot of children who have arrived in greece or italy have registered there. then, like many refugees, they set off on foot to join their families elsewhere. and that is something that we all allow because we have an asylum policy under the motto 'survival of the fittest'.
first, bringing unlimited fortune seekers to europe, pushing european countries into a gigantic crisis, and then proposing more european union as a solution: that is what the european commission is doing over and over again, on the way to a european superstate. so is today. but it could be crazier! because if a country declares that it can no longer cope with the influx, if it wants to defend itself against the tsunami of asylum seekers and additional islamization by closing its borders, it can expect a fine of up to eur 250 000 per asylum seeker. solidarity is what mr timmermans calls it. i said: it's totally crazy. the ruins of the european union cannot be solved by more european union. and you're not gonna solve it by blackmailing me and threatening me with fines. it is not solved by taking away more sovereignty from the member states. i therefore call on you to abandon these plans and to let the countries take back control of their own borders.
blue-card question.– i hear you say we should take as many people as we are able to take in, referring to a kind of absorption rate. so what are you going to say to the people who are fleeing a war situation, who come to europe and who, according to your definitions, we cannot absorb any more? what are you going to say to them? but my more important question is: do you not think that it is an absolute disgrace to have that kind of language in the richest, most prosperous, freest and safest continent in the world, to say that we actually do not have the capacity to take those people in?
all member states must cooperate in finding solutions for the reception of refugees. unfortunately, it is impossible to make a compulsory distribution of refugees across the member states, but in the light of this political reality, we must think of alternatives. a fine of a quarter of a million euros per rejected asylum seeker, that's a lot of blood. in this way the commission is imposing a very large amount of pressure on the member states. this proposal will divide europe and that is the wheat on the mill for geert wilders and his european political friends. my counterproposal is: the commission has also proposed to set aside eur 5 billion per year from the structural funds to support member states which take in asylum seekers. half of this money comes from the cohesion fund, the fund which mainly benefits countries which have not yet cooperated. i think this is a fair, budget-neutral solution which will, above all, prevent further european division.
it is disappointing to see that our governments in europe lack the support and political courage to make a thorough review of the dublin agreements and thus the willingness to work towards a genuine common european asylum policy based on solidarity and shared responsibility. that is why we must do so with this proposal from the european commission. not a big leap forward, just a small step in the right direction. for example, the proposed distribution key prevents member states from having to come to the table again and again to quarrel over which country should take in how many people when another member state cannot temporarily cope with the influx. the proposed penalties for countries which refuse to honour previously agreed agreements do not deserve the prize of beauty. it is too bizarre to say that we must enforce solidarity within the eu, but unfortunately the current situation, in which some countries are reluctant to take in refugees at all, leaves the commission with no other choice. i would urge the european governments to speed up this decision. thousands of people in italy and greece are still waiting for the promised redistribution. we cannot leave the countries on the periphery of europe to take the blame for this humanitarian crisis. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, on behalf of the alde group i am very glad that we are having this debate. we requested it because we welcome the roadmap that was presented by the commission for restoring a fully-functioning schengen system. however, in practice we do not see much progress. we see more, rather than less, border controls. we see walls being put up, not broken down, and a couple of days ago we even heard about a violent incident on the border of one of the schengen countries with refugees being shot at, so the situation is actually getting worse, not better. maybe schengen is not yet dead but it is certainly on life support. what i would like to know from the commission is how strictly are you applying the criteria, because you keep saying it has to be necessary and proportionate, but that seems to have become a bit of a mantra because we do not see border controls disappearing. i do not see any steps in the direction of abolishing them. we seem to think that it is a kind of replacement for a fully-fledged refugee and security policy or a replacement for a european border and coast guard, but this is in the hands of the member states. the member states should take their responsibility and no longer hesitate about a common asylum policy, the common security policy and a border and coast guard, rather than reinstating border controls. i think it has been said by others that schengen is a great achievement and the current border controls come at a huge economic cost, so we have every interest in abolishing the border controls as quickly as possible. i would really like to know from the commission: how do you assess the recurrent requests from the member states for authorising border checks? quite frankly i have not seen many cases where the commission has said: sorry, this is simply not justified. when we say it has to be ‘exceptional and temporary’ we are talking about, i believe, six schengen countries now, or eight, for a very extended period of time. that does not sound like exceptional and temporary. i understand that you are under a lot of pressure from the member states who want the commission to actually clean up the mess left by the member states, but i really hope that you will resist the pressures.
president-in-office of the council. – madam president, let me start by saying that i consider this debate as a truly useful opportunity to hear the views of this house on the issue. from the outset i must however underline that my own comments will be preliminary. the council has just started examining the report presented by the commission; in other words there is no council position yet. first of all i would like to stress that visa liberalisation for turkey is a long-standing commitment. it was decided to start this process back in 2011. the roadmap was adopted already in 2013 and what did the eu-turkey statement do on 18 march? it accelerated the existing process. secondly, i would like to stress that visa freedom for turkey is something that benefits, at the very end, all parties concerned. not only can turkish citizens travel to europe more easily, but the millions of europeans visiting turkey for holidays or business, for example, can also do so more easily. it will facilitate people-to-people contact. it was, i believe, one of your meps, judith sargentini – i do not know whether she is in the chamber today – who said it is also about the turkish human rights defenders who will be able to travel to europe freely to tell their story. in other words this is not just a present for president erdoğan, neither should it be seen as just a concession by europe to turkey. it is something from which we all, at the very end, benefit. now in the commission’s third report it is concluded that turkey has achieved remarkable progress. however it also becomes abundantly clear that turkey has not yet managed to fulfil all 72 benchmarks. seven benchmarks remain unfulfilled. regarding two of these seven outstanding requirements, the commission has noted that for practical and procedural reasons, turkey needs a longer timeline for implementation. with regard to the biometric passports, the practical solution consists of limiting visa freedom to the holders of the latest generation of biometric passports. when it comes to turkey’s obligation to build up a track record under the eu-turkey readmission agreement, we simply need a bit more time as this agreement only becomes effective next month. for the other five requirements, the commission has invited the turkish authorities to undertake urgently the measures that are necessary to fulfil these benchmarks. in other words, turkey has a lot of homework to do and i also, from this chair, urge the turkish government to intensify its efforts. now as you know, we have, besides turkey, visa freedom proposals also on the table for georgia, ukraine and kosovo. at the same time the commission has presented a proposal to amend regulation 539 with a view to revising the current suspension mechanism. the main objective is to strengthen this mechanism by making it easier for member states to notify circumstances leading to a possible suspension, and by enabling the commission to trigger the mechanism on its own initiative, and yes, at a time when we are considering granting visa liberalisation to a 130 million citizens, it is only natural to look again at the current suspension mechanism. the commission’s proposal responds to a broadly-felt need in the council to have a more effective mechanism and we are grateful to the commission for this proposal. it will undoubtedly facilitate decision-making. it is now up to us, parliament and the council, as co-legislators, to start working on the different proposals and as i said work in the council has started. the council’s visa working party met this morning and if one thing becomes clear from the initial discussions, it is that this is by no means an easy decision. what also came out of the discussions is the firm determination of all member states to stick to a merit-based approach. we will not take a decision based on political expediency. turkey needs to fulfil the benchmarks and i do trust that this house shares that conviction. there is broad consensus within council to advance quickly on the revision of the suspension and monitoring mechanism with the aim of agreeing a position at the justice and home affairs council of 20 may. that is the good news. in closing, the presidency will take the necessary measures to make sure that all proposals are discussed in a timely manner in the council and that talks with parliament are started as soon as you are in a position to do so.
member of the commission. – madam president, on 4 may the commission presented its third report on turkey’s progress in fulfilling the requirements of its visa liberalisation roadmap dating from 2013, in parallel to comply with the strict timeframe set by the eu member states heads of state and government and on the understanding that the turkish authorities will take the necessary measures to fulfil all the outstanding benchmarks. on 4 may the commission also presented a proposal to transfer turkey to the list of visa-free countries. the commission’s proposal opens the way for the european parliament and the member states to decide to lift turkish citizens’ visa requirements once the benchmarks have been met. it also gives – and it is equally important – adequate time for consulting the national parliaments on this issue. let me underline that in the last months, the turkish authorities have made really substantial progress in fulfilling the visa liberalisation roadmap, with a further acceleration since the 18 march eu-turkey summit. as a result of this, for example, they are now fully cooperating with greece on readmission. we see a clear reduction of the number of irregular arrivals in greece from 6 000 a day in october 2015 to around 140 with a continuing downward trend. we also have to commend turkey for hosting around 2.7 million syrian refugees and more than 200 000 non-syrian asylum seekers and refugees, granting to all of them free access to healthcare and education, and now also to legal employment. meanwhile, they are also improving the asylum procedures. they have ratified all international conventions and protocols and judicial cooperation indicated by the visa liberalisation roadmap, and as requested by the roadmap they have also adopted several pieces of legislation, for instance on trafficking in human beings and on judicial cooperation and on equality and anti-discrimination, and action plans on social inclusion of citizens with a roma background, on corruption, in the fight against drugs trafficking, in the fight against organised crime which they are starting to implement. but let me be clear as well that there is still progress to be made. this implies for turkey to take the following steps: to adopt measures to fight against corruption in line with the recommendations of the council of europe; to align its legislation on personal data protection with eu standards; to conclude an operational cooperation agreement with europol; to offer effective judicial cooperation in criminal matters to all member states; and last but not least, the need to better align with the european standards its legislation and practices on terrorism. in the eu-turkey statement of 18 march, the turkish authorities expressed their determination to fulfil all benchmarks of the visa liberalisation roadmap by june. i am confident they remain committed to implement all aspects of that statement. in any case, the commission will continue to monitor closely the fulfilling of these benchmarks, and naturally i and my services will ensure that you are regularly updated. we have to remain optimistic that the turkish authorities will give that final push before the end of june. i want to be clear: this agreement is beneficial for both sides. we are not watering down our standards in order to provide visa-free, but we are doing everything we can to provide support and expertise to the turkish authorities to accelerate what are also quite important reforms in their own right. what is also important in their path towards the european union? the implementation of visa reforms will not only contribute to the internal welfare of turkey but will also help the eu and turkey to develop effective cooperation within the framework of the global strategy that we are building with this key neighbour. we are confronted with the same challenges. cooperation is necessary. i will give an example. only yesterday turkey was hit by another terrorist attack. i take this opportunity to express my thoughts for those injured, and my indignation. terrorism must be condemned in every form and everywhere. and we know how sensitive the issue of terrorism is for turkey. but this proves how beneficial the close cooperation with europol is for both sides. for this to be achieved, turkey needs to further align its data protection standards to the european ones. not just because this is required by the roadmap, but because the reforms required by the roadmap are the basis for an even stronger cooperation on both security and migration, with sensitive data exchanges that need adequate protection. it is true that turkey is considering this cooperation as a catalyst, i would say, to reenergise our relations as well as its european perspective. indeed, it is an opportunity for turkey to come closer to the european standards on all issues. the fulfilment of the visa liberalisation roadmap is an important step towards this direction. i would now like to mention another element of the package, adopted on 4 march. the commission also proposed to revise the existing suspension mechanism of the visa-free regime. i want to be very clear on this. the suspension mechanism is not about turkey. it is a horizontal provision which will apply to all visa-free countries and not only turkey. i hope that the co-legislators will look favourably at this proposal, although it should not delay the deliberations and the adoption of the visa liberalisation proposal.

madam president, the time when turkish people feel least european is when they wait in line for a visa. this statement was made by a former turkish eu minister some years ago, and i believe it is still valid today. the lengthy and bureaucratic procedure to obtain a visa is of great nuisance for turkish citizens who want to travel to the eu for business, studies or simply for enjoying their holidays. it is therefore good that the eu and turkey started negotiations two years ago in order to lift this visa requirement. as part of the eu—turkey deal on refugees, it was agreed that the eu is willing to accelerate that process if and when turkey meets all 72 criteria. but while accelerating that process was part of the deal, lowering our standards certainly was not. let us be honest: turkey over the last few months has come much closer to meeting the set of technical standards than any of us would have thought possible, but the commission has also made it clear that there are still five criteria where turkey does not fulfil the benchmarks. among these are the fight against corruption, an independent data protection authority and, most importantly, turkey’s very broadly defined anti-terror legislation – the same anti-terror legislation which has been criticised for many years by us here in parliament, not because turkey does not have the right to fight against let me start on the positive. the european commission seems to have seen the light and is making a welcome change. that is what i thought when commissioner moscovici published the economic forecasts last week. the economic outlook no longer focuses on reforms, but this time on the commission's concern about the growing inequality in europe. better half turned than all lost. i fully endorse this analysis. a more equal society is not just fairer. it's also a proven recipe for economic growth. it was also the reason for the oecd to ring the bell and call on countries to take action. the commissioner just said that our social model is fit for purpose. but if that were the case, we wouldn't be dealing with rising unemployment and rising inequality. in recent years, the european commission has paid little attention to the uneven results of the compelling advice to member states to cut back on the welfare state. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft budget. i am therefore very curious to see what conclusions the commissioner will draw from his own new insights. is there a change in the direction of policy advice to member states? will there be policies that counter rather than reinforce inequality? and secondly: will there be a greater role for trade unions? after all, labour income has been falling for years. the employees are getting a smaller and smaller piece of the pie. the top of the corporate world and the shareholders are walking away with most of the profits. if we put inequality on the agenda, not for charity but because it threatens prosperity, then it has to be about more than analysis. we must take concrete action to combat inequality.
i understand that some consumers want to know where the ingredients in processed foods come from. however, i am not in favour of the eu making it compulsory to mention this on the label. i want you to imagine: a bowl of breakfast yogurt. that yogurt is made from milk, milk powder, fruit, grains and sugar from different countries. the proposal would make it compulsory to indicate the origin of all these ingredients. that's going to be a long tag. the administrative burden and controls for this origin labelling cause higher costs and thus a higher price. a higher price that the consumer is not always willing to pay. in addition, some manufacturers already voluntarily indicate the origin of ingredients on their labels in order to distinguish their products from others. in other words, if the consumer really wants to know where the ingredients come from and also wants to pay for these extra costs, they can just do that. the eu does not need to intervene in this respect.
compulsory labelling of origin is a political instrument of some regions and member states for their own product marketing, at the expense of farmers, at the expense of the european food sector and at the expense of our internal market. this has nothing to do with the fight against food fraud, but with the unrelenting protectionism of member states. food fraud is tackled with equal enforcement across the eu, with effective inspection and not with unnecessary and useless additional bureaucratic requirements on origin labelling for ingredients. fresh meat must already be traceable and the origin must already be indicated on the label. i'm not going to introduce double bureaucracy. how is our butcher supposed to start chopping his balls or labeling his sausages? how should a dairy cooperative separate its milk by origin and set up separate production streams? compulsory labelling leads to excessive extra production costs for our smes and for the food sector. the origin labelling is a threat to agricultural jobs, because it can increase production costs by 8 to 45%, as we read in the commission's report. this is a serious disadvantage for food businesses in the union's border regions. their ingredients are often sourced from several member states. the consumer, meanwhile, is not prepared to pay for the slightest price increase in the store. the price pressure of these enormous additional costs is thus, as so often happens, being passed on to our farmers, who are already in a crisis. the compulsory labelling of origin is an unworkable, unaffordable bureaucratic burden for the craft sector and entrepreneurs, a bureaucratic burden which we, the epp group, want to reduce together with the european commissioner frans timmermans. don't do this to the consumer. the consumer does not want mandatory origin labelling, which would result in such a large price increase. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's fisheries policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new species.
we are talking about european interference with working conditions, namely parental leave. the rapporteur herself says in her own report that social security is a matter for the member states and not for the european union. the proposal is therefore simply contrary to the treaty, to the agreements reached in the ilo and to the legal obligation in the dutch consultation on working conditions to have open and genuine consultations with the social partners. in short, the whole directive is illegal, reprehensible, but also contrary to all the agreements that have been made. why are we even discussing this in this parliament? that is because this parliament considers itself to be a higher moral authority, because this parliament does not believe in subsidiarity and because the european parliament arrogantly thinks it knows better than millions of workers, employers and trade unions, who have been achieving results for decades.
one day to pick up mother and child from the hospital and one day to register the child with the municipality, that's what dutch men get paid parental leave in 2016. and after those two days, get back to work. meanwhile, a father is missing a wonderful part of parenthood. there are arrangements for payment in caos. when caos provide for paid parental leave, you see that fathers are more likely to take it. but legally, there are only two paid parental leave days for fathers. the father is missing something, but the mother is missing something essential, because by staying at home for a long time, she loses her connection with her work and loses her skills and experience. so it's not just the parents who are hurt, but the economy and society as a whole. that is why i am so pleased with mrs arena's proposal, because it challenges the commission to come up with real modern proposals for a modern division of labour and care, which challenges and stimulates parents, and which does not impose choices but allows choices.

the commission is proposing to harmonize all the rules on internet purchases and to make it a single market. this is not at all illogical, but it is a lot at once and it also means that the specific problems facing smes and consumers are sometimes not solved. i'll give you two examples, there are many. the commission acknowledges that smes often have difficulty in being visible in the results of search engines such as google or on price comparison websites. large companies have more money to get priority on such platforms and the result is that the consumer does not get a complete overview of all providers. although this is an acute problem for smes, the commission will first study this for six months to see if it can be solved by self-regulation. i think that's a missed opportunity. every day that a company is not visible on platforms is costing revenue and further undermining the level playing field. i therefore call for more support in this area. a very different example: a practical problem facing consumers is the interest on concert tickets. in its proposals, the commission is insisting on the rule that a website offering such tickets should charge the same price to all consumers in the european union. that is good, but a much bigger problem is the lack of transparency of the various ticket providers. for example, large quantities may be resold by hand and tickets sold out on the organisation's website but offered at high prices via other online merchants. will the commission do something about this?

i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, mr markus ferber, for the cheerful and constructive but also tenacious way in which he has guided us as a negotiating team through these difficult, difficult discussions. these have enormous implications not only for the financial markets themselves but also for all users, all european consumers and all the businesses that are affected. because we are dealing with the difficulty of finding the right balance. the commission is of course well aware of the ambition of the capital markets union. we want more jobs, we want more economic growth. we all support that wholeheartedly. the financial sector must also make a major contribution to this by making financing easier and by ensuring that the money needed as a lubricant also flows. we are also, of course, dealing with the commission's better regulation agenda of commissioner timmermans. that is very difficult in a complex file like mifid/mifir, which is incredibly technical. i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken the initiative to present this report to the european parliament and to the council. how do you balance those interests? we have seen that this is a struggle, that it has not been easy and that this has also caused delays. we understand that. it is more important to have good, workable regulations than to have them drawn up quickly. we also felt that the risks of disrupting markets were too great, so we fully support this delay. but that does not mean that we should not use the deferral - as mr dodds has just said - to make the rules really better and more workable. there are also still issues around - i'll just mention one - a definition of research. what is research? that is the essence of this regulation, it is not just a detail. so we're going to have to find good solutions to that to get as much clarity as possible. clearness on those parts of the legislation which are essential. because we're not getting anywhere with giving people all this unnecessary information. it should be relevant information, not information about transactions that may never happen, but information that is relevant to investment decisions. to quote einstein again: not everything you can measure is valuable, and not everything that is valuable can be measured. we will always have to use common sense in this. it is also good that there is constant contact with consumer protection organisations, but also with the financial sector itself, to ensure that we finally get workable regulation that benefits consumers and investors, and that does not have to be too much for the sector because it creates too much administrative burden.
