mr president, the eu needs effective, flexible and meaningful measures to tackle unfair trade practices such as subsidies and dumping, and it is unacceptable to note that right at the moment when we need it most, with the imminent expiry of a key provision of china’s wto accession protocol later this year – and right when overcapacity on the global market is hurting our already struggling industries, for example the steel industry – we are paying the price for paralysis and disagreement in council. the council has no time to lose in finding agreement on trade defence instruments – while avoiding protectionism, because that is no way to strengthen open economies and fair trade. equally, we must be aware that the best way to address breaches of fair-trade rules by others is for us to lead by example. eu measures must be wto compliant. let us also be well aware of the other side of the coin. while they may not be so vocal, the eu also has many companies that import, also providing jobs for people. our economies and other economies are globally interdependent and retaliatory measures can hurt. of course, other factors such as low oil prices impact many, including the steel industry. it is an illusion for anyone to think that trade defence instruments alone can save one industry or another. so our group, the liberal group, is seeking an overall balanced trade defence instruments package to ensure fair trade globally, not only vis-à-vis china, although it is an important player here. we need the right measures suitable for an interdependent global economy, flexible and effective and wto compliant.
there is a great deal of uncertainty about what we are going to see in the next six months as regards china's place in world trade. as you can see, there is a lot of anxiety in this room. of course, we assume that china will not be granted market economy status, but article 15a (2) of the accession treaty will lapse anyway. i would therefore urge the council and the commission to come forward with a proposal which also guarantees that the eu can continue to use the analogue country method in the future to determine dumping from countries and sectors where there are clearly no market economy conditions. we need that method to continue to protect our industry effectively against unfair competition.
on behalf of the enf group. mr president, there are legitimate concerns about japan's decision to resume whaling, despite the decision of the international court of justice. japan plans to catch about 4,000 whales over 12 years, and the recent killing of 541 whales, including 207 pregnant females, off australia seems completely irresponsible. the scientific justification given by japan is not sound, and is similar to the arguments in favour of laboratory animal testing, which are for the most part completely useless. the proposals of our parliament to put pressure on japan, or even to bring legal proceedings against it, are interesting, but they are vague and do not suggest any concrete means of pressure. but there are ways, particularly in the fisheries agreements with developing countries. japan often has fisheries agreements with the same countries as the european union. could not the resumption of whaling be an argument to be made in the negotiations? i'm asking the question. finally, japan is not the only threat to the survival of whales, as plastic waste is poisoning them and leading to massive strandings, especially on european coasts. and it is not plastic bags that kill them, but the building blocks of agricultural greenhouses used in the netherlands and spain. it is therefore important to sweep up the mess outside our door, also by dealing with this problem which affects us directly.
today is world oceans day. people worldwide are concerned about the survival of whales. many are angry at japan, for despite a ban, japan has again killed over 300 whales in recent months, including 200 pregnant ones. the largest animals on our planet are hunted with large-bore weapons. after a long chase, the exhausted animals are shot with explosive harpoons that pierce deep into the flesh. the heavily wounded animals are tackled on board and shot to death. this ordeal often lasts several hours. there is no justification for such a slaughter. europe can put an end to this suffering by applying pressure to japan through its trade policy. if we really want to do something about whaling, we will not talk about a trade agreement as long as japan continues this cruel and irresponsible illegal whaling. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.

member of the commission. – madam president, honourable members, on behalf of the european commission and of course commissioner karmenu vella who is responsible for the file. in the college? i would like to thank the rapporteur, shadow rapporteur and the committee on fisheries for all the hard work they have done to enable agreement to be reached for the multiannual plan for the management of the stock of cod, herring and sprout and fisheries in the baltic sea. the baltic plan is the first of its kind; a landmark. it is the first of a new generation of multi-annual plans. these are important tools for the further implementation of our reformed common fisheries policy and for implementing its most ambitious element, the obligation to end wasteful discarding of fish and land all catches. in addition, these multiannual plans provide an important framework for the development of a more decentralized way of fisheries management, which what we called regionalisation. with regionalisation in mind, i would also like to take the opportunity to needed to state formally our declaration in relation to regional cooperation. when proposing and adopting delegated acts under their regionalisation provisions of this plan, the commissioner will comply with the framework set out in the basic regulation, in particular article 18, and the practice established under the basic regulation implementing the regionalisation process will continue as it has since 2014. the baltic plan is the culmination of a process that has involved many players working together to reach consensus across many interests of involved parties. this process has included the baltic fishing industry and the fishery ministers of concerned countries as well as the legislators of course, and their interests. the outcome is a balanced regulation that has incorporated the key concerns raised throughout the process. the agreement of both legislators has provided the blueprint for this new generation of multi-annual plans. this is very important for other marine regions for which the development of these plans will allow for sustainable long-term management. i will look forward to cooperating with you as we present the next set of proposals for further fisheries management plans for your careful consideration.
i think that no one can be happy about this multiannual plan for the baltic sea because this plan does not protect the fishermen and this plan does not protect the fish. this plan raises the maximum sustainable yield, the point at which waters are fished to their maximum. and maximum sustainable yield is nothing but policy greed. pillage as much of the available fish stock as possible. and we're not talking about irons here, we're talking about living things that can feel pain and fear. and even the maximum catch from the sea is not enough. this plan goes one step further: increasing the maximum sustainable yield, and for that i have only one word: overfishing. and so this baltic sea plan weakens the whole common fisheries policy. and that also opens the door to other waters, like the north sea, to continue to be structurally depleted. many of you have joined me in opposing this plan for overfishing. and that's why i'm surprised you're defending this plan today. i can't do that anyway. the rapporteur has invited us to have a glass of champagne tomorrow and i ask you, ladies and gentlemen: can you raise your glass tomorrow with your head held high? if this is the maximum that europe can achieve, then overfishing will only stop when the seas are really empty, and unfortunately most of us will have to live through that. i also believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
we have finally reached agreement on a fisheries plan for the baltic sea. the draft plan was presented in october 2014, negotiations started in may 2015, and now after a year and a half, we finally have a legislative agreement. it was a long road and the multiannual plan actually became a multiannual plan for this parliament too. i hope that the examination of other multiannual plans, particularly the north sea plan, will now be able to proceed much more rapidly. the framework is clear: we have chosen to set limits within which member states may set fishing quotas. these bandwidths are particularly needed for the north sea, where we have a lot of mixed fisheries. and with those bandwidths, we can reach maximum sustainable yield and all fish stocks can benefit from that, too. that is the objective of the common fisheries policy. finally, i would like to thank mr wałęsa and his team very much. it was a spirited negotiation. but we got along.

the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: why does the eu pay employers to fire people? why is frontex getting money to pick up so-called refugees instead of stopping them? and why did i have to travel a day to talk here in strasbourg for a minute and a half? spending ceilings are being raised. member states are obliged to finance all the objectives of the european union, while brexit will reduce the number of net contributors to the union. and although each british farmer receives a subsidy of 20 000 euros a year, those same farmers voted to leave the union. they too prefer free enterprise to subsidized citizenship. american research shows that increasing regulation has reduced economic growth by 0.8% per year since 1980. that's $13,000 a year for every citizen. but it takes energy and taxes, and therefore economic growth, to control the lives of others and to be controlled. it is no wonder that for 25 years the european union has promised nothing but prosperity and employment. because nowhere is economic growth lower than here. and that the public sector is a cost item is a lesson that the european union has clearly not understood. the eu budget is not a pillar of support but a chokepoint!
i have to say, we're way behind. it's really a sign on the wall that money going to tax havens has grown twice as fast in the last decade as savings in the world. and it is a sign on the wall that a third of foreign direct investment is through financial structures in tax havens. we are lagging behind and we must make up the gap, especially as a new offshore centre is emerging, namely the united kingdom, which now wants to reduce corporate tax rates to 15% and increase tax competition. i would therefore like to see a blacklist of tax havens, but also a framework for the united nations in which countries constantly agree on the approach to tax avoidance, including developing countries. the report by michael theurer and jeppe kofod is very welcome in making up for this backlog. thank you for that. it also shows that there is a complex of financial institutions, tax advisors that contribute to that tax avoidance. it needs to be addressed. financial institutions should lose their licence if they participate in transactions that are questionable, but there should also be a code of conduct for tax advisers if they participate in structures that are questionable, and the intertwining of universities and tax advisers should be ended. and if i read the commission saying that it will shed light on these activities, that is not enough. we're gonna have to take a hard line on this industrial complex of tax advisors and financial institutions.
this was a rush job and i don't really understand why we're proud of it. this proposal is a rattling one, and if i may give you an example, i'll make it personal: the dutch government does not control its borders. we have a long coastline and we're not doing anything about it. and the other member states feel that they cannot and they want to intervene. and which country is going to help? belgium, please! that land is next to us. belgium is going to control the dutch coast. and then the belgian coast guard picks up a smuggler. whether it's drugs or people, but the belgian coast guard does. then where do we land this smuggler to stand trial? in rotterdam or in ostend? under whose authority is this happening? or we make it worse: that smuggler is not getting caught. no, in a clumsy operation by the belgian coast guard in dutch waters, the smuggler drowns. who's in charge here? not the belgians. the dutch, because it's dutch water. ladies and gentlemen, it's not going to happen, this system. it's not the european border and coast guard. it is still national activities, it is frontex plus. it's not helping our refugee crisis, it's a front wheel. it's a rush job, it's a crochet job!
i live in the beautiful town of stramproy in the dutch province of limburg, and i live there a few hundred paces from the belgian border. i am therefore experiencing the luxury of a europe without internal frontiers as no one else has. but that luxury also brings obligations, and that obligation is that we are jointly responsible for the protection of our external borders, and that requires strict, good and efficient surveillance. the dutch external border is no longer at molenbeersel, but at lesbos and lampedusa. we just saw in 2015 that with that joint border guard, there's still a lot of backlog maintenance. it is therefore also good that europe has now taken action, that within six months this wish for better border control has become a reality and i look forward to its swift implementation. europeans expect us to ensure that the member states have their affairs in order when it comes to protecting the external borders. and it's up to us to carry that out now.
the question of approving a european border guard is not so easy to answer. the title of the agency raises questions such as: are we now going to have eu marshals guarding our borders? the original proposal of the european commission was indeed in that direction. the commission wants to be able to deploy border guards at the european external borders, if necessary, without being asked. in the negotiations between the council and parliament, that point has disappeared, and i think that is very right. now we have a reinforced frontex agency. it will be able to have a reserve of some 1 500 border guards provided by the member states. fifty of them are from the netherlands. the border guard will be deployed at the european external border only at the request of a member state and after a decision by all member states in the council. the agency shall support member states which continue to monitor their borders themselves. and that's especially true in hotspots, the arrival points for refugees. the border guard will also assist member states in returning asylum seekers who have been processed. we want open borders in europe, where they can be opened in a safe and responsible way. that's good for business, good for our citizens. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the european border guard is therefore an example of cooperation to which we say yes.
the european parliament has called for the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the european union's research and innovation policy is properly implemented. this will lead to economic growth, but also to new jobs. but the fact is that europe lags behind other continents when it comes to spending on research and development. that means that we must make every euro invested from the european budget pay off as much as possible. that was also the message of commission president juncker when he took office. he promised us in his state of the union address last year to increase the impact of european investment in research and innovation. the commissioners then announced, together with commissioner crețu, a seal of excellence to improve the quality of research funding. unfortunately, in my view, a juncker promise has remained too much, a hallmark and a manual, and we are still seeing too little of the promised increased impact in practice. the possibilities offered to companies and knowledge institutes are great on paper, but the practical implementation remains very difficult. for example, it remains difficult to combine the use of horizon 2020 and the european structural and investment funds to finance one large ambitious project. i would very much like to see the european commission take concrete steps to simplify this. i do not think that these measures should be devised overnight in a brussels office, but that they should be formulated together with practical experts. a little less from the top, a little more from the bottom. more synergy between horizon 2020 and other funds is not an end in itself, but it is important to make the best use of the money available in a tight budget. let us now look at this matter in the years ahead. let us ensure that investment in research and innovation leads to the greatest possible growth and jobs.
i thank you for the good examples you have given, but it can, no, it must be better with the synergy between horizon 2020 and the other funds and instruments. 90% of horizon 2020 investments will be in the old member states. 80% of the european structural and investment funds go to the new member states. it is a very important and necessary fund, but it is like two different worlds. it is not good that many young people are leaving the new member states and that rich regions are benefiting more where others are stagnating. europe is after all a balanced development and a good territorial cohesion. brexit showed more or less what the consequences of two separate worlds can be: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment in the event of a fire. these people turned their backs on such an eu. that is why we are now calling for a new innovation-driven design in the midterm review. strengthen the chain of knowledge, innovation in products and services across europe. it could be. we already have the esi funds with 60 billion euros for innovation and smart specialisation, as you say. this week robert madelin presented his "mission to innovate", an innovation pact, a new way of working together. our resolution and your idea for the european innovation council are timely for that adjustment.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, we are discussing the commission work programme at the most difficult moment in the history of european integration. the european union is disintegrating at a frightening speed. anti—eu forces are on the rise, demolishing what has been built up over decades by the post-war generations but, instead of offering an alternative vision for the future, they just jump ship. thank you, mr farage. against this this backdrop, the work programme of the commission must be more than a mere list of legislative proposals. it must offer the outlines of a new european union because the kneejerk reflex to the current anti-eu mood is to weaken europe even further, assuming that if we scrap a few more directives people will suddenly embrace the european project. but that is a dramatic mistake. people’s existential anxieties will not be addressed by eliminating rules on vacuum cleaners or the curve of cucumbers. people are anxious and insecure because, in just a few decades, the world has changed beyond recognition. globalisation, the digital revolution and urbanisation have radically changed the way that we live. our communities have become much more diverse with immigration and advancing gender equality. political and economic leadership is no longer the monopoly of middle-aged white alpha males. agriculture and industry have been overtaken by the knowledge economy and what was considered a very good cv 30 years ago no longer opens doors today. our old familiar world has gone. there is a 50-50 split in the population that is reflected in the electorate, not just in europe, but also in the united states and elsewhere. each side is terrified of the world view of the other and our task is to offer a plan for healing this rift. we must do justice to both. it is not just about a victory of one group over the other, it is about creating a society where both feel safe and at home. the kneejerk reflex of weakening the eu in response to the rise of anti-eu sentiments is the wrong one. it does not do justice to those who do want to grasp the opportunities the 21st century world offers, nor does it help in any way those who feel threatened by a new world. it is a mistake to think we can weaken the anti—eu populists by weakening the eu. we have to make the eu stronger, more democratic and more accountable, and more able to act and address the big challenges of today. the european parliament and the european commission have, by and large, understood this, but the national governments are tearing the european union apart like a bunch of political hooligans, using europe like a punchbag, and this must stop. i call on all national politicians to stop europe-bashing. stop the blame game. stop the lies. take your responsibility for europe. we all have a responsibility to offer a real prospect to all european citizens, a europe where everyone has a place, a place for all – young and old, city and countryside, men and women, people of all shapes and sizes and all walks of life – and that must be the message of the commission work programme.

rapporteur. – mr president, let me start by thanking my fellow rapporteur, pablo zalba, for the excellent work so far and i am also looking forward to working closely together in the coming months. this interim report is primarily procedural and there was a reason for it, because we only just started with our work. there is, however, one strong political point in it and that is that we call upon the commission to better and more swiftly cooperate with the work of our committee of inquiry. that was the case when we adopted this interim report in committee. i must say that since then the commission has considerably improved its cooperation, and i hope that we can continue that way of working. so far, our committee of inquiry has collected a lot of, well, let us say, technical evidence. we spoke with car manufacturers, producers of emission control technologies, software experts, academics, ngos. most of the technical part we have done so far, and we have just begun with the political part of our inquiry. we have had hearings with a whole bunch of commissioners starting with former commissioners dimas, potočnik, verheugen and tajani, and yesterday evening with the current commissioners bieńkowska and vella. very crucial also will be our focus on the member states; i am very much looking forward to the hearings with national ministers in the coming weeks, and i sincerely call upon those ministers who have not yet confirmed their attendance to do so. european democracy also means that national ministers are accountable for their position in europe. we will also hear the american epa and the national type approval authorities. what have we found so far? well, first of all, we of course discovered that this is much more than just the volkswagen case. we have seen that other car manufacturers are shutting down their emission abatement technology when the temperature goes below 17°c, or after 22 minutes. that comes close to the 20-minute test cycle, so we have seen that the problem is much bigger than just volkswagen and that is something that we have to explore further on. also we still have a lot of questions concerning the role of member states. we do see that there has been quite a weak implementation and enforcement of european legislation and that is something that we sincerely have to discuss very closely with the national ministers. another question that we are still digging into is why did it take so long to develop this new real driving emission test? in 2010 the commission work document said that it would take roughly two years for the technical exercise to develop this test; we all know that it took many many more years and that is something that we have to dig into as well. finally, it is one year since the scandal broke and since then we have done a lot, but there are still millions and millions of diesel cars on the european roads that do not comply with current legislation. that is something that we have discussed yesterday with commissioner bieńkowska and i am very happy with her strong approach now. we are awaiting, within weeks, some infringement procedures, but it is certainly something that we are still going to focus upon. also the recall of vehicles, that is still primarily based on a voluntary approach. i am very much looking forward to the coming months. we will conclude our report in march next year and i hope that we can draw not only conclusions about what happened in the past, but also some conclusions, some lessons for the future, because that might be the most important part of our inquiry.
mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for being here today. i think if you just look at what we learned after half a year of the committee of inquiry, what is very important is that all the technical experts say there is no technical reason at all to switch off any abatement technology. it is technically not needed for protecting engines or anything else. we know that a lot of, if not all, car manufacturers are using software to switch off these technologies. only volkswagen has been sentenced for using defeat devices, but fiat are using it, shutting it off after 22 minutes, and other car manufacturers are turning it off below 17°c. there is no reason for that. still, no other car manufacturer is being blamed for using defeat devices, and action by the commission is urgently needed. more importantly, as we speak, polluting cars are still driving on our streets. when is the commission going to coordinate a european recall to get those dirty cars off our streets?
the committee of inquiry, chairman, is unanimous halfway through the drive: the investigation is moving in the right direction and more information needs to be received from all concerned as quickly as possible. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. to name a few hot pursuers: how should the interconnectedness between the public authorities and the automobile industry be tackled? and those thousands of lobbyists? and how do we get really clean air? i think, mr. chairman, there's only one solution: zero-emission cars as soon as possible. there is no such thing as a clean diesel car. there are no clean gasoline cars. not with and not without rogue software. driving on electricity and hydrogen, that's the future. let the european union devote all its energy to this. clear the way for the zero-emission car.
rapporteur. – mr president, i believe that this was a very encouraging debate, a debate that was obviously not about the interim report, because that was limited to procedures, but much more about the current state of the investigation of our inquiry committee. why did i believe that it was so encouraging? because i noticed a very broadly shared common understanding of the problem that did not necessarily already reach commonly shared conclusions, but will be the challenge for my dear colleague, pablo zalba, and myself to get parliament behind shared conclusions in the end. but i do believe that we are on the right track. we should not forget that we are only half-way through our investigation. there is still a lot to be investigated and a lot of evidence to be found. i would like to highlight two elements. i think it was seb dance who very eloquently talked about the myriad of responsibilities. he said – and many others did as well – that there seems to be nobody really responsible in the end – not the commission, not a member state, not national authorities, etc. that might be the core of the problem that we are facing here. i am sure that, when we dig much deeper into that, we can also draw conclusions for the future, not only reflecting the car industry, but other policy fields as well. finally, i think it was my colleague ivo belet who said that, if we look at responsibilities, a lot has to do with the inability of the member states to take their responsibility. i believe that it is a very important thing that came today. many talked about naming and shaming, whether to do so or not. i believe that in politics in a well-functioning democracy political accountability is crucial, and that might mean naming and shaming if that is necessary. i am not afraid of it and i am not looking for it, but it is part of a strong democracy. that is what we are still building together in europe, so let us continue with that.

for the greens/efa group i did not realise how different the view of the present polish government was from that of the european commission until the prime minister of poland came to speak here in january. she made no distinction between the party, the government, the nation and the people. questions and comments on the new legislation, which restricts judges and silences journalists, were seen by her as criticism of the citizens of poland. which brings us to the following misunderstanding: european standards and values, european fundamental rights, concern the individual, sovereignty over his own body, sovereignty over his own thoughts, such as the right of women to dispose of their own bodies. i would like to say to the present polish government: you seem to have a different definition of law and sovereignty: the right and the sovereignty of the group, of the people. however, poland has been fighting hard for democracy in recent decades. you ended a totalitarian system. you took back the rights of the citizens. you became a democratic country in a union of democratic countries. and right now, you're going back down. where the law shifts from the individual to the group or the state, people fall between the cracks. for example, women, half the population. restricting the right to abortion forces women and girls to commit criminal acts. that is very unwise. and we all know that the number of abortions in a country is not about right, but about need. the green message to the polish government is therefore: you claim to be the heir of solidarnosc. then you cannot restrict the freedoms of women, of the law or of journalists. and to the polish people, i say: the european greens will continue to fight for your rights.
on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, after a summer in which the events in turkey were headline news all over europe, it is good to have a debate here with federica mogherini, high representative of the union for foreign affairs and security policy and vice-president of the commission, on the current situation in that country. it is a lot that the turkish population has to deal with: more than three million refugees, terrorist attacks on an almost daily basis and, on top of that, a bloody coup attempt on 15 july 2016. that night left a deep impression in turkish citizens’ minds. the government said that the temporary state of emergency would not influence people’s daily lives, but so far more than 100 000 people have been directly affected either by losing their jobs or by ending up in a prison cell. it is understandable that, in the aftermath of the coup, a number of measures had to be taken, but the current situation also leaves us very concerned. it is difficult to understand what the link is between respected journalists like asli erdogan and the coup plotters. it is difficult to understand what the link is between thousands of teachers and the coup plotters, and it is also difficult to understand how appointing trustees to municipalities and dismissing directly elected mayors is related to the events of 15 july. turkey can be proud that its citizens massively went out on the streets to defend their democracy that night. it can also be proud that all political forces joined hands in rejecting any illegal overthrow of the democratic institutions. turkish democracy does have the chance to come out of this situation strengthened: by embracing media plurality, which played a crucial role that night in informing the people; by working together with all political forces on much-needed constitutional reform; and, as a last point, by proving that the rule of law is the guiding principle in bringing the perpetrators to justice. on a final note, i would like to also highlight, on our part, the fact that cooperation with all the eu institutions on turkey is of great importance and it is important that we keep sending the same messages. blue-card answer. – yes, i think there is one lesson that we must learn: that although we have great concerns about certain developments in turkey, just shutting the door and thinking that if we do not speak with the government or address the issues, perhaps the next day things will be wonderful in turkey is not sufficient. i think we have to be realistic here: not talking with one another is not an option. when we talk, we have to do it respectfully. so, i think, yes, turkey is a valid partner to discuss things with and also a valid partner sometimes to raise our concerns with.
mr president, first of all, the relations between turkey and the european union and the member states should be governed by facts and not by all kinds of emotions. this means we should be clear that everybody who has a role in the eu, its institutions or the member states has denounced the coup d'état. there was no sympathy for it at all and there was a direct reaction. happily, today we see that both members of the opposition – of the democratic opposition in turkey – and also some government representatives have come to the conclusion that there was some overreaction, and that the process of dealing with the aftermath of the coup d'état and bringing to justice those who committed it, or are thought to be involved in it, should be governed by independent judges and by the rule of law. that is vital. secondly, turkey is an important neighbour of the european union, and is a nato partner, irrespective of the whole discussion about membership. this means that visa liberalisation should be subject to the criteria set. if they are met, liberalisation will follow. if not, it will take more time. no deadlines should be applied. the same goes for the membership issue. let us also come to the conclusion that the visa deal is on. it is good both for turkey and for the european union. the turkish government should do everything possible to prevent people who sympathise with erdogan or gülen taking to the streets in the netherlands and in germany: such activity should be stopped. and we should keep calm and have a constructive talk with turkey.
this morning i spoke by telephone with a turkish citizen about the situation in his country. he painted a sinister picture of a totally poisoned social climate. you would be accused of being a gülenist for whatever reason. then the prison gates will open for you. sadly and alarmingly, the same political accusation (of being a gülenist) is causing a great deal of unrest and division among the turkish diaspora in eu member states, including my country, the netherlands. because, as today's evidence shows: erdogan is influencing the turkish diaspora in europe in a way that has never been seen before. his main tool for this is the diyanet, the presidium for religious affairs. instead of the applicant country turkey implementing european political principles, the eu is now faced with the implementation of erdogan's islamist ideology on its own territory. and in passing, erdogan continued to push for visa-free travel for turkish citizens to the eu, probably with success. then erdogan is the hero of the home front and the diaspora. it is a good illustration of the failure of european policy towards turkey.
mr president, the despicable coup in turkey was rightly condemned and today we stand with the people of turkey, but i fear that the national unity we see will be short-lived. turkish democracy needs deepening and needs to be meaningful. yes, the perpetrators should be brought to justice but in fair trials and with proportionate measures, and that excludes the death penalty. let us be clear: the measures we now see after the failed coup and the anti-terror laws are being abused for disproportionate cleansing and silencing of critics. the rule of law was, is and will remain our group’s key priority. the fact that gülenists and the ak party together built an illiberal coalition for so long and the government now accuses the gülenists of this coup is a confirmation of a breakdown of accountability, checks and balances and transparency. we must work with turkey on security measures; trade and people-to-people relations are strong; but let us not narrow the relationship to the toxic migration deal or the crippled accession talks. turkey needs tough love. ‘friends tell each other the truth’ goes the old turkish saying, and we should remind them of that and build our relations on our own principles.
written. with nearly 60 million people worldwide on the move, it's good that the un is coming together to talk about the big challenges around global migration. but the great challenges call for ambitious solutions, which i regret that i do not see reflected in the provisional conclusions of the summit. the un countries cannot agree on a large-scale resettlement, so it is decided to discuss it again next year, but a solution is needed now for millions of people who are entitled to international protection. the european commission proposes to resettle 20 000 people a year in europe, but i would ask federica mogherini to adjust this ambition, and advocate resettling at least 100 000 vulnerable refugees a year. the eu must take the lead and fulfil its humanitarian duty by setting a good example, namely: the european union is also committed to the implementation of the un convention on the rights of persons with disabilities.
we all know the bulgarian truck driver who is on the road for months, eating his meals along the highway. we know the polish construction worker who works in a dutch construction pit and shares a room with ten others at night. and we also know that western european worker who sits at home unemployed and thinks, what are we going to do about it? social dumping takes many forms in europe, and unfair competition through secondment is one of them. as rapporteur, i want to work very hard to make this posting fairer in the future, but the european single market will not be social all at once if the posting directive is simply amended. competition between workers and firms too often creates a competitive battle with a race to the bottom. the strengthening of social rights very often goes against the dictates of the free market. so more needs to be done. that is why i am very pleased with the report by mr balas. fortunately, it's a very good start. i hope that the commission will indeed respond quickly to the proposals and come forward with proposals on mobile workers, pseudo-independence and social security fraud.
social dumping is a very real problem. there is no legal or political consensus on the definition of the term. but ask the dutch lorry driver who is unemployed at home or ask the portuguese concrete slab worker who is put to work in another member state under dubious circumstances. they can tell you very clearly what social dumping feels like. we need to do a lot better at enforcing existing rules. we need to implement new rules, such as the enforcement directive, much better and faster. we must quickly close existing loopholes in the legislation, such as the posting directive. we need to better equip labour inspectorates to operate and enforce across borders. but let's also be clear: we must not, under the guise of fighting social dumping, put into practice the unrealistic wishes of the socialist group in this house. i am thinking in particular of paragraph 49, for example, which calls on the commission to radically change the rate of income depending on work and to demand a basic income for all europeans. let us not pollute the debate on social dumping with proposals of this kind, but let us focus on realistic, efficient measures which actually benefit the hardworking european.
author. – mr president, it is a privilege to have, for the second evening in a row, a midnight debate with commissioner vella and i also welcome the council. let me start by thanking all my colleagues who were co-authors of this resolution. i think that we found a unique way of working together to come forward with a very coherent cross—party resolution. yesterday there was an article in the independent by the secretary of cites (the convention on international trade in endangered species), john scanlon, and the director of the united nations development programme (undp), helen clark, in which they stated that the upcoming cites conference is the most critical cites meeting in history. i think they are right. the poaching crisis has reached an unprecedented level and because of that there is huge pressure on the cites system. can we fix it? can we make sure that it will work in the future in a better way than it is doing now? for europe, this cop is quite unique as well because it is the first conference of the parties in which the eu is participating as an official partner party. that is, of course, a very important step. strangely enough, it has led to criticism from two sides: from third countries criticising the eu for wanting to have 28 votes with one voice, like the us – and i hope the commissioner can explain what the latest state of play is there – but also internally from our own member states. i understand that during the coreper meeting where this was discussed there was quite an intensive debate about which topics the eu should speak on with one voice, and which it should speak on with 28 voices. i hope that is not a precedent for the future, especially because i was present in 2010 at the cites cop in doha, and the eu contribution there was very ineffective. the commission, of course, had no speaking rights at that conference of the parties and the member states were very disorganised and not united. there was a common position but there did not seem to be any common strategy, and that was quite painful to see. so my question to the council is to ask whether it can explain how it is going to ensure that we are united and speaking with one voice and making the best of the fact that we have a common position there. i believe that the eu is ready for this conference of the parties. we finally have our eu action plan, which we discussed previously. parliament is now working on its reaction to that so i am looking forward to a strong european position. there again, i must say that what is lacking are strong commitments from the member states. let me come to the resolution. this resolution is different from previous resolutions on cites. i believe it is much more integral and holistic than ever before. we are not only talking about elephants, rhino or the psychedelic lizard: no, we are focusing on the main problem that we face in the world in this regard, namely wildlife crime – a growing threat to biodiversity and the environment, and also a global problem in terms of security, the rule of law, human rights and sustainable development, because we all know that those things go together. like the agenda of the conference of the parties, we are focusing on different issues than before, like demand reduction, sustainable livelihoods, combating corruption and effectiveness and implementation of cites. i must say that the success of these elements will really determine the future of cites, i would like to highlight five elements. first of all, how are the commission and the member states going to succeed in getting stronger enforcement of the cites rules? one of the main problems is that the rules are simply not enforced in the right way. that applies not only in countries where we might expect it to be so, but also in the eu itself, and paragraph 28 of our resolution is pretty clear. also within the eu we see widespread abuse of cites documents. secondly, corruption is a fundamental problem and the eu has tabled a resolution, together with senegal. my question is to ask whether you believe that this resolution will be successful in johannesburg and, if it were adopted, would it also be effective in practice? maybe you can elaborate on that. thirdly, regarding demand reduction, we see several proposals on the table. please tell us the latest state of play and how we can assist in reducing demand in many countries. my fourth point concerns the cites secretariat. everything that we want them to do puts more work on their shoulders but, if you look at their budget, in real terms it is now lower than it was 10 years ago. so the cites secretariat is simply not fit for the tasks that we give it, and official contributions are relatively small. so my question is to ask whether both the commission and the member states, on an individual basis, are willing to increase their contribution. that would not only help the secretariat but would also show commitment and trust in cites. finally, on the resolution on hunting trophies: this is important for us and it is also important to send a political message to the world that trophies should be subject to strict criteria, if allowed at all. but i understand that the usa and canada do not support the european resolution. so, do the commission and member states still foresee success in this field? to conclude, we need a high sense of urgency, unity and, please, a strong european common focus.

in writing. ‒ the pvda-euro delegation believes it is important that democracy and the rule of law are protected in all eu member states. we therefore support today's joint resolution on the situation in poland. the polish government is undermining fundamental principles of a functioning democratic state of law by, for example, restricting the independence of the constitutional court. this is contrary to the european treaties and we therefore support the european commission, which has launched legal proceedings against it. however, we cannot support the amendments tabled by the gue/ngl and the greens/efa groups, even though we agree in substance with the points raised, such as lgbt rights, women's rights and the protection of refugees. we believe that a european parliament resolution on the rule of law in a member state should be directed at laws actually adopted by the government. however, there are no legislative proposals in poland on these subjects. that is why we abstain on these amendments. we are of course following developments in poland closely in the field of abortion and minority rights. if the government adopts laws in this area that are contrary to european values, we will fully support any measures from the eu.

the european parliament has called on the commission to take the necessary steps to ensure that the eu's asylum policy is properly implemented. i won't play that game. to send asylum seekers who have been processed back to their country of origin, identity papers are required. the greens are also very sympathetic to this. it is logical to adapt a laissez-passer to modern times by adding a passport photo and other personal characteristics. the greens will nevertheless vote against this proposal. - why? - why? the european commission did not provide an impact assessment but it does make it appear that this will increase the return. the assumption that a modern identity card will persuade third countries to take back their citizens is not based on anything. many asylum seekers who have been processed end up stranded because their country doesn't want them back. there are even those who would like to return but cannot. we are also concerned that third countries which are much less democratic than we are will have access to the personal data of their formerly displaced citizens. that could put their citizens in danger. the number of refugees in the world is growing, and since the tunisian revolution the european commission's proposals have become increasingly repressive. if that's what we learned from the summer of 2015, we're on the wrong track.
i am glad that we can adopt this regulation tomorrow for a european travel document and that we are thus also showing the importance of an efficient return policy. the return policy is a crucial precondition for a functioning asylum and migration policy. but it is also a weak link in the european process. to illustrate this, i would like to read a passage from the european commission's website, which states: every year, between 400 000 and 500 000 foreign nationals are ordered to leave the eu because they have entered or are staying here irregularly. however, only 40% of them are sent back to their home country or to the country from which they have travelled to the eu. forty percent of the people who aren't allowed to stay here just go back. that means that 60 percent -- more than half, or between 240,000 and 300,000 migrants a year -- don't care about our rules, processes and laws and just stay put. some african countries have only a 16 percent return rate. this is a gigantic, gigantic problem. if we cannot do anything to make this return policy more efficient, to ensure that the rates of return are higher, it will be very difficult to maintain the support and the financial resources in the long term for the reception of refugees who really deserve our help. for let there be no misunderstanding about that: it is the real refugees who flee war and violence and seek protection in europe who are the victims.

the rapporteur. when i started working as rapporteur for this legal aid directive over a year and a half ago, i seriously considered that it would be many years before we had an agreement on it. this was because the legislation in question is at the intersection of criminal law and social security, and this is a sensitive area. i think the commission was very much aware of this, because the commission's original proposals really only extended to legal aid in the case of a european arrest warrant and to provisional legal aid in other cases. in a separate recommendation, the commission also included rules on, inter alia, the quality of legal aid. that was very modest. if we are to have real trust in each other's legal systems, then that includes trust in each other's legal aid systems. there must be no place in the european union for class justice. equal access to justice for all, regardless of income. the right to a lawyer was already regulated by the eu in a separate directive. it was then logical to me that in those cases where you have the right to a lawyer, you also have the right to legal aid, if at least you cannot afford one yourself. i also think it is important that certain aspects of the recommendation, in particular the quality of legal aid, should also be incorporated into the directive. the majority in the european parliament supported this approach and so we entered the negotiations with the council with an ambitious stance. the vote in the council was rather reserved. despite the good efforts of the luxembourg presidency, the differences between the council and parliament remained wide. it was thanks to the great effort of the subsequent dutch presidency that we were finally able to agree on a text that was stronger than the original commission proposals, even though parliament did not get its way on all points. the main advantage is that legal aid has been provided in ordinary criminal cases, not in all cases, but in cases of deprivation of liberty and in certain invasive investigative methods involving the accused himself. the right to legal aid is guaranteed when the suspect is questioned and, in the case of european arrest warrants, there is a right to legal aid in the executing member state, but also in the requesting member state, if necessary. in all cases, the main rule is that an independent body is responsible for adding a lawyer. only in emergencies does the police have a role to play. member states should also ensure that there is sufficient funding for a high-quality form of legal aid. is the european union going to change a lot now? this will vary from one member state to another, but all member states will now have to set up a high-quality legal aid system for people who cannot afford a lawyer. for some member states, as the impact assessment we ourselves as parliament have carried out has shown, this will mean a considerable financial investment. in any case, it means that there can be absolutely no question of the tendency of governments in other member states where legal aid is available, such as in the netherlands, to cut back on legal aid. a large part of the rights incorporated in the directive are derived from the case law of the european court of human rights. the directive also explicitly refers to important un instruments, such as the principles and guidelines on access to legal aid, as a guide for member states' implementation practices. this strengthens the power of all these international instruments and makes it easier to monitor their implementation. all in all, i'm satisfied with the end result. i am convinced that in practice it will benefit many people who are currently deprived of a good lawyer. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs and the commission and the council.
the european parliament has been asked to make a statement on the situation in the middle east. we are setting up a european minimum for legal aid and parliament - and i congratulate mr de jong on this - has finally joined the commission and the council. we have already had the right to a lawyer at the first interrogation enshrined in law, but what does that mean when you hear in a number of member states: yes, you have the right, but how you're going to pay for that right is a secondary issue. legal aid gives people with little money the opportunity to get themselves represented and that has made our rule of law a little better again. we also see some improvements in the implementation of the european arrest warrant. you now have the right to a lawyer in the country where you are arrested, but unfortunately not in all cases in the country where you are sent. yet this is a considerable improvement. we see too many cases of civilians being handed over for a minor offence or on the basis of a weak suspicion. and where you're at home in your own country, you can wait for a trial by just working through it and eventually get a call to report, you're put on probation in another country. we know that pre-trial detention is not carried out in the same way in all european countries. you're put on probation, and then you say goodbye to your job, your life, and maybe your relationship, and sometimes you say goodbye to your health. so yes, the greens are happy with this step, and now the next steps. i want to warn you of two things: further harmonization of criminal law must not take place at a minimum level of protection, because this would undermine national achievements. and a second point is: don't let money be the guiding principle. the cost of good protection of suspects and victims is often used as an excuse to avoid improving procedural rights. it is precisely at a time when european member states are being alarmed by terrorist attacks that we need a well-functioning criminal justice system. so we need to harmonize procedural rights at a good level. because if we don't, we risk the guilty getting released on administrative grounds and the innocent languishing in pretrial detention. (the president declared the common position approved as amended)
blue-card answer. – i am always happy to give a colleague the space to make a point, but let me try to understand what you are saying. i think what we have here are minimum standards. what i am pleading for is not to lower our standards to a bare minimum. that is one thing. secondly, yes, legal aid is costly in countries that do not yet have legal aid, but it is much more costly for society to put the wrong people behind bars. so i would say that, in a european union that looks across the border to find criminals, we need to look across the border to provide people with legal aid.
the rapporteur. i think the debate showed that there is a great deal of agreement in parliament on the need for this directive. i would also like to thank the commissioner for her commitment to the beginning of the road we have travelled with the council. during the negotiations, its representatives certainly contributed at the crucial moments. the work is not done. we shall now have to see - the council is not here - what the member states are going to do about it. because you're going to be relying on good information about what's going on in practice to gather information. is it being done properly? is that quality really being monitored or are there going to be further cuts in some member states in the social security system, in legal aid? that requires an active commitment from the commission, an active commitment from parliament and also an active commitment from ngos that are fortunate enough to be there, such as fair trials international and others who are monitoring this. then for the future. there was one comment from the commissioner which i found a little startling, because it gave the impression of: now we're done with the criminal justice side of things. i think there is at least one area, but there may be several areas where we really need to go, and that is the area of pre-trial detention, of pretrial detention and the circumstances of it. i hear too many voices of people who have been transferred to another member state and then find themselves in rather humiliating situations. so i wholeheartedly support the call of a number of ngos and also of a group in parliament who say: now we must proceed with the following proposals. the route map is far from complete. i also count on the commissioner to give further details of the point in particular.
rapporteur. – mr president, the cross-party collaboration and the successful outcome of the negotiations with the commission and council sent three strong messages today: europe delivers, and it is stepping up its efforts to curb the trade and promotion of torture and death-penalty goods; trade policy helps strengthen human rights; and the european parliament played a decisive role in achieving this important upgrade and update of the legislation. so i would like to thank very much the shadow rapporteurs, as well as everyone in this house, for working towards the biggest majority we have seen on a trade-related issue in the current parliamentary term. this strong eu position and ep position allowed us to improve key points, despite initial reluctance in council. i am glad that advertising online for banned goods, and also trade fairs, will be illegal. this extends the ban on services facilitating this dreadful market of torture and death-penalty goods and services. i am glad we included the ban on transit via the eu for prohibited goods and that, in addition, knowledge-based transit bans will be in place for multipurpose goods. we also added the urgency procedure, which allows for rapid bans or controls on new products, and, to make the rules future-proof, we expanded the reach of the urgency procedure to cover all annexes to the legislation, thus enabling the commission swiftly to add new items to annexed lists of banned or controlled items if this is necessary. we will add law-enforcement standards, so that, when the competent authorities are looking at the supply of technical systems related to annex 3 goods, they must see that there is a provision promoting law-enforcement standards that respect human rights and contribute to the prevention of torture. another key element is the inclusion of several steps for better sharing of information. the updated agreement covers the possibility of the competent authorities sharing information with the national preventive mechanisms established under the optional protocol to the un convention against torture. an anti-torture coordination group will monitor the application of the rules, with parliament’s experts attending meetings on the preparation of delegated acts and the updating of annexes. we have made this eu regulation a baseline, and there is room for national measures. member states are permitted to adopt or maintain additional national measures that restrict certain ancillary services, such as transportation, financial services, insurance, advertising or promotion in relation to annex 2 goods. and, in order to keep the door open for possible further strengthening, we have asked that the commission, when reviewing the rules at the end of july 2020, should assess whether they should also include activities of eu nationals abroad. this is something that we in parliament requested, and i hope it can be included in the review. this whole update would not have been possible without the active involvement of civil society and of a number of people in the eu who care deeply about upgrading and updating our human rights record, and i would like explicitly to thank them as well. i would emphasise that we want to make sure we practise what we preach in europe, and that we have to make sure that all foreign policy instruments, including for trade, help to address the world’s most pressing human rights issues.
rapporteur. – madam president, i would like to thank everyone for their contributions, including commissioner malmström for her leadership across the board in her groups at the commission. together we have closed most loopholes, and we must ensure solid monitoring at the member-state level. it is indeed, as the commissioner said, hard to imagine the kinds of products and services that are offered at trade fairs and in online or offline catalogues. this must end. today’s update shows the eu leads in raising the bar globally to take concrete steps to end the death penalty and torture. the increase of torture and the death penalty call for such measures, from iran to saudi arabia or egypt, from china to the united states. we must push back and challenge these kinds of human rights abuses. in fact, we need more eu-wide leadership to push back against those who challenge the universality of human rights. today’s trade measures – and i hope they will be adopted with a large majority – will of course not in of themselves end the death penalty or torture. so we must see this step in sync with other measures and instruments the european union has and must use. we have to keep in mind when we do our work those people who are suffering from repression, violence, torture and the death penalty, often at the hands of their own governments. they must find in the european union an advocate for their freedom. today’s regulation is an important element to give meaning to the european union’s human rights agenda, and i hope for all of your support.

i have asked for this debate, mr president, because the situation in calais is completely untenable for everyone, for refugees, for local residents and for drivers. i held a meeting with my esteemed colleague, mr dennis de jong, in rotterdam last year and we asked dozens of drivers about their experiences there. there's a lot of guys in between, but a lot of them were scared to death to drive to calais, because drivers are threatened at calais. they get spat on, they get beaten. many loads of fruit and vegetables disappeared in waste containers after refugees pooped and peed on them. and then the driver is also fined €1,000 for having the stolen goods in his car. so we are talking about a european problem and the european commission must deal with it. not by sending a few letters to governments but by organizing a summit in a few weeks with all concerned to find a solution. then it's about the refugees, selection at the gate, resettlement of real refugees. that must be done now and at calais france and britain must work together to find a solution. take those refugees there. france must close the camp and ensure that no new camps are built along the channel, plus secure parking spaces, no more fines for honest drivers and a quick passage through customs. it is time to stop talking about this for years, because how many french ministers have been to calais and said: yes, it's very bad, we're going to end it now. and now mr hollande again. it doesn't work like that. so mr avramopoulos, it's up to you now to show action and not talk. we in rotterdam say, not words but deeds. announce results, not in the spring of 2017 but before january, and realize that by holding a summit with all stakeholders. i am not talking about words, commissioner, but action!
the situation in calais has become untenable. it is not sustainable for the thousands of refugees, it is not sustainable for the local population and it is not sustainable for the transport sector. i support the french government's plan to dismantle the jungle, but that does not mean a structural solution. to prevent new jungles in europe, we need a better european asylum and migration policy, european countries, member states, and european agreements that are implemented faithfully and quickly. solidarity is also needed. in this context, in the light of solidarity, i would ask the british government to take part in the reception of refugees from calais, and i am thinking in particular of the unaccompanied children who have family in the united kingdom. all member states, including the united kingdom, have a responsibility. then take the responsibility.
this debate comes as the french president has announced that he will close the camp before the end of the year. he said: i'm going to do it in a humane way so that the refugees can apply for asylum in a decent way. we shall of course keep him on his toes. but waiting until the end of the year is no longer possible for many drivers. i am surprised that the commission says: wait and see, early next spring there will be a letter from the commission on the transport situation. i think we should ensure that drivers, as long as their safety is at stake, can drive to calais by other routes without their bosses calling them on it. i don't think that ensuring your safety is a refusal to work. all drivers have the right to a safe journey, just as asylum seekers have the right to a safe procedure. i ask the commission to be more ambitious in protecting the very workers who are affected by the unsafe situation at calais.
it 's been said before: europe is emotion. and emotion can be experienced by traveling. that is the essence of the proposal we are discussing this afternoon. i am therefore grateful to mr weber for combining the ideas of many people in the state of the union. why should the nationalists and the populists be allowed to shout everything and we be allowed to say nothing? we are doing something about that today. it's got to be normal people. all those erasmus people will be there, but the middle management of europe must travel around to see what europe is. this plan is good for mobility. i agree with mrs harms: out of the planes, into the trains. that's good for the environment and good for mobility. the epp never puts a plan on the table without money. that, of course, is the crux of this debate: where are we gonna get the money? we shall find a solution in cooperation with the commission. but the railway companies are getting new customers, and these could be customers for life. so they can contribute a little bit. another limitation is that the card must be applied for. we're not gonna be giving out free cards all over europe. it would be most welcome if mr weber were sent a report on the trip afterwards. then i'm sure his e-mail box is completely full.

the european council has again put the issue of migration on its agenda, and rightly so. migration is tearing apart the member states. it destroys our culture, our prosperity and our physical security. thousands of jihadists have come with the migration. these soldiers of allah are waiting like sleeper cells for the signal to launch devastating attacks in the eu. it is now also clear that these terrorist units have weapons of war. the european council must listen to viktor orbán. he recognizes the danger and he deserves our full support and imitation. therefore, the eu and the member states should take the following effective measures: frontex must stop being a ferry service to pick up jihadists and other migrants in north africa, the internal and external borders must be completely closed to this invasion and the invaded settlers must be returned without pardon.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on international trade. – madam president, together with shadow rapporteurs from the committee on international trade – and i would like to thank them – we focused on the trade and investment aspects of eu-iran relations one year after the important jcpoa, otherwise known as the iran nuclear deal, which of course must be implemented. since then, several delegations travelled to iran to explore the potential, which in theory is huge. but both the iranian people and eu companies learned that doing business, in practice, in iran is not only a matter of lifting sanctions, and it is not as easy as it seems. the high levels of corruption, the grey economy, as well as terror financing but also the lack of a truly private sector, with up to 70% state-controlled, make it difficult to do business, and trade and investment must of course be done according to the law and benefit the population, which has suffered enough and continues to suffer. the human capital is iran’s most valuable asset, so imagine what is possible when development goes hand in hand with human rights and fundamental freedoms, when labour rights are improved – including for women – and when environmental protection is upgraded. we must bring iran under the rules-based system according to the financial action task force against money laundering and where possible explore the wto path.

thank you, president. the eu lists the military wing of hezbollah as a terror group. iran supports it. the emphasis on these key topics was lacking in the report, and we found it important to strongly and unequivocally condemn human rights violations and the damaging role that the islamic republic plays in the region. now this is not, mr howitt, an attack on the nuclear deal, its implementation or you personally. it is merely a matter of differences of opinion and priorities, which i think is entirely normal in a democracy. my group calls on iran to be a part of the solution to the war in syria and – as we are also asking of saudi arabia – to halt interference in the affairs of neighbouring partners, recognise israel and stop denying the holocaust. we should work together where possible on the basis of clear benchmarks, and we need an eu delegation on the ground. our group sees opportunity to cooperate on environmental protection, on which what little civil society remains in iran is bravely pushing for more action. we believe that people-to-people contact through exchanges is key, but people must be able to return safely to iran when they have dual nationality. people-to-people connections also take place online. therefore, the emergence of a free and open internet in iran, one that is not nationally and centrally monitored and censored, is key for young people likewise to come out of isolation. i want to stress that it is time for a real step out of the dark, out of isolation, and that includes free and fair elections. our group seeks to balance this report and hopes that our key amendments will be adopted: otherwise, unfortunately, we will not be able to support this resolution.
