in writing. slowly but surely, we have seen the priorities of european foreign policy shift in recent months. whereas the protection of human rights and the strengthening of the rule of law are important principles in our cooperation with third countries, the focus now seems to be shifting more and more towards the prevention of migration. i therefore call on the commission and our foreign ministers not only to look at our foreign policy through anti-migration glasses but to show solidarity with the countries in the region which have taken in millions of refugees. for example, europe must fulfil its humanitarian duty by providing the most vulnerable refugees in those countries with a safe and legal route to the eu through large-scale resettlement. not as a reward for the country concerned, but out of the bitter necessity.
rapporteur. – madam president, i am very pleased that we are having this debate here today because we have travelled quite a road since we decided a year and a half ago to draw up this legislative initiative report. first of all, i would like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs and my co-rapporteur on the committee on constitutional affairs (afco). these are not just hollow words; i think that we have had a very fruitful and constructive cooperation, and the result of that cooperation is before you and will, hopefully, command a large majority when we vote later today. i note that the only party that is actively campaigning against my report is the front national, which i find reassuring in a way. the european commission will today present its working programme for 2017. commissioner timmermans always says that the european commission has to be big on big things, and i very much agree with that. but i do think that democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights are a big thing. we europeans have something to be proud of: we have been leading for many decades in the area of democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights, so we have a heritage that we can be proud of. so let us now take the opportunity to further deepen and strengthen that culture of democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights in the european union as a whole, because the eu is more than the sum of member states. democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights (or drf, if i may coin a new abbreviation in eu jargon) is key to the functioning of the european union. it is key to policy areas like police and justice cooperation, and asylum and refugee policies, but also the internal market and, as we have seen recently, international trade. we are all aware of the so-called copenhagen dilemma. in other words, when a country wants to join the european union, it has to meet the highest standards – but member states of the european union cannot be held to those standards. we do not really have sufficient means to enforce our standards of democracy, the rule of law and fundamental rights, but we do feel that the need for a mechanism for upholding drf is growing. one of our colleagues, viviane reding, who was a commissioner at the time for that purpose, took the first steps to the creation of the rule of law framework, which is now triggered by mr timmermans. we have seen the creation of the rule of law dialogue in the council, the creation of the friends of the rule of law group in the european council, and mr timmermans, when you were still a minister of foreign affairs in the netherlands, you yourself called for the creation of a rule of law mechanism. we have the european commission rule of law framework, and that is very important. i think it is very good that you have triggered it in the case of poland, but that same case also illustrates the weaknesses and the shortcomings of that mechanism, and therefore the european parliament has proposed the drf pact. the principles, i hope, will find their way into the proposals that the commission will, hopefully, put forward. the principles are that the pact, or the mechanism, should be applied to all member states equally. they should all be assessed on the same basis, and also the eu institutions. it should be a permanent mechanism and not crisis-driven, as we have now in the case of poland. there should be ongoing monitoring. it should be objective, evidence-based, and not politically motivated, and it should also be building on existing instruments and be realised under the current treaties. finally, our mechanism foresees close involvement of the national parliaments. i am very much looking forward to the debate, and i very much hope that the european commission will join the big majority in the european parliament and come forward with proposals that build on the report that we will adopt today.
mr president, like you and most of my colleagues, i received this letter last week: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. the letter concludes that the vote will be published this afternoon in our own member states. this is undoubtedly intended as a kind of threat, but it is a nice piece of transparency which the anonymous letter writer could have taken as an example. i shall be very happy to explain in my own member state why it is so important that fundamental rights in the eu are made available for discussion in a structured, objective, constructive and balanced way and why they must be taken out of the scare-mongering and taboo sphere of this writer. let 's face it . if we as the european union are to play the role of champions of fundamental freedoms on the world stage and in our immediate neighbourhood, we must first of all take care of our own affairs.
rapporteur. – mr president, i would like to thank my colleagues for a very interesting debate and for all the support. i would just like to briefly say to that side of the house (which has largely disappeared incidentally): i have heard a lot of hysterical language about dictatorships and imposing values, tyranny, submission, treason. i would just like to point out that 28 countries decided to join the european union voluntarily. they signed up to the treaties voluntarily and the treaties include article 2. for those who have read the articles: article 1 in the treaties basically says ‘hello, everybody’ and then article 2 is about what we are all about as a european union – a community of values. the irony is that we have endowed the european union with the instruments to enforce all the other policy areas: competition policies, police and justice cooperation, foreign policies, etc. but our core values are not protected by instruments that are sufficiently strong to make sure that the values are upheld throughout the european union. i very much concur with what commissioner timmermans just said: it is all about the objectives and not so much about the means. but i do hope that i misunderstood when i thought i heard you say in your initial presentation, commissioner timmermans, that you think that the existing tools are sufficient, because there i disagree and i think that has been echoed by everybody in the house. i think that we all expect that the mechanism you have triggered against the polish government is not, unfortunately, going to lead to an improvement in the situation in poland. nobody expects that by the end of this week the polish government is going to say: we are going to follow up on your recommendations. i would have hoped that they would, but they are probably not going to do that. so we do need a mechanism that has a preventative arm that applies to all the member states. i think that is really a key principle. also, in response to your question: yes, i do think it should be on a regular, preferably an annual, basis and not be incident-driven, because if it is incident-driven then there is the inherent risk that decisions to intervene will be politically motivated, and we want to avoid that. one final remark about the panel of independent experts: i hear people expressing concerns about this. i would recommend people read the text because it is very clear that the lead is with the commission. the commission will adopt the annual report but it will do so in consultation with the panel of independent experts that will be partly nominated by the member states and partly by this parliament. finally, i would like to remind the commission, and colleagues, that it is not as if we are reinventing the wheel. we already have the justice scoreboard, the corruption report, the corruption and verification mechanism (cvm) for bulgaria and romania, the media pluralism monitor, the fundamental rights report, the reports on access to documents, the schengen evaluations, etc. these mechanisms also include panels of independent experts and country-specific recommendations, so we are not inventing something new. i look forward to the commission’s proposal. i hope that we can have a constructive dialogue, as we have had inside this house in the political groups, the same dialogue between the institutions and come up with something that will really serve democracy, the rule of law, and fundamental rights throughout the european union.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his update. commissioner, i very much welcome your intention of giving us a monthly update. on the evaluation, i also very much appreciate your emphasis on the need for evaluation of the implementation, and indeed the pnr case demonstrates that. although i personally voted against the directive, i think it is extremely silly to pass laws which are not then implemented. i also wonder where the rush came from, if now the member states are not acting six months after the adoption. i think we should also get a bit more information about the – in total now – eur 130 million which are being spent on pnr. but we should not only evaluate the implementation but also the effectiveness. if you compare it to the war on drugs, that has failed dramatically. is the war on terror any more successful? i do not know, because we have passed so many laws and spent so much money, and the threat levels seem to be going up, not down. we seem to get less of a grip on the phenomenon, not more. we should also evaluate the impact on fundamental rights and the rule of law, as you have mentioned. but i would also like to see included in your monthly updates the need for democratic and judicial oversight and more transparency and accountability, because i think that is sorely lacking. while defending our democracy and rule of law against terrorist attacks, we seem to be throwing the baby out with the bathwater. so i really hope that you are going to focus more on that. three final remarks. first of all, on prevention: i also welcome your statements on that, because i think that so far we have done a terrible job on prevention. i think we have made matters worse by creating deep divisions in society. secondly, we all agree on the urgent need for more information-sharing. but, as i said in the previous debate, information-sharing is a lot more than just interconnectivity. that is the technical side of things, but we now need to actually share information, and not on a voluntary, ad hoc, random basis, but systematically. finally, in the report that this house adopted in 2011, we stressed already very heavily the need to invest a lot more in what they call humint – human intelligence, people on the ground. i think that the situation in belgium and the investigation that they did has shown that the real problem is insufficient capacity. so, rather than spending zillions on megalomaniac it systems, we should be investing in people on the ground.

in writing. ‒ the european commission considers that supplementary legislation on a subject falling within the competence of the union automatically meets the subsidiarity test. the pvv disagrees with this, as do the parliaments of sweden and lithuania. the pvv therefore voted against.
in writing. ‒ the european institutions are spending even more on their own security, while in no way taking seriously the growing insecurity of open borders or acknowledging that islam is the inspiration for modern day terrorism. it is only right that the institutions should be subjected to the same insecurity as the citizen on the street. the pvv voted against this budget.
in writing. ‒ although i voted in favour of this report, i do not support the proposals concerning the office of the european prosecutor. nor do i support the call for mandatory rules establishing that convicted persons should not be eligible to stand for election or to work in or for the public administration. this type of measure should be seen as an additional sanction, normally only imposed for a limited time by the court. otherwise, the convicted persons are punished twice which is a violation of the ‘ne bis in idem’ principle.
in writing. – although i voted in favour of this report, i do not support the proposals concerning the office of the european prosecutor. nor do i support the call for mandatory rules establishing that convicted persons should not be eligible to stand for election or to work in or for the public administration. this type of measure should be seen as an additional sanction, normally only imposed for a limited time by the court. otherwise, convicted persons are punished twice, which is a violation of the ‘ne bis in idem’ principle.
in writing. ‒ the pvda delegation abstained from voting on the report on 'human rights and migration in third countries'. we are of course in favour of protecting human rights worldwide, and eu migration policy must not be in conflict with international and european law, such as the 1951 refugee convention. however, this report is inconsistent because it does not seem to differentiate between migrants and refugees. we think this distinction is important. in order to monitor support for the protection of refugees driven from their home countries by war, violence and persecution it is important that there is also an effective return policy for migrants who do not have the right to asylum, and especially those who have come to europe for economic reasons. this lack of nuance has made it impossible for us to agree to this report. we are in favour of the call to take human rights into account in migration policy, so the most obvious choice was to abstain.

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i was much more privileged than that because i received it 13 minutes before the start of the debate, so i had much more time than you did to prepare for this debate. i would like to start by noting that the council, except for the honourable gentleman here, seems to be absent for the debate, although you refer specifically to the bratislava declaration in the document on the priorities, so i think it would have been proper for the council to be present in greater numbers. i always find this kind of debate very difficult because we talk about matters or issues that we all find very important, but for some reason i get the feeling that most citizens do not really care that much. i commend the european commission for trying to cut red tape, focus on the essential, etc., but i wonder if that is really what citizens are lying awake over. repealing a law for energy labels for vacuum cleaners, fine, but is that really what is keeping people awake at night? i think that people are terribly concerned about the way that the european union is working and one of the things, i think, that should be at the top of the list is transparency and accountability. you mention it in the document and yet i know that in practice, when we ask for documents, when we want to get information – i am not talking about you personally, but within the commission, the council, and yes, the european parliament too – there is still a culture of confidentiality and secrecy. if we really want to regain the trust of citizens, that is something that we should address and should, i think, really put at the top of our list. secondly, better law-making, in my view, is not only about cutting red tape and repealing legislation that is considered unnecessary. it also means passing legislation that will stand up in court. now we have seen safe harbours and data retention scrapped, and as regards pnr and canada we do not know. we see that the council is now making all kinds of agreements with turkey and other countries, and these seem to have no basis whatsoever in the treaties. we do not know who anyone is accountable to; we do not know who is exercising oversight. that is not better law-making: it is terrible law-making. it is neither democratic nor transparent nor accountable. so if we really want to regain trust, those are the issues that we should be focusing on, and that includes ourselves: we can be critical of ourselves rather about subject headings.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for giving an overview of the commission work programme for next year. indeed, i also only received it 30 minutes before 3pm so i also had the privilege of a long reading time of more or less half an hour now. looking at what is on the table and what first vice-president timmermans was explaining to us in plenary, as to how we are going about things, i think we should just scrap this self-congratulatory tone. we are in a serious situation and still there are not so many people who feel involved and feel that the european union is there on their behalf. i think that is still our biggest concern, for us all. we want a european union that works for our citizens, for our consumers, and i think a lot of things need to be improved there. that is also why i get a bit tired when people say we have to focus on the big things: europe should be big on the big things and small on the small things. well let’s just conclude today that we all agree on that. the political discussion is what you call big, and what i call big, or what someone else calls small, which i call big. that is a political discussion. let’s not hide as if a commission work programme is a technical analysis. this is about priorities and political decisions, on where the european union is focusing, and there are good things. if i look at what the commission wants to do on the social pillar side, i think that looks good, but, how exactly is that going to look? here i would like to subscribe to what my colleague from the s&d group was saying. on the other and, if i look at the trade part, it is just business as usual, it is just continuing: ‘we are doing a great job so we continue’. well sorry, but maybe some people have noticed that there is a huge debate on which direction the eu is going with its trade deals. we are not discussing trade deals more. ceta stands for comprehensive economic and trade agreement. it is more than just a trade deal. that is why you are now having discussions with national parliaments. that is why there is now such a huge discussion, and the commission is saying, we will just continue with it because it is working fine, which is just turning a blind eye to what is going on outside. i think that should be mentioned far more in the work programme. the last point is on refit and this is a question to the commissioner. i know there will be some discussions also on ecodesign and i know that you are a very good person who can convince people, so can you convince people also within your commission how important it is to reduce our energy use? that is a big thing. with the vacuum cleaners we have saved energy equivalent to four coal-fired power plants. that is good for the environment, that is good for our economy, and it is good for our self-reliance on energy. that is what the ecodesign directive is about and i am sure that you can convey that message, rather than what a lot of tabloids are doing in portraying this as a regulatory burden, because i as a consumer do not mind what the rules are, but if in the end if i buy appliances that are much more energy efficient that is a good thing for us citizens.
mr timmermans is familiar with the cry 'without transport everything stands still'. i'm thanking him for the piece that i was able to scan quickly. much attention is rightly being paid to the decarbonisation of the transport sector. we are aware of mrs bulc's proposals. these are now included in the work programme. we as the european parliament will be working very hard on this. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a full answer to this question. i would therefore like to draw attention to the general economic significance of the transport sector, the social issues - there is a lot to be done about social dumping in the transport sector - and border control for the report of this meeting. schengen is more difficult to transport. the calais problem will, if it is to be resolved, be reasonably settled with the french government this month. but the transport sector is also a major source of innovation, and in particular of competition with the americans in the field of the self-driving car and connected cars. these are all issues that need to be addressed here. finally, the imo and the iko. but we will discuss that further with the trade union commissioner.
the juncker commission came up with the idea of the social pillar during the course of this year. that social aaa rating is indeed a good and important principle, especially in this day and age. but if we look at the commission's work programme, we do not find much of it. it remains with brief references to possible initiatives. but the idea was to anchor our social model in the european acquis and to guarantee that there would be social security for all europeans and that all europeans would also be entitled to a living income in all member states. there is a dutch expression: to promise much and give little makes the fool live in joy. i think we should prevent that. let us make good on that aaa promise to the people of europe. that is possible, but the commission will have to come up with concrete proposals for the social pillar.
every parliament in the world has a comment on the general budget every year. depending on your political colour and your degree of blindness to reality, you have a comment or two. because the result is disappointing, or it's not quite what was intended, or there's a better use of the money. but there is one exception in the world, and that is the european parliament. this parliament has decided in committee that no expenditure can be cut! everything seems to be perfect, nothing is wasted. so no more touching! that, mr president, is a sign of the mental laziness of a committee on budgets which does not do its job and of the conviction that everything the eu does is well done. and of course, a widely held idea in this parliament that taxpayers' money is free. mr president, that money is not free. ordinary people have to work their asses off for it before it gets taken away. the eu is wasting money like water and acknowledging that is a first step. but that is a step which this parliament cannot seem to take.
mr president, mr vice-president of the commission, minister: let me make one point. you brought back the backlog from eur 25 billion to some eur 3.9 billion now. that is an enormous success, but my reflection today is that solving is one thing, but prevention is another. this is what brought us in the committee on regional development (regi) to an initiative to look deeper now, because now we have the first results of the partnership agreements and the new programme and we can see exactly what will happen in 2018, 2019 and 2020. we cannot go back to the member states and say that they have to pay more money in later years. now you are getting money back from the decommitments, and the net payers book in this money. so getting interest from our citizens needs a sustainable approach, and that is why a resolution is now being prepared in the committee on regional development which will be brought to the plenary at the end of this year or the beginning next year. i would like to invite the commissioner to come and debate in depth what is really happening. it is a late start, but the programmes will be there and the bills will be there. i thank you very much if you want to accept this invitation for this investment policy – one third of the eu budget: cohesion.
the eu budget will be higher in 2017 than in previous years. this time it's up 4.4%. the institutions are invoking new priorities, including the need to boost the economy with public spending, cleverly labelled as investment. the migration crisis is also a problem which, understandably, requires sufficient resources. but does prioritising not automatically lead to a higher budget? is it not reasonable to reduce non-priority expenditure? the european court of auditors has advocated this thematic concentration. we find very little of that in the budget. the european commission, like most eu institutions, has implemented the agreed staff reduction of 1% per year. the european parliament pretends to abide by this, but makes an exception for various categories of staff which are growing in size. the fact that the 3.4% increase in the budget is covered up by the veiled language in the resolution does not give a good idea of how the majority thinks it can be packaged. unfortunately, such tricks give the impression that the eu institutions are not prepared to make reforms that citizens can identify with. something the british have no doubt come across in the past year. the union must not maintain the image of being irreformable if it is to be forward-looking.
a deal is a deal and rules are rules. if we agree that we are going to put our books in order and that we need structural reforms to compete globally, then we all have to do that. if member states do not comply with this, it is bad for the european support, but especially for those countries themselves, because you are actually passing the bill on to your children. in that sense i agree with what commissioner dombrovskis said about investment. but i do want to give you a heads up: investing is different from spending more. i have recently seen, for example, a member state buying extra trains which it does not really need, just to keep a company in distress in its own country running. that's not an investment to me. that is simply more spending and perhaps even a form of state aid. i would therefore urge the commissioner to continue to look very critically at everything that is understood by investment.
i am speaking on behalf of the alde group. first of all, i would like to thank the rapporteur and my fellow shadow rapporteurs for the constructive work we have done in the european parliament. but i would also like to make a brief statement on our position as the alde group, because trade brings prosperity but is under pressure in every possible way. the debate is conducted with a lot of rhetoric, without looking at the content. since the 1970s we have been assessing the risks involved in importing plants from third countries. we call that an open import system. this is a healthy balance between protecting against plant diseases and protecting our important commercial interests. as early as may 2013, the european commission wanted to address weaknesses within the current system without jeopardising the open import system. the european parliament wanted the same thing in april 2014. but the outcome of the negotiations now puts this in jeopardy: strict rules and vaguely worded criteria allow the prohibition of the import of plants and plant products from third countries without a sufficient risk assessment. in other words: we can be left to the arbitrariness of member states which block imports for political reasons. this is unacceptable! that is why i have tabled an amendment on behalf of the liberals in the european parliament to reject this agreement. traders in ports of import such as schiphol and antwerp will also face administrative burdens and higher costs. unfortunately, tight borders will not keep plant diseases out of the eu completely, nor will they prevent them.
i also speak for my colleague werner kuhn. as politicians, it's our job to take care of the people. so that also means that we are working on people's health. most citizens eat far too much of the wrong kind of fat, the so-called industrial trans fats from, say, cookies. the consequences are clear: the risk of developing heart disease is increased by the risk of developing obesity, diabetes, cardiovascular disease. the european commission and the food industry must therefore act quickly: a limit of 2%. i should now like to warn against the attempts by some groups in this parliament to denigrate natural, animal trans fats from normal products such as butter and milk. you're wrong. in fact, recent research has found that some trans fats in meat and dairy, as well as in human breast milk, have beneficial medical properties. american nutritionists and the world health organization confirmed this in 2015. of course, everything in moderation and in natural proportions, including our healthy european cheeses and meat products. healthy nutrition in a proper diet with the knowledge of today and the experience of yesterday. that is why i shall be voting against paragraphs d and e tomorrow, as will probably the whole cda group, because they say that natural fats are just as dangerous as industrial fats. and, commissioner, you don't want that to happen, do you?
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, there seems to be a recurring theme in all the interventions, and that is the total inability of the council to take any kind of decision up to now, but i will come back to that. first, on ceta: the alde group wants ceta to be salvaged, and if more time is needed then more time is needed. it is not the date, but the result, that matters. however, we cannot unpick the work that has been done, because trade is essential for our prosperity, and the credibility of the european union – as has been said by others – has been severely damaged. the winners of this episode are not the walloon farmers or the walloon entrepreneurs; the winners are china, russia and other countries, because they will get to set the standards, not europe. some parties in this house are actually welcoming the veto by wallonia because they are against ceta, but that is a very dangerous kind of opportunism. welcoming a veto when it suits your purpose will boomerang, because tomorrow a veto will be used against your priorities. the ceta episode exposes a much bigger problem, as i said: the weakness of the council. the eu cannot continue like this. if we look at the list of topics on the table, we have: sanctions against russia – blocked; the ukraine agreement – jeopardised; ceta – blocked; refugee relocation – blown out of the water; the decision on article 50 with the eu – kept dangling for nine months, and now one of the countries is threatening to veto the budget. we cannot continue like this. the eu is like a three-engine plane with one engine completely broken down. it is still flying, as the other two engines are still working, but it cannot stay on course until the council engine is fixed. mr tusk, please ensure that the european union will not undergo the same fate as poland in the 18th century, when the polish parliament was completely paralysed by the ‘liberum veto’, allowing every single nobleman in the parliament to block a decision. as a result, poland did not become greater; it became irrelevant. the council has to take responsibility for the european union as a whole and it has to do so soon, because not only will our own citizens tire of the inability of the eu to respond to the big challenges of today, but europe is rapidly making itself irrelevant as a global actor. that has an impact and it has already been mentioned. the victims of the same inability to take decisions are the people in syria. if the eu fails to decide on sanctions against mr putin, we lose any leverage we may have to force him to stop the bombing. we need a political transition urgently, and that means that we have to be able to put pressure on mr putin. victims are equally the refugees, and i am actually a bit surprised to hear both the council and the commission be quite jubilant about the successful refugee policies, when the united nations just yesterday said that 2016 is the deadliest year so far. more people have drowned in the mediterranean than any year before. how can you say this is a success? how is it that the council can still look at itself in the eye in the mirror and say this is a success? mr tusk, the council – the member state leaders – have a duty to end the paralysis, a duty towards the eu citizens and a duty towards the world.
if ceta is the blueprint for how the european union negotiates its free trade agreements, then we better stop today than tomorrow. is it part of the blueprint that the president of parliament takes over the negotiations without any mandate? is it part of the blueprint that parliaments are hardly given any time to discuss the treaty in depth? is it part of the blueprint that we do not clarify essential issues such as the legal values of the interpretative declaration, the compatibility of investment protection with the european legal system and the question of how exactly the powers are distributed? is it part of the blueprint that the negotiations take place behind closed doors with business at the forefront and citizens and ngos far behind? is it part of the blueprint that we are giving away democratic control of our regulations to the business community through the arbitration system, through the pre-cooking of our rules and now again - as mr verhofstadt and alde propose - by excluding national parliaments when they do not do what business demands? if ceta is the blueprint for our future free trade agreements, then we should be ashamed to call ourselves a democratic institution. stop the ceta!

increased vigilance, tighter controls at sea, hermetically sealed borders and the designation of all migration as illegal; that is how the european council approaches the refugee question. whereas peace and security, the protection of human rights and respect for the rule of law have been central to our foreign policy, the focus seems increasingly to be on one dimension, namely the deterrence of migrants. last week there was talk of further implementation of the refugee agreement between turkey and the eu, but the heads of government failed to emphasise the original objective. the deal was to trade the illegal and dangerous routes for an airlift. the airlift for refugees has not yet been achieved and eu leaders have now even neglected to mention it in their conclusions. if we as the european union want to show that we take our own values seriously and are a reliable partner for third countries, then it is high time that we fulfilled our own commitments.

in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the mff mid-term review. the ep calls for more flexibility in and between eu budget headings. such flexibility exempts the european parliament from setting policy priorities. all political objectives are therefore equally important and parliament does not have to make choices. this flexibility gives the ep the legitimacy to avoid budgetary discipline. in addition, it undermines the veto of member states over revisions. the pvv cannot support the resulting waste of scarce resources.

how can the eu help to ensure the continued presence of the christian and shiite minorities in northern iraq after liberation from the barbarism of is? to this crucial question that was going through my mind, i received a crystal clear answer last night by phone and by mail from erbil, from the region itself, or rather: the european union is also calling for the immediate release of all the hostages and for the immediate release of the hostages of the palestinian people. the eu should press for fair political representation of the christian shia minority in northern iraq at national and regional level in iraq. independent local government councils for the various minorities are the proposed and, in my view, the most appropriate instrument for this purpose. urgently, the eu is pressing the iraqi government to change recent discriminatory legislation against non-muslim minorities. for example, why should only a non-muslim be allowed to change his religion? the applicant shall provide the applicant with a copy of the application form.
ladies and gentlemen, fallujah, ramadi, mosul - these are the names of cities that have already been conquered by isis or where the fighting is still going on at the moment. of course that is a good thing, but the experiences of fallujah and ramadi teach us that military success alone is not enough. that is why i welcome the words of the commissioner in that regard. the question is: what's our plan after that? there is little or no such plan, neither in iraq nor in the international community. the big question, of course, is: if that common enemy, isis, is defeated, will the rivalries between the various factions in iraq and the tensions caused by foreign interference come to the surface? i fear so, when i look at a recent report by amnesty international, which already points to serious human rights violations by the government army and by shiite militias. much of the focus in our debates is often on the tension between sunnis and shiites, the major groups in the country. but the small minorities in iraq are in danger of being completely crushed. the number of christian minorities has fallen from 1.5 million in 2003 to between 200 000 and 300 000. in a resolution adopted in february this year, the european parliament spoke of genocide by is against these christian minorities. how will the european commission and the high representative follow up that resolution and ensure that the political plan with iraq is implemented, with particular attention to those small minorities?
the battle for mosul is now entering its second week. more and more villages in the vicinity are being liberated from isis, but the terrorist group still holds iraq's second city. at the same time, not only is is being encircled, but also the one million civilian population. thousands of people have already fled and an unprecedented influx of refugees is expected in the coming days. another humanitarian disaster if we don't get there in time to help. the €50 million the eu has made available for humanitarian aid in mosul is a good start, but it is also vital that a strategy is developed to provide civilians with safe shelter. and with the situation becoming more dangerous by the day, there is urgency.
in afghanistan, women have few rights, young men are being recruited by the taliban and daesh, and entire provinces are not under kabul's authority. echo, our emergency relief organization, is warning of a humanitarian disaster this winter. and with that country europe is making a deal! it 's a kind of turkish deal: elusive but very far-reaching. and if it quacks like a duck, swims like a duck and looks like a duck, then it's a duck! this joint way forward on migration is a readmission agreement and the european parliament has the right to give its consent to it. then why don't you just put that proposal to us? if afghanistan wants it and the european union wants it, can it not be settled simply by a proper democratic decision-making? now that you're not, you're just starting to suspect that something's not right here. what's also not on the hook is how we're funding this. the commission intends to use part of the money for the reconstruction of afghanistan to reintegrate asylum seekers who have been expelled from europe. that 's not allowed . there are rules for that . development money should be used for development cooperation and not to fund our repressive migration policies. why are the afghans agreeing to this? i don't think that's the answer either. they pull in all directions on the short end, right? more people, less money. this deal will further destabilize that country and it's the number one time the west has helped to break it down.
it is a good development that the eu is increasingly working with other countries to shape migration policy. but the way in which a cooperation agreement with afghanistan was concluded behind closed doors earlier this month is not a beauty contest. without any communication or consultation with the european parliament, a joint declaration was drawn up between the eu and afghanistan. the text of the declaration also seems to focus disproportionately on the return of rejected asylum seekers to afghanistan. what is missing is how the eu will contribute concretely to peace and security in that country, so that people no longer have to flee. it is necessary to have an effective return policy for those who are not entitled to asylum in europe, but in the case of afghanistan, caution is warranted. only 21 percent of asylum seekers are still rejected from the country. let us not forget that most of the people of that country are entitled to international protection and deserve our solidarity.
on behalf of the s&d group. – madam president, during the coup attempt on 15 july 2016, cnn türk’s ankara bureau chief, hande fırat, entered into our living rooms through our tv screens all around the world. in her hand was an iphone. president erdoğan was addressing the nation through facetime. media plurality played a crucial role in preventing the coup plotters from succeeding in their attempt to illegally take over democratic institutions in turkey that night. let that not be forgotten. in the last years, media freedom in turkey has been under great pressure, but the level of the crackdown the turkish journalists are facing under the current state of emergency is unprecedented. not only were 150 media outlets closed and 2 300 journalists fired in the last two months, but 99 journalists and writers were also jailed, with no charges presented against most of them. this brings the number of media workers detained on charges, believed to be related to their work, to at least 130. this is the highest number of jailed journalists in europe. that is not a cool list to be heading, especially not for an eu candidate country. in the european parliament, we attach great importance to press freedom. one of this year’s candidates for the sakharov prize is turkish journalist can dündar. one year ago, we also had a debate about the freedom of media when the ‘zaman’ newspaper was closed down. in the joint resolution that we will vote on tomorrow, all political groups are united in their call on the turkish government to immediately release these journalists; distinguished journalist of proven integrity, including former editor-in-chief ahmed altan, columnist şahin alpay, and well-known novelist aslı erdoğan, are being punished without due process through a long period of pre-trial detention. these people did not participate in the coup and the long period of pre-trial detention serves no public good. it seems the journalists are being punished purely for the opinions that they held, rather than for any incitement to violence or actual violence. the events of last night show that the curtailment of free speech in turkey is not limited to journalists only. the co-mayors of diyarbakır, gültan kışanak and fırat anlı, were detained yesterday. if democratically elected politicians are no longer allowed to freely express their opinion, then who can? to conclude, we understand that turkey is going through a difficult time and people are traumatised by the deteriorating security situation, both inside the country and surrounding it. however, these threats can only be curtailed by strengthening democracy; by strengthening the rule of law, and by getting back to a normal situation. i hope, for all the people in turkey, that this time starts in turkey sooner rather than later.

in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the discharge for the budget of the european council and the council. the pvv takes the view that it is not within the european parliament's competence to discharge the budget of the european council. the national parliaments of the member states will deal with this. the european parliament's withholding of discharge is therefore fine. the pvv can therefore understand why the council feels that it does not have to abide by the ep's budgetary rules. but that doesn't mean the pvv approves the budget.
in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the discharge for the budget of the eniac joint undertaking. the pvv takes the view that whatever the use made of the budget by agencies such as eniac, the european union does not add value to research budgets. research can be financed by the sovereign member states themselves without any problems and researchers can decide for themselves when and how they enter into cooperation with other (foreign) research institutions. the pvv therefore considers that there is no reason to support or finance eu agencies.

in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the discharge of the iter joint undertaking's budget. the pvv takes the view that whatever agencies like iter spend their budget, the european union does not add value to research budgets. research can be financed by the sovereign member states themselves without any problems. iter has not produced much of a result for the billions of euros spent on fusion research. the pvv therefore considers that there is no reason to support or finance eu agencies.

in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the motion for a resolution (rule 170 (4)) replacing the non-legislative motion for a resolution a8-0250/2016, as education and youth policy is an exclusive competence of the member states. and that 's good too . at national level, a member state can use youth policies and available resources in a more relevant, effective and coordinated way, according to its own priority.
this year marks exactly 100 years since daylight saving time was first introduced. it was a german idea during world war i and it was just about saving on coal. the regulation of summertime is now a european competence: we're going to go over that. we introduced it during the oil boycott by the arab countries to save energy. meanwhile, it turns out that the whole energy argument doesn't add up. it's out of date. several studies show that this arrangement saves little or no energy, but causes many disadvantages, such as and then we are talking about human sleep disorders. this has been scientifically demonstrated by various experts here in this group or in this parliament. the biological clocks are getting totally out of whack, and we're all dealing with it. it's a social jet lag. children should be up an hour earlier in the morning and elderly people in nursing homes should be put to bed an hour later. delaying the time often makes parents and children more tired, and family life suffers. sleep disturbances occur with all sorts of consequences: in the united states, the number of people who are sick is increasing. other problems include missed appointments and the damage of old clocks by the tinkering with the hands. the industry also has to adapt machinery to new times; i am thinking of the bakers and the signals from the transport sector. what is true of man is also true of the animals. and then i think about dairy farming, because not everyone has a milking robot. in addition, the clock's reversal leads to more social problems: morning commuters drive longer in the dark, which is dangerous for traffic. evening fillies ride more in ozone heat, which is bad for the climate. more and more countries are therefore deciding to stop moving the clock: russia, turkey and japan. they're not in the game anymore. to save energy, we can insulate homes better, install more solar panels, or have cars indicate actual use. the time has therefore come for the european union to move the clock for the last time after a hundred years. stop this unnecessary business. we must listen to the criticisms of the citizens and this affects every citizen. i am sure that will help to make our europe a little more popular with the citizens, and that is what our european commissioner frans timmermans, who is dealing with this, wants too, is he not? our commissioner of health is always talking about prevention, so we should be doing this, right? we don't have to sit in a meeting for hours. there's only one question left: we are not going to vote for the amendments. as far as i'm concerned, for summer time.
the gue/ngl group supports the green group's request with full conviction. ceta is a large and far-reaching agreement with far-reaching implications for our regulation, our decision-making, our legal system, the precautionary principle and our economy. the investment court system (ics) in particular is a highly controversial part of the treaty. a public consultation in 2005 received 150 000 responses to its predecessor, the investor-state dispute settlement system (isds), of which 97% were strongly critical. some of those errors have been corrected in the investment court system, but in essence ics is the same system as isds. this has been argued by a number of leading academics, including canadian professor gus van harten, and has also been demonstrated in a study entitled 'investment court system put to the test' by a number of leading ngos. they have shown that the most contentious isds cases can still be brought under ics with the same or worse outcome. it is true that the legal service of the european parliament has said that it does not expect any problems with ics, but on the other hand there are letters from the european association of judges and the deutsche richterbund that contradict that. we need to be able to talk about this.
the euro is a giant on muddy feet. from the top, that stainless steel sculpture from luxembourg, germany and the netherlands still looks nice, but the further you go down, the more clay you find. there have been attempts, mr president, to strengthen that image, but, strangely enough, by adding more clay. for example, the maintenance of the crumbling structure has been totally neglected. last week, heavily indebted countries were given further deferment to take reform measures. a disastrous decision, made of mud. inflation also remained irresponsibly low with many negative effects, for example on pension funds. president-elect trump has already had a more favorable effect on inflation in the past month than the european central bank's qe program in the past 20 months. the current approach to the problems in the eurozone increases the risk that the image of the euro will one day collapse with a thunderous blow.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, first of all i would like to thank and congratulate the rapporteur for doing a very good job and ensuring a compromise that will indeed be supported by my group. as the previous speaker said, this is a very important topic for citizens. this is about everyday products that they use. everybody has insurances, loans, mortgages, pension savings, investment accounts, etc., but we see that only three percent of the market – three percent of the potential – is being used and that is worrying. but i have to say that what worries me even more is the lack of ambition and the lack of urgency – just look around the room – because, even if the report is very good, we shy away from the taboos. i also note that the commission had initially planned to come forward with an action plan in september and now the talk is about next year. i understand that some people are afraid of rapid developments – fintech was mentioned – but this is reality. this is what is happening and, as long as we sit on our hands, we will be overtaken by other parts of the world. if i look at the level of investment by the united states and china, this overtakes the european union by far. the european union is fragmented into 28 national markets. we are weak and not attractive for investors. i get very worried when i read in the newspaper that a major european bank is actually investing eur 20 million euros in fintech in china. it should be the other way around. we should be attractive enough for chinese investors to come here and develop our own services. europe has the potential to become the hub for retail financial services but, if we do not act more speedily, with more urgency and with more ambition, then we are going to lose the race because, colleagues – or those of you who are still around – this is not necessary for multinationals; they will find their way anyway. this is, however, urgently needed for consumers and smes, because they do need the european union in order to eliminate the barriers. alde and the green group have tabled amendments and i very much hope for the support of the epp group and the s&d group, because we do not think it is enough to make noble statements here about the objective of eliminating barriers, maybe sometime next century. we need concrete deadlines and we need concrete objectives. that is why we hope that these amendments mentioning the deadlines will be adopted. finally, it is a pity that, as usual, the council is absent – the council representing the member states – because we all know that the biggest obstacle of all is national governments protecting their national champions. so where are they? even the biggest eurosceptics are always saying how important the internal market is. the government leaders are always saying how important the internal market is but, when it really comes down to it, they are actually blocking the opening—up of the internal market, doing a big disservice to their own citizens. so i hope that the commission will come up with a really ambitious action plan as soon as possible.
we are no longer living in an era of change, we are living in a change of era. and that's especially true when you look at the financial sector, because all of the new technologies, from big data analytics to robo-advice to blockchain, you name it: all the new things coming together at the same time these days is changing the landscape tremendously. this will make it possible for many products to become cheaper, better, faster and, i say to mrs anneliese, also more accessible to many people. so there are enormous opportunities, because the main objective that we have all set here is to create jobs and economic growth. to do that, we must be able to compete internationally. we need a sophisticated financial infrastructure for that, too. to that end, this report, and i congratulate mr ludvigsson on that, can provide an important impetus to ensure that we are innovation friendly and at the same time ensure consumer protection and, of course, also keep an eye on the systemic risks that are present in our entire financial system. we have to find that balance between ourselves, because as my colleague in the field has just said, there is a tremendous amount of investment in this all over the world. if we're not careful, we're gonna fall behind in this area. we are doing our economy a disservice, but above all we are doing our citizens a disservice, because they are very keen to be able to play a part in the world. you need those upgrades to be able to do that.
in the time of the financial crisis, we have seen a lot of banks around the world go into trouble and we have had to bail out banks with taxpayers' money. we had to do the same in my country. we certainly don't want to go back to that situation. that is why it is very good that the capital requirements for these banks have been increased, because banks simply have to be able to hold their own. but what we are seeing now in the basel committee is that standards are being set at the international level, where everyone is treated equally while the situation is not equal. because, as has been said by other speakers, our banking system is very different from, say, the american one. if you look at the situation around mortgages for example, then in the netherlands, even at the height of the housing crisis, there was hardly any loss on mortgages. so it doesn't make sense to treat them in the same way as mortgages in america, which have caused a lot of problems there. i am therefore very pleased that we as a parliament can together give a strong signal that we do not want the way the basle committee is now proposing, because our entrepreneurs have had a very difficult time in recent years in getting credit. if we were to apply these requirements again, they would be dripping from the rain. so, as far as we are concerned, vice-commissioner dombrovskis can, in football terms, with a straight leg, represent our interests in the basel committee and take care of them.
the agricultural sector and smes are very concerned about the future lending of the basel iv standards. of course, financial stability and a robust banking sector with sufficient equity are paramount, but there is a fear that the proposed rules will have serious repercussions on this real economy. the agricultural sector in europe and the netherlands is currently going through a difficult period, partly because of the low milk prices and the russian boycott. the agricultural sector, especially young farmers with a lot of land equity, often have to take out a new mortgage, among other things to comply with animal welfare, the nitrate directive and the climate agreement. that's why they need a continuation of their mortgage. the future of many farmers depends on the granting of transitional credits. the same is true of our construction industry, which is coming out of a deep valley. citizens should continue to be able to take out a mortgage to buy a house. that is why the european commission cannot remain on the sidelines. there is a possibility to use key instruments such as the juncker plan, and this is also suggested in the report by cees veerman of the task force on the future of the agricultural sector. in conclusion, mr president, i should like to say: what you should remember from my speech is that we have to prevent a credit crunch. economic growth, the stimulation of the labour market and the survival of a strong agricultural sector in europe require this. europe stands for peace, but also for sufficient food, sufficient credit to continue producing food, including by family and family businesses. what is a liveable countryside without a healthy farm, with a healthy bank, of course?
the imf's global financial stability report is a heartbreaking read. europe has too many banks, too many weak banks and then a few too big banks. there are also banks struggling with non-repayable loans. there is no reason for european arrogance or complacency, but rather for haste. the resistance from the financial sector is not surprising and not convincing. in their story, rules always get in the way of growth. in their story, attracting additional capital is always a stumbling block. but let's face it, a well-regulated and well-capitalized financial sector is a prerequisite for economic growth. it is therefore good that this resolution, with thanks to the rapporteur, sends a balanced signal, highlighting the specific difficulties that may arise in the european market, but also showing that there must be robust internal models and that banks must also be sufficiently capitalised. it has happened too often that banks have paid dividends but have not had their capital in order, with deutsche bank as a clear example. they've paid out another $5 billion in dividends since 2008. so i welcome this discussion, i welcome the fact that the important work of basel is being discussed openly in this chamber, but let us not be arrogant or complacent.
we have one coin. that requires a policy. i am also glad that the commission is looking at the impact of budgetary balances on the euro area as a whole. is it stabilizing or is it not? the commission also concludes and recommends that an additional eur 50 billion should be spent. this shows that the commission has taken a new course, and rightly so. investment expenditure is necessary for economic growth, now and later, and certainly the netherlands and germany have room for it. it's a new course that not everyone is taking. wolfgang schäuble has already written a letter of resignation and seems to be ignoring that advice, despite the historically low interest rates. but he's left alone. he's becoming more and more like the old greedy scrooge in dickens' christmas story. it is christmas soon and i still hope that wolfgang schäuble and the hawks will come to their senses.
a lot of the most terrible things in this world are tied to money laundering and can't even be done without it. whether it's terrorist financing, drug trafficking, human trafficking, you know, they all use laundry detergent. it is therefore very good that mr maurel has written this report and that the commissioner has made this proposal, in order to at least take a step towards exchanging information between tax authorities on this matter. we must do everything we can to stop money laundering. it must be done in an international context. the working conditions for the commission have not improved in this respect, but i would still like to call on the commissioner to show in the panama papers that it is necessary and to do his utmost to be able to tackle this issue internationally, because we cannot do it in europe alone. however, there are other shortcuts i would like to point out. i am very pleased that my amendment, which extends the scope of the directive to the so-called virtual currencies, has been adopted by the econ committee. after all, there are now so many billions in circulation in digital currencies like bitcoin, of which, for example, there are more than 10 billion in circulation this year alone. so you want to prevent criminals from using the anonymous bitcoins and all kinds of other currencies to jump the gun again. after all, it's not just about the bitcoin. you also have litecoin, peercoin, namecoin, quarkcoin, zcash and even, you wouldn't believe it, dogecoin. the name alone should make the eyebrows frown. i therefore ask the commission and the council to take over this.
in writing. the vvd abstained in the final vote on the report on the effectiveness of development cooperation, because it is not the eu's job to oblige member states to spend 0.7% of their gdp on development cooperation. in addition, member states should remain free to use development cooperation funds to address acute crises such as the refugee crisis. the vvd supports proposals to spend development money more effectively by ensuring greater transparency and by fighting corruption in developing countries.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on agriculture and rural development. first of all, i would like to thank my colleague julie girling for her hard work and dedication to this difficult issue. as rapporteur for the committee on agriculture, i am glad that methane has been left out of the agreement. this has prevented double legislation and double regulation. methane is a greenhouse gas and is already regulated through climate targets. moreover, it allows the agricultural sector to retain the flexibility to determine how it will reduce its emissions of various greenhouse gases. i want to avoid farmers being forced to keep their cows in the stable to capture methane that cows emit. that's exactly what society doesn't want. we must also take account of the organic sector. organically raised animals, on average, emit more methane because they are fed a different composition of feed. on the other hand, it is the organic and conventional sectors that can make a major contribution to reducing greenhouse gases in the atmosphere by sequestering co2 in the soil via organic matter.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, first of all i would like to thank the rapporteur, ms girling, for her very good work on striking this deal. indeed, as a previous colleague said, it was a very interesting and tough negotiation path, but i do think that in the end ms girling was very helpful in trying to strike a deal. however, if you assess the deal we can only be disappointed. just look at the demands that we had as a parliament. we wanted higher ambition. we had to give in substantially. we did not want any flexibilities on top of that. the council and the member states were asking for more and more flexibilities. we do not have a 2025 target, so it is now only talking about 2030, which is very, very far down the road. methane is out, so the agriculture sector is off the hook again for cleaning our air. all these things together can only lead to one conclusion and that is that this package is not good enough. and i think i have to turn now to the council – to the dutch presidency maybe in particular – who did good work in striking this deal. but if you turn to all the member states, i really have a question. we are discussing air pollution. if there is one thing that is clearly a european competence and that is really cross-border, it is air pollution. if we do something about it, it will lead to jobs, cleaner technologies, cleaner air, better improvements for our citizens, lower costs for health. all these things we can achieve. what we saw at the member state level was just a purely nationalistic approach; what can i do? i shall pay no mind to my neighbouring countries and only do what i can deliver. that approach really caused the deterioration of this deal, and it is a shame. i ask the member states to keep in mind that if you want to fight for europe, you have to fight for european solidarity on these kind of issues. otherwise, we are giving away this project, and that is the worst thing we could do.
mr president, we as humans can do a great deal to improve our own health. we can move more, we can eat healthy, we can decide not to smoke. one thing we as individuals cannot do is to control the air we breathe. we breathe the air around us, here, in this chamber, and if we are in brussels, in the rue de la loi or rue belliard. and that is why the responsibility of governments is so great. the government is the only one who can determine how clean that air is. public health is one thing. economic damage is a second. and i find it incomprehensible that the member states themselves have been caught up in the cost-effective scenario of the european commission. mr president, today the european environment agency is presenting its annual air quality report, and it shows once again that one sector is doing far, far too little to improve air quality, and that is the agricultural sector. and let that be the sector that is being kept out of the wind again today. ammonia targets are being rapidly reduced and methane even eliminated altogether. is dirty air, dirty emissions, less harmful than the air from agriculture? maybe someone here can explain to me why they always have to be treated so special. president. - the member states have really failed. citizens have a right to clean air and they are being denied that right, and that is why i regret to have to vote against this agreement.

member of the commission. – mr president, i would like to thank you all for your pertinent and constructive comments. i am encouraged by the broad support for the agreement that has been reached. it is close to the original commission proposal in terms of ambition and, given the difficulty of the negotiation, is in my view a good compromise between various positions. i know that concerns remain on flexibilities and on interim checks on the trajectory to 2030. however, the additional flexibilities are tightly constrained and we will monitor their application closely. likewise, for the interim trajectory there is now a robust management framework in place to ensure that member states are on track to meet their commitments. the commission will evaluate the national programmes, including the credibility of the reduction pathways, and those evaluations will be made public. i have listened to and note the concerns of some about the level of effort required in particular member states, but those are the very member states whose citizens will benefit the most from implementation of the measures. they are the member states that have the greatest problems in complying with ambient air quality standards and they are the member states that will save most in terms of health and other economic benefits. we will work with non—member states to maximise their support, including from european union financing and from synergies with, for instance, eu climate and energy policies for the successful and cost—effective implementation of the directive. on the review of methane emissions, the commission has made a declaration because it considers that there is a strong air-quality case for keeping the development of methane emissions in the member states under review in order to reduce the ozone concentrations in the eu and to promote methane reductions internationally. the commission confirms that, on the basis of the reported national emissions, it intends to assess further the impact of methane emissions on achieving the objectives set out in article 1(2) of the nec directive; it will consider measures for reducing those emissions and, where appropriate, will submit a legislative proposal for that purpose. in its assessment the commission will take into account a number of ongoing studies in this field, due to be finalised in 2017, as well as further international developments in this area. i would like to close by again thanking the rapporteur and shadow rapporteurs for making possible this major step forward in air pollution control. declaration by the commission on the procedure for adoption of implementing acts the commission underlines that it is contrary to the letter and to the spirit of regulation 182/2011 (oj l 55 of 28.2.2011, p. 13) to invoke article 5§4, subparagraph 2, point b) in a systematic manner. recourse to this provision must respond to a specific need to depart from the rule of principle which is that the commission may adopt a draft implementing act when no opinion is delivered. given that it is an exception to the general rule established in article 5§4 recourse to subparagraph 2, point b), cannot be simply seen as a "discretionary power" of the legislator, but must be interpreted in a restrictive manner and thus must be justified.
nearly half a million people die every year in europe from the effects of air pollution. the current european air quality standards are far too weak to protect human health. europe should adopt the world health organisation guidelines, which impose much higher requirements on particulate matter, ozone, nitrogen dioxide and sulphur dioxide. but instead it presents a strong weakening of the current weak standards for air quality. the party for the animals considers this proposal shameful and unacceptable. compared with the current guidelines, this proposal will result in up to 10 000 additional deaths per year. under pressure from the agricultural sector, the greenhouse gas methane has been removed from the proposal and the standards for ammonia are being lowered. while these substances are a huge contributor to the pollution of our air and also cause people to suffer serious health problems or die prematurely. europe is suffocating its citizens and the party for the animals is not a party to that.

in writing. ‒ free trade agreements such as ceta and ttip are a disaster for animals, for nature and for the environment, but they also pose a threat to democracy. the party for the animals believes that the european court should be given sufficient time to examine whether the trade agreement is in conflict with the eu treaties. there are still many uncertainties surrounding ceta, so a judgment by the court is certainly not superfluous. of course, such a procedure takes time, but this delay is a good thing. it is during this period that a full debate on ceta can finally take place in the european parliament. without debate, there is no democracy. that is why i voted for an opinion of the court of justice.
in writing. ‒ the sp voted in favour of this report because it represents progress compared to the current emission ceilings and should be seen as a step in the right direction. at the same time, we do not think that the ceilings are sufficient and we are seeking stricter standards.
the vvd abstained from the final vote on the report on the implementation of the common security and defence policy, because the establishment of a european military headquarters is not appropriate at the moment. nato is and remains the cornerstone of european security policy and nato's efforts should not be duplicated. the vvd supports the proposal for a single defence market, increased defence investment, including in the field of research and development.
in writing. ‒ the sp recognises the dangers of disinformation from third parties such as russia and is, but abstained because the report makes too many calls for things we do not support, such as strengthening the european external action service (eeas) and closer cooperation with nato. the report is also not sufficiently critical of the provision of information to the eu.
in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the motion on sign language and professional sign interpreters. the motion demands, among other things, that: the european parliament has already expressed its concern about the situation in the country, and the commission has already taken action to ensure that the situation in the country is not affected by the current situation. the pvv believes that the participation of people with physical disabilities in society is guaranteed in the netherlands. the netherlands is far ahead of the proposals made in the european parliament. the proposals also go beyond the ep's mandate. the european union's obligation to include sign language in school curricula cannot support the pvv, as the ep has no control over the curricula in education. the eu is also calling for the eu to improve its support for deaf and hard of hearing people. but that does not require this motion, it can simply be done.
in writing. ‒ the european commission wants to extend the approval of the widely used herbicide bentazone for as much as 15 years, and that is despite the fact that bentazone is certainly not an undisputed toxin. there is evidence that bentazone contaminates groundwater and has endocrine disrupting properties. until there is conclusive evidence that the use of bentazone is safe for humans, the environment and animals, the precautionary principle should apply. the european commission cannot then simply grant approvals and renewals. the party for the animals considers that the approval of the active substance bentazone should not be renewed. that is why i voted against this extension.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs. first of all, thank you to brian hayes for the pleasant cooperation between our committees on this dossier and congratulations on the result achieved. three points were the guiding principles of our committee's deliberations on this subject. first, pension funds serve a social purpose and should be treated as such. secondly, the european unity sausage in the field of pensions is not going to work. the differences between member states are too great and the pension landscape in europe is too different. thirdly, we must ensure that we in europe encourage those countries which do not yet have icpvs to develop such systems and not the other way around. all three of these points have been incorporated into this directive thanks to the good work of the council and parliament. we have found a good balance between, on the one hand, the need to have good european standards in the areas of governance, supervision and transparency, while at the same time ensuring that member states have the flexibility to adapt these standards to their own specific context. and that is certainly an outcome that cannot be underestimated in the light of the original proposal.

are talking today about further opening up of the european railway market. it is an extension of the first, second and third railway packages and it is a further step, because this railway package will not bring about a complete opening up of the market. the european public now also thinks a little differently about this opening up of the market than when these proposals were written, and that is something we should look at. there is a tension between the opening up of the market and the provision of public services. many people think that the public service will increase because of the opening up of the market. but we certainly do not have that guarantee, especially since there are many exceptions to the opening of the market. member states or, in any event, the competent
