- i'm the reporter. i would like to focus on two elements. first of all, the comment of my liberal colleagues mrs meissner and mr riquet. i myself would have liked to see a little more ambition in the fourth railway package, and the commissioner said that the commission's proposals were more ambitious. i also think that the first reading of the european parliament showed the same ambition. then we got the trilogies, and in those years, things have changed. the member states which were initially very strong in their support for market opening have been slow to act. i do not blame those member states because the debate on market opening versus services in europe has taken a different turn in recent years. you can dwell on that, but it's good to implement what we have now and then look beyond. the second point concerns the social component. i understand very well the concerns of my colleagues in the s&d group, but on the other hand we must also be honest. there are national schemes, there is subsidiarity and there are the guarantees given by the european commission. moreover, as research shows, the companies which have been privatised have generally not suffered any deterioration in their working conditions after privatisation. i said it in my first speech: we must find a balance between social protection, mobility and innovation. i think we've found that balance.
i would like to thank mr fleckenstein for his work. 2001, 2004: the proposal for a new european port regulation has so far failed. i believe that this was mainly due to the 'one size fits all' approach that the european commission has always advocated. but europe and its ports are very diverse and we have taken this into account in the proposal before us. the proposal that the commission put forward for the third time was still largely a 'one size fits all' approach. however, we have been able to make enough adjustments, so that this proposal is good for europe, good for the netherlands and good for ports like rotterdam. what have we improved? the original commission proposal called for the opening of ports to a whole range of service providers: rowers, dredgers, and pilots. that's a nice thought, but it's got to work in practice. for example, some ports are too small to accommodate all of that. safety must also be maintained, so it is not always wise to have several pilot services in one port. our amendments have now created all sorts of exceptions, and a port like rotterdam, for example, can now continue to operate with one pilot service and also have control over who can and cannot work in the port. at the same time, we have made sure that there is not too much administrative burden. for example, the commission proposed that workers in ports should be provided with relevant training and education by the port authority. yes, that is a nice idea, but a lot of ports in my country, for example, don't deal with that at all. so we've taken that out and reduced a large set of requirements for independent oversight to realistic and reasonable proportions. the last and very important point is that the provisions on financial transparency have been improved and have been maintained. ports must continue to be transparent about their finances, which is crucial for a level playing field. we do not want member states to benefit financially from their own ports. so i support this amended proposal.
before we know it, 12 december 2016 will be another historic day as we complete both the fourth railway package and the port regulation! i thank the rapporteur, mr fleckenstein, for all the work he has done, because it was not an easy job. my colleague peter van dalen has already indicated quite clearly what elements we have deleted and changed and mrs vozemberg has also done her job as a greek mep. we must pay particular attention to the question of state aid. mr fleckenstein has constantly tried to explain to mrs bulc's colleagues what we now have to understand by this state aid. it is not yet clear, but i understand that we are still to receive a detailed explanation from mrs bulc's colleagues. in the meantime, the ports are doing quite well and i would also like to draw attention to the training element. we see in the maritime sector, both inland and offshore, a shortage of personnel and proper training is a requirement. i think we're making a good move today, and i thank you very much.
for the enf group freedom, security, equality and freedom of expression are all fundamental rights of which the european union is so proud. but ironically, the same brussels elite hates the people they want to defend, the people who stand up for their own culture, who stand up for their own country, the people who want their country to guard the borders to protect the dutch against profiteers, against rapists, against criminals and against terrorists, the people who want to protect their country against anti-violence against women, anti-violence against homosexuals, honour killing and anti-semitism, in short, the people who want to protect their country against the hateful ideology called islam. those people who are being thrown out of here as racists, as xenophobes or islamophobes and the politicians who are advocating them are being accused of - and i quote from today's report - 'hate propaganda and violent political views' when it is the european union that has brought europe in with its terrible policy of open borders, of hatred and violence. and you're trying to dismiss concerned citizens and politicians with policies like today's as pariahs and take away their fundamental right to free speech and free thought. but i can tell you that you will never succeed because we will continue to defend freedom, security and freedom of expression, all those fundamental rights which have been dismissed by the brussels elite and which seem to apply only to a group of people selected by you. and we do it simply because we want what is best for our country, because the netherlands is our country and must remain so.
the seas are wide and the oceans are deep. but the bottom has been reached. there are an estimated ten million species of plants and animals living in our oceans, and many of these species have not even been discovered yet. they live on or just above the seabed, in the deep sea. this deep sea begins at 200 meters and contains beautiful coral reefs. some are over 100 feet high. the oldest are about 8,500 years old, and there are fish that can live up to 150 years without human intervention. and these beautiful fish are ground into cheap animal feed and fish oil. where do we get the right to wipe out these unique species in one fell swoop from this unique ecosystem of monstrously large trawls? that's not fishing, that's ocean vandalism! and international waters have very few rules. the fish have been declared free of birds and after years of debate, a half-baked plan to combat this ecocide has been put to the test. but once again, the rights of the strong outweigh the interests of the weak. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of trawls and seines, and to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of trawls and seines. only the deepest seabed is a little bit more protected, and that's a blanket for bleeding. it is with a heavy heart that i shall vote in favour of this proposal, because without this plan the seabed will be completely at the mercy of the vandals of deep-sea fishing. i also believe that european agricultural and fisheries subsidies should be abolished.
in writing. ‒ the pvv voted against the report. the report calls on the european commission to develop long-term policies for the cultural and creative industries, including by developing a common legal basis. the pvv believes that the dutch legislator should be well placed to develop a long-term policy for the creative industries. subordinating the dutch parliament to yet another legally binding agreement is undesirable and contrary to the principle of subsidiarity.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, today’s debate about human rights in the world comes at a time of growing pressure on the universality of human rights, from the banning and intimidation of civil society, to attacks on human rights defenders, journalists and bloggers, or restrictions on the free expression of lgbtis, artists and those who demonstrate peacefully. the list is long and requires clear push-back that involves more leadership from europe. it is difficult to be proud of europe when we watch aleppo fall and bombardments by russian and assad’s planes being rewarded, when refugees fleeing this atrocious war are being fenced off, and international law and human rights are discarded when they get in the way of counterterrorism efforts or ‘managing’ migration. it is now more important than ever for us to stand unequivocally for the very values upon which our open societies are based and give meaning to those values globally. that means a more forceful and credible stance for the importance of and respect for human rights. young women, such as the sakharov prize winners who courageously fight the horrible violence caused by daesh, have fled to europe, but imagine the many, many, many who have spent years in refugee camps now, who have been married off with absolutely no perspective. what will we tell them? we cannot be complacent and must make sure that europe remains a beacon of freedom, a safe haven for free thinkers and a meaningful representation of what universality of human rights is actually about. it is not only the right thing to do, but it is in our self-interest to offer perspectives for people who need them so desperately, such as in north africa and the middle east. i therefore urge you, as my final comment, to avoid deals like the one made with turkey and now being copied with countries like mali, in order to avoid a situation where the eu’s foreign policy becomes a mere instrument to manage migration without having a clear focus on the need to defend and protect human rights. that is what we should give much more attention to and prioritise in our foreign policy.

in writing. ‒ this explanation concerns amendment 10 that was tabled to this text. the vvd delegation in the european parliament wholeheartedly supports the rights of disabled people, and is in favour of improving access for disabled people to public transport and other facilities in general. however, the vvd delegation chose not to support this specific amendment as its adoption would imply a rejection of a crucial part of the railway package in second reading. this would lead to a disproportionate delay in the legislative process, which the people of europe do not deserve. moreover, it is unlikely that a reopening of the negotiations would lead to an alternative outcome concerning this specific issue.

in writing. ‒ the fpa supports the development of new and existing (financial) risk management instruments - within the framework of the common agricultural policy - which can make farmers more resilient to price fluctuations. the vvd believes that binding european rules on unfair trading practices are not the right instrument and awaits the impact assessment of the european commission on this matter. the vvd wants more attention to be paid to voluntary and self-regulatory initiatives initiated by the sector. the vvd also does not support the idea that the cap objectives should prevail over the competition rules and that the crisis reserve should be placed outside the eu budget.

on behalf of the s&d group. – mr president, my group, the s&d group, recently pleaded in a letter for restoration of collective bargaining in greece. labour market reforms have made collective bargaining practically non-existent, and the troika said not to worry because reforms would strengthen collective bargaining at firm level instead. but that didn’t happen, and the situation of greek workers is dire. mr juncker’s letter in response to us stated that the commission acts in full compliance with the eu treaties, but that the eu charter of fundamental rights doesn’t apply as such to greek measures. this is a flagrant contradiction. the situation in greece is neither in compliance with the eu charter of fundamental rights, nor with the core ilo conventions. it is not because the memorandum of understanding is not eu law that we can now exclude greece from the european social model. i will not stand for that. the memorandum cannot be a legal no-man’s land, and besides, the recommendations of the already-mentioned expert group are crystal clear. the commission writes that the troika is discussing which recommendations to consider. let me remind the commission that the experts were endorsed by all memorandum partners and that cherry picking from them is unacceptable. it’s time to bring collective bargaining in greece back in line with good practice and with eu law.

who wouldn't cry over the terrible terrorist attack on the christian community in egypt on sunday when you see the photos of the victims? i share the growing concern of egyptian christians over a series of incidents between muslims and christians in villages, including the dragging of an elderly christian woman naked through the streets. and i share the concern about the lack of the basic freedom to convert to christianity as a muslim. egyptian christians want a government that effectively protects their lives, property and fundamental rights. the european union must urge president sisi to do so and must give him all the support it can.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of animal welfare. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat the spread of this disease, such as the use of bullfighting, the endless dragging of animals in overcrowded cattle cars, the force-feeding of ducks and geese for the production of foie gras, the killing of stray dogs in romania, the structural tail-cutting of pigs, the terrible fate of slaughter horses and the abolition of animal testing. in short, citizens are very concerned about the way we treat animals in europe, and that is understandable, because it is one of the few values we still share in europe. this common value is enshrined in article 13 of the european constitution. animals are sentient beings that we must take into full consideration. however, the majority of these petitions are rejected by the commission with the words 'that is not our competence'. how can the commission be surprised that the people are turning their backs on europe? europe is faltering because you completely ignore the concerns of the citizens. if you want people to think the european union is important, give them a reason for it. make good rules for animals and make sure they are enforced. do what you should do and protect our animals. because 15 years ago, we were on the cutting edge of animal welfare, and now we're way behind, and if we stand still any longer, we're going to be overtaken by china. i also believe that european agricultural subsidies should be abolished.
author. – mr president, let me also take this opportunity to welcome the ambassador of myanmar, who was present here today, and also thank the delegation of the parliament of myanmar for their visit to brussels last week. i think this is a showing of willingness to engage in a constructive dialogue with us, and constructive dialogue is exactly what we need at the moment. we are, as all our colleagues have said, extremely worried about the discrimination and the repression of the rohingya minority in myanmar who suffer, in the words of the un high commissioner al-hussein, from: deprivation of nationality which renders them stateless; severe restrictions on freedom of movement; threats to life and security; denial of the rights to health and education; forced labour; sexual violence; and limitation on their political rights. last year we hailed the elections in myanmar as a historic moment and an important step towards an inclusive democracy. that was one year ago; we now urgently need to see a commitment from the myanmar government, a commitment to the basic human rights of all its citizens.
for the ecr group it is chilling and alarming that iraq is probably the only country in the world with an official "society for the preservation of mass graves". which brings us to the chilling fact that isis's mass graves are a continuation of an existing murderous practice: the mass graves of the saddam hussein era and then of al qaeda. but the shiite camp in iraq has also committed war crimes and mass graves in the inter-islamic conflict with the sunnis. this applies both to the iraqi army and police forces and to their shia allies, the murderous and predatory militias of the popular front. given this situation - a general atmosphere of revenge - you are very anxious to see new mass graves in iraq after the liberation of mosul. that's the reality.
in writing. ‒ the sp is of course of the opinion that any future international aviation agreements should never have the effect of reducing the working conditions or conditions of personnel working in the aviation sector. we do not want to prejudge the content of the treaties. that is why we abstained on amendments nos 4 and 6.
in writing. ‒ the sp is of course of the opinion that any future international aviation agreements should never have the effect of reducing the working conditions or conditions of personnel working in the aviation sector. we do not want to prejudge the content of the treaties. that is why we abstained on amendments nos 4 and 6.
